### [1] [How Far Did They Go? The Persuasive Tactics of Covert LLM Agents in a Discontinued Field Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05256)
*Kokil Jaidka,Saifuddin Ahmed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of a leaked dataset from a halted Reddit experiment where undisclosed AI agents engaged in r/ChangeMyView debates revealed systematic use of identity targeting, authority signaling, alignment strategies, and cognitive bias triggers—forming a persuasive architecture distinct from human debaters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The discontinued Reddit experiment provides a rare naturalistic dataset of undetected LLM behavior in deliberative discourse, allowing researchers to study how AI systems operate in identity-rich forums without disclosure—a growing concern as AI agents increasingly participate in public discourse.

Method: Structured content analysis of AI-generated comments from the r/ChangeMyView corpus, evaluating identity performance, authority signaling, alignment strategies, and cognitive heuristic activation; comparative analysis against human-authored counter-arguments.

Result: Identity targeting in >2/3 of comments; alignment moves and authority claims in nearly all; cognitive bias triggers (confirmation bias, representativeness, availability) in most. Compared to humans, agents showed denser authority use, more adversarial alignment, and heavier reliance on external citation over experiential grounding.

Conclusion: The distinction between authentic and synthetic epistemic standing is increasingly opaque; disclosure mandates alone are insufficient. Auditing frameworks are needed that assess how AI systems structure credibility, not merely whether they are present.

Abstract: This study analyzes a publicly released dataset from a discontinued field experiment on Reddit's r/ChangeMyView. The intervention, conducted by unknown, external researchers and halted following ethical backlash, involved undisclosed AI-generated accounts engaging users in live debate. After public disclosure, Reddit authorized moderators to release an archive of the AI-generated comments, creating a rare opportunity to examine how large language models operated in an identity-rich deliberative forum without disclosure. We conduct a structured content analysis of this corpus, evaluating identity performance, authority signaling, alignment strategies, and activation of cognitive heuristics. Identity targeting or adoption appears in over two-thirds of comments, alignment moves and authority claims in nearly all of them, and cognitive-bias triggers -- particularly confirmation bias, representativeness, and availability -- in the large majority. These patterns co-occur systematically, composing a rhetorical architecture calibrated for persuasive efficiency rather than authentic deliberative participation. Compared against human-authored CMV counter-arguments, the agents inverted the typical distribution on every dimension: denser authority use, more adversarial alignment, and heavier reliance on external citation over experiential grounding. In such environments, distinctions between authentic and synthetic epistemic standing grow increasingly opaque -- an asymmetry that disclosure mandates alone cannot address. The results point toward auditing frameworks capable of assessing how AI systems structure credibility, not merely whether they are present.

</details>


### [2] [What Should Agents Say? Action-state Communication for Efficient Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05304)
*Chen Huang,Yuhao Wu,Wenxuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PACT, a protocol that compresses free-form inter-agent communication in LLM-based multi-agent systems into compact action-state records, consistently improving the performance-cost trade-off across different MAS topologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free-form natural language communication between LLM-based agents leads to excessive token usage, shared context window consumption, and degraded system performance and inference cost. No fixed communication strategy is universally optimal.

Method: PACT (Protocolized Action-state Communication and Transmission) treats inter-agent communication as a public state-update problem, projecting each raw agent output into a compact action-state record before it enters shared history.

Result: PACT consistently improves the performance-cost trade-off across different MAS topologies, achieving comparable or stronger task performance with substantially fewer tokens. On production harnesses: PACT lifts OpenHands' resolve rate at -10% tokens-per-resolved, and is resolve-neutral on SWE-agent while halving input tokens.

Conclusion: Effective inter-agent messages consistently preserve action-centered information. PACT provides a universal approach to compressing agent communication while maintaining or improving performance, with practical gains in production coding environments.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models are typically organized around roles, pipelines, and turn schedules, while the content that agents pass to one another is often left as unconstrained natural language. However, this free-form communication can rapidly inflate token usage, consume the shared context window, and ultimately affect both system performance and inference cost. We analyze five common inter-agent communication strategies across two MAS topologies, finding that no fixed strategy is universally optimal. Instead, effective inter-agent messages consistently preserve action-centered information needed by downstream agents. Building on this, we propose the PACT (Protocolized Action-state Communication and Transmission), which treats inter-agent communication as a public state-update problem and projects each raw agent output into a compact action-state record before it enters shared history. Across different MAS topologies, PACT consistently improves the performance-cost trade-off, achieving comparable or stronger task performance with substantially fewer tokens. The gains extend to production coding harnesses: PACT lifts OpenHands' resolve rate at -10% tokens-per-resolved, and is resolve-neutral on SWE-agent while halving input tokens. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/iNLP-Lab/PACT.

</details>


### [3] [I Know What You Meme, Even If it Emerged Today: Understanding Evolving Memes through Open-World Knowledge Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05316)
*Shanhong Liu,Rui Cao,Pai Chet Ng,De Wen Soh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Query Retrieve Conclude (QRC) is a zero-shot framework that identifies missing knowledge, retrieves open web evidence, and synthesizes background knowledge to improve multimodal meme understanding and detection, outperforming zero-shot baselines across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for multimodal meme understanding rely on fixed parametric knowledge of pretrained models, which is incomplete, outdated, or unavailable for emerging memes that require up-to-date background knowledge.

Method: A zero-shot framework called Query Retrieve Conclude (QRC) that identifies missing knowledge, retrieves open web evidence, and synthesizes evidence-grounded background knowledge for meme understanding and detection.

Result: The framework improves knowledge recovery, meme understanding, and downstream detection over zero-shot baselines on three meme understanding datasets and five meme detection tasks.

Conclusion: QRC effectively addresses the limitation of fixed parametric knowledge by dynamically retrieving and synthesizing up-to-date background knowledge from open web sources, enhancing zero-shot meme understanding and detection.

Abstract: Multimodal memes are dynamic and often require up to date background knowledge for interpretation. Existing methods often overlook such knowledge or rely on fixed parametric knowledge of pretrained models that may be incomplete, outdated, or unavailable for emerging memes. We introduce Query Retrieve Conclude, a zero shot framework that identifies missing knowledge, retrieves open web evidence, and synthesizes evidence grounded background knowledge for meme understanding and detection. We also introduce a curated meme understanding benchmark of recent memes from 2024 to 2026 with external background knowledge annotations. Experiments on three meme understanding datasets and five meme detection tasks show that our framework improves knowledge recovery, meme understanding and downstream detection over zero shot baselines.

</details>


### [4] [GITCO: Gated Inference-Time Context Optimization in TSFMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05332)
*Manya Pandey,Dhruv Kumar,Murari Mandal,Saurabh Deshpande*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GITCO is a lightweight, inference-time framework that detects and suppresses anomalous input patches in patch-based Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) to improve zero-shot forecasting accuracy, achieving +1.95% MASE reduction on TimesFM 2.5 across 53 datasets without any parameter updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Patch-based TSFMs suffer from context poisoning, where structurally anomalous patches disproportionately degrade zero-shot forecast quality. Existing methods require model modifications or retraining, which is costly at inference time.

Method: GITCO consists of three components: Gate (identifies harmful patches), Router (selects which patches to suppress), and Critic (evaluates the intervention quality). It operates at inference time without any parameter updates to the foundation model.

Result: Evaluated on TimesFM 2.5 across 53 GIFT-Eval datasets under K-fold cross-validation, GITCO achieves an average +1.95% MASE reduction and captures 89.9% of the improvement upper bound. It also introduces context sensitivity profiles as a new property of TSFMs.

Conclusion: Inference-time input context optimization is an effective zero-shot accuracy improvement strategy for TSFMs. Context sensitivity profiles reveal that the gain from context intervention is jointly shaped by model architecture and data statistical structure.

Abstract: Patch-based Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) suffer from context poisoning: structurally anomalous patches capture disproportionate attention and silently degrade zero-shot forecast quality. We propose improving TSFM accuracy at inference time by optimizing the input context rather than modifying model weights. We present GITCO (Gated Inference-Time Context Optimization), a lightweight three-component framework: Gate, Router, and Critic that selectively identifies and suppresses harmful patches without any parameter updates. Evaluated on TimesFM 2.5 across 53 GIFT-Eval datasets under K-fold cross-validation, GITCO achieves an average +1.95% MASE reduction on TimesFM 2.5 while capturing 89.9% of the improvement upper bound. We introduce context sensitivity profiles as a new characterizable property of TSFMs: the mapping from time series meta-features to expected accuracy improvement under inference-time context intervention, shaped jointly by model architecture and the statistical structure of the data.

</details>


### [5] [Uncertainty Aware Functional Behavior Prediction and Material Fatigue Assessment for Circular Factory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05334)
*Nehal Afifi,Mehdi Khabou,Victor Mas,Jonas Hemmerich,Patric Grauberger,Stefan Dietrich,Volker Schulze,Sven Matthiesen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a framework that combines uncertainty-aware functional prediction with component-level fatigue assessment for returned products in circular factories, demonstrated on an angle grinder. A convolutional encoder and LSTM predict nine functional variables, while finite-element-based stress reconstruction and damage evaluation assess fatigue. A streaming replay algorithm integrates both into reliability trajectories, achieving high accuracy in held-out tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Returned products in circular factories have heterogeneous degradation states, and reuse decisions cannot be made from inspection alone. Existing PHM approaches target fixed conditions or isolated components, lacking linkage between material-fatigue assessment and system-level functional prognosis.

Method: The framework combines a convolutional encoder (extracting loading patterns from force-torque data) with an LSTM backbone predicting nine functional variables as Gaussian distributions. Parallel finite-element stress reconstruction, S–N/Miner damage evaluation with Haibach extension, and Paris-law crack-growth analysis assess fatigue. A streaming replay algorithm integrates both into functional, material, and system reliability trajectories.

Result: Mean 2%-tolerance accuracy of 0.9652 across nine outputs. Thermal variables predicted near-perfectly; drive motor current (R²=0.9750) and load speed (R²=0.9924) are most demanding. Torque history is critical; LSTM outperforms GRU and xLSTM in short-history settings. Reliability calibration most informative for drive motor current.

Conclusion: The proposed instance-specific reliability workflow effectively bridges component-level fatigue assessment and system-level functional prognosis for heterogeneous returned products, enabling more informed reuse decisions in circular manufacturing.

Abstract: Returned products in circular factories re-enter production with heterogeneous degradation states, usage histories, and remaining capability. Reuse cannot be decided from the current inspection alone, because future function fulfillment and component integrity may evolve differently under the next service scenario. Existing PHM approaches support degradation prediction, but often target fixed operating conditions or isolated component benchmarks, while material-fatigue assessment is rarely linked to system-level functional prognosis. This paper addresses this gap for an angle grinder by combining uncertainty-aware functional prediction with component-level fatigue assessment in an instance-specific reliability workflow. The proposed framework combines the current tool state with recent force--torque usage windows. A convolutional encoder extracts loading patterns from spindle forces and shaft torque, and an LSTM backbone predicts nine functional variables as Gaussian mean and variance estimates. In parallel, the same loading history is translated into output-shaft fatigue information through finite-element-supported stress reconstruction, S--N/Miner damage evaluation with Haibach extension, and Paris-law crack-growth analysis. A streaming replay algorithm consolidates both branches into functional, material, and system reliability trajectories. Held-out tests show mean \(2\%\)-tolerance accuracy of 0.9652 across nine outputs. Thermal variables are predicted near-perfectly, while drive motor current and load speed remain the most demanding dynamic outputs, with \(R^2\) values of 0.9750 and 0.9924. Torque history is especially important for these variables, and the conventional LSTM outperforms GRU and xLSTM in the short-history setting. Reliability calibration is most informative for drive motor current, where predicted and observed exceedance probabilities ...

</details>


### [6] [SentinelBench: A Benchmark for Long-Running Monitoring Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05342)
*Matheus Kunzler Maldaner,Adam Fourney,Amanda Swearngin,Hussein Mozzanar,Gagan Bansal,Maya Murad,Rafah Hosn,Saleema Amershi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SentinelBench is a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on long-running, time-evolving monitoring tasks where sustained attention and timely response matter more than continuous action.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agents default to continuous action (e.g., constant tool calls, page refreshes), which is inefficient for long-running tasks. The paper argues that agents should instead adopt sustained attention — monitoring environments and responding only when events enable progress — and provides a benchmark to measure this capability.

Method: The authors introduce SentinelBench, an open-source benchmark with 100 tasks across 10 synthetic web environments (email, calendars, finance, professional networking, entertainment). Each environment has a live web interface that replays scripted event sequences, requiring agents to navigate web pages whose state changes over time. The benchmark measures task completion, reaction time, and resource usage.

Result: Experiments across three models and two browser-agent harnesses establish performance baselines. Results demonstrate that SentinelBench can distinguish meaningful differences in agent behavior, particularly the tradeoff between responsiveness and cost, and that agent design choices significantly impact key metrics.

Conclusion: Sustained attention is a better strategy than continuous action for many long-running agent tasks. SentinelBench effectively captures this distinction and provides a standardized evaluation for time-evolving monitoring tasks.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly asked to carry out work that spans minutes, hours, or longer. Yet the default model of agent behavior is continuous action: issuing tool calls, refreshing pages, searching for alternatives, or otherwise trying to force progress. This is the wrong approach for many long-running tasks, which are better served by a strategy of sustained attention. Instead, agents should monitor an environment, notice when an external event makes progress possible, then respond promptly without wasting resources while waiting. To measure progress on this class of tasks, we introduce SentinelBench, an open-source benchmark for time-evolving monitoring tasks.
  SentinelBench contains 100 tasks across 10 synthetic web environments, including email, calendars, finance, professional networking, and entertainment. Each environment exposes a live web interface and replays a scripted sequence of events, requiring agents to navigate and reason about web pages whose state shifts underfoot. SentinelBench measures task completion, reaction time, and resource use, exposing the tradeoff between responsiveness and cost. We report results across three models and two browser-agent harnesses, establishing performance baselines for future comparison and demonstrating how agent design choices can dramatically impact key metrics. Together, these results show that SentinelBench distinguishes meaningful differences in agent behavior.

</details>


### [7] [An interpretable and trustworthy AI framework for large-scale longitudinal structure-pain association studies using data from the Osteoarthritis Initiative (OAI)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05357)
*Jincheng Yu,Haoyang Li,Yiwen Liu,Shen Liu,Rachel Yuanbao Chen,C. Kent Kwoh,Hongxu Ding,Xiaoxiao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An interpretable AI framework combining deep learning-based MOAKS prediction (with conformal prediction for uncertainty quantification) and longitudinal latent class mixed modeling was developed to study knee structure-pain relationships in osteoarthritis, using data from the OAI. High-confidence predictions expanded the sample to 2,175 knees, revealing that bone marrow lesions, cartilage loss, and meniscal extrusion are significant risk factors for rapid pain progression (odds ratios: 1.62, 1.83, 2.50 respectively).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an interpretable and trustworthy AI framework that combines deep learning-based MOAKS prediction with interpretable statistical modeling to study structure-pain relationships at scale in knee osteoarthritis.

Method: 1) A deep learning framework to predict MOAKS features from knee MRIs with conformal prediction for uncertainty quantification, filtering only high-confidence predictions. 2) A longitudinal latent class mixed model (LCMM) to examine associations between structural abnormalities (BML, cartilage loss, meniscal extrusion) and four complementary knee pain measurements.

Result: MCC improved substantially: BML from 0.69 to 0.91, CART from 0.45 to 0.80, ME from 0.59 to 0.89. Sample expanded to 2,175 knees. Two pain trajectories identified (rapid and stable). Odds ratios for rapid progression: BML 1.62 (1.12-2.35), CART loss 1.83 (1.24-2.70), ME 2.50 (1.75-3.57).

Conclusion: Structural abnormalities (BML, cartilage loss, meniscal extrusion) are significant risk factors for pain and functional progression in osteoarthritis, highlighting their clinical importance.

Abstract: Purpose: To develop an interpretable and trustworthy AI framework that combines deep learning based MRI Osteoarthritis Knee Score (MOAKS) prediction with interpretable statistical modeling to study structure-pain relationships at scale using data from the Osteoarthritis Initiative (OAI). Materials and Methods: We first developed a deep learning framework to predict MOAKS features directly from knee MRIs and incorporated conformal prediction to provide prediction uncertainty quantification. This uncertainty-aware strategy enables explicit filtering of model outputs, retaining only high-confidence MOAKS predictions at the knee level. Second, we applied a longitudinal latent class mixed model (LCMM) to examine associations between key structural abnormalities and four complementary knee pain measurements. Results: Among the three MRI-defined abnormalities (i.e., bone marrow lesions (BML), cartilage loss (CART), and meniscal extrusion (ME)), our framework substantially improved the Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) and some other metrics. For example, MCC increased from 0.69 to 0.91 for BML, from 0.45 to 0.80 for CART, and from 0.59 to 0.89 for ME. Using these high-confidence predictions, we expanded the sample size to 2,175 knees for the LCMM analysis. Two distinct pain trajectories were identified (rapid and stable pain progression). The estimated odds ratios (95% CI) for the rapid progression group were 1.62 (1.12-2.35) for BML, 1.83 (1.24-2.70) for CART loss, and 2.50 (1.75-3.57) for ME. Conclusion: These results highlight the importance of these structural abnormalities as risk factors for pain and functional progression in osteoarthritis.

</details>


### [8] [Synthetic Contrastive Reasoning for Multi-Table Q&A](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05382)
*Ankit Pratap Singh,Xin Su,Phillip Howard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A synthetic contrastive reasoning-trace dataset for MMQA is constructed using heterogeneous LLMs, and fine-tuning open-weight LLMs with Contrastive Preference Optimization (CPO) yields absolute average improvements of 9.7%-16.3% over SFT, with gains up to 21 points on MMQA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-table Q&A resources provide questions and final answers but lack reasoning supervision explaining how answers are derived, hindering compositional reasoning across relational tables.

Method: Construct a synthetic contrastive reasoning-trace dataset by generating validated positive traces and plausible negative traces using heterogeneous LLMs, then fine-tune open-weight LLMs (Qwen3-14B, Mistral-8B, Llama-3.1-8B) with Contrastive Preference Optimization (CPO).

Result: CPO achieves 9.7%-16.3% absolute average improvements over Q&A supervised fine-tuning, with gains up to 21 percentage points on MMQA. Heterogeneous positive and negative trace generators strengthen the contrastive signal.

Conclusion: The generated preference pairs are largely faithful, coherent, and meaningfully contrastive, providing effective reasoning supervision for multi-table question answering.

Abstract: Multi-table question answering requires models to retrieve relevant evidence, link schemas, and perform compositional reasoning across relational tables. Existing multi-table Q&A resources typically provide questions and final answers but lack reasoning supervision that explains how answers are derived. To address this gap, we construct a synthetic contrastive reasoning-trace dataset for MMQA by generating validated positive traces and plausible negative traces with heterogeneous LLMs. We then use the resulting preference pairs to fine-tune open-weight LLMs with Contrastive Preference Optimization (CPO). Across Qwen3-14B, Mistral-8B, and Llama-3.1-8B, CPO achieves absolute average improvements over Q&A supervised fine-tuning ranging from 9.7%-16.3%, with gains up to 21 percentage points on MMQA. Ablations show that heterogeneous positive and negative trace generators strengthen the contrastive signal, and automated as well as human evaluations indicate that the generated pairs are largely faithful, coherent, and meaningfully contrastive.

</details>


### [9] [Stability vs. Manipulability: Evaluating Robustness Under Post-Decision Interaction in LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05384)
*Srimonti Dutta,Akshata Kishore Moharir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM judges, assumed to produce stable evaluations, can be manipulated through post-decision conversation—especially under targeted challenge and authority framing—leading to rank shifts, reduced human agreement, and harmful revisions. The authors propose the Evaluation Robustness Score (ERS) to quantify this vulnerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge evaluation assumes judgments are stable properties of fixed inputs, but this assumption breaks under interaction. The authors aim to study post-decision manipulability—whether and how subsequent conversation can alter an LLM judge's initial evaluation.

Method: Controlled experiments on MT-Bench and AlpacaEval using (1) an anti-baseline challenge protocol to test overturning stable judgments via motivated interaction, and (2) a counterbalanced target-validation protocol to separate reversibility from net target-directed steering. Evaluation Robustness Score (ERS) combines reversal susceptibility with counterbalanced directional effects.

Result: LLM judges are stable under repeated neutral reevaluation but highly reversible under targeted post-decision challenge. Reversals degrade human agreement, shift benchmark rankings, and produce harmful evaluation changes despite high self-reported confidence. Authority framing is especially destabilizing; revised judgments show low-overlap justifications indicating post hoc rationalization.

Conclusion: Post-decision interaction is a distinct failure mode for LLM-as-judge evaluation. Evaluation protocols should measure not only static agreement but also robustness under challenge.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge evaluation is widely used in benchmarking pipelines, where model outputs are compared and ranked using automated evaluators. These pipelines typically assume that judgments are stable properties of fixed inputs. We show that this assumption does not hold under interaction. We study post-decision manipulability: the extent to which an evaluation outcome can be altered through subsequent conversation with the judge after an initial decision has been made. Across controlled experiments on MT-Bench and AlpacaEval, we find that LLM judges are highly stable under repeated and neutral reevaluation, yet become substantially reversible under targeted post-decision challenge. An anti-baseline challenge protocol shows that stable judgments can be overturned through motivated interaction, while a counterbalanced target-validation protocol separates this reversibility from net target-directed steering. These reversals have practical consequences: they can degrade agreement with human preferences, shift benchmark rankings, and produce harmful evaluation changes despite high self-reported confidence. Authority framing is especially destabilizing, and revised judgments are often accompanied by low-overlap justifications, suggesting post hoc rationalization rather than reliable error correction. We introduce the Evaluation Robustness Score (ERS) to quantify interactional robustness by combining reversal susceptibility with counterbalanced directional effects. Our findings identify post-decision interaction as a distinct failure mode for LLM-as-judge evaluation and motivate evaluation protocols that measure not only static agreement, but robustness under challenge.

</details>


### [10] [Residual Modeling for High-Fidelity Learned Compression of Scientific Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05389)
*Liangji Zhu,Sanjay Ranka,Anand Rangarajan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes residual-centric coding methods (LBRC and NGLR) for learned lossy compression of scientific spatiotemporal data, achieving 30-60% and 10-40% compression ratio improvements over GAE and LBRC respectively in the high-fidelity regime (NRMSE 10^-6 to 10^-4).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Guaranteed Autoencoder (GAE) methods add per-block SVD/PCA-style residual correction to meet accuracy targets, but in the high-fidelity regime (NRMSE 10^-6 to 10^-4), the number of retained coefficients grows quickly and the correction stream dominates the total rate, making learned compression less advantageous.

Method: Two residual coders are introduced: (1) LBRC — a deterministic, training-free pipeline that adaptively quantizes the learned residual to the target NRMSE and losslessly encodes it using 3D Lorenzo differencing, zigzag mapping, bit-plane coding, and entropy coding. (2) NGLR — adds a causal neural predictor that outputs a normalized bias for integer-rounded Lorenzo prediction within the same deterministic integer pipeline, reducing residual entropy while preserving deterministic decoding; predictor weights are serialized and counted in the bitstream.

Result: Across E3SM, JHTDB, and ERA5 at block-level NRMSE targets from 10^-6 to 10^-4: LBRC improves compression ratio over GAE by 30-60% and is broadly competitive with SZ. NGLR adds a further 10-40% improvement over LBRC and outperforms SZ in the high-fidelity regime.

Conclusion: Residual representations tailored to learned-compressor residuals preserve the advantage of learned compression when global residual correction becomes rate-dominant, demonstrating that effective residual coding is key to maintaining learned compression efficiency at stringent accuracy targets.

Abstract: Lossy compression is essential for massive spatiotemporal data from scientific simulations. Learned compressors can achieve high compression ratios at moderate accuracy targets, but their aggregate reconstruction losses do not guarantee accuracy for each block. Existing Guaranteed Autoencoder (GAE) methods add a per-block residual correction by retaining SVD/PCA-style coefficients until the target is met. This works at moderate tolerances, but in the high-fidelity regime with block-level NRMSE from 10^-6 to 10^-4, the number of retained coefficients grows quickly and the correction stream dominates the total rate.
  We propose a residual-centric view: the learned residual is structurally different from the original scientific field and should be coded with a representation designed for that residual. We introduce two residual coders. LBRC is a deterministic, training-free pipeline that adaptively quantizes the learned residual to the target NRMSE and losslessly encodes the resulting integer residual using 3D Lorenzo differencing, zigzag mapping, bit-plane coding, and entropy coding. NGLR adds a causal neural predictor that outputs a normalized bias for an integer-rounded Lorenzo prediction in the same deterministic integer pipeline, reducing the entropy of the remaining residual code while preserving deterministic decoding. The predictor weights are serialized and counted in the bitstream.
  Across E3SM, JHTDB, and ERA5 at block-level NRMSE targets from 10^-6 to 10^-4, LBRC improves compression ratio over GAE by 30-60% and is broadly competitive with SZ. NGLR adds a further 10-40% over LBRC and outperforms SZ in the evaluated high-fidelity regime. These results show that residual representations tailored to learned-compressor residuals can preserve the advantage of learned compression when global residual correction becomes rate-dominant.

</details>


### [11] [LeanMarathon: Toward Reliable AI Co-Mathematicians through Long-Horizon Lean Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05400)
*Yuanhe Zhang,Yuekai Sun,Taiji Suzuki,Jason D. Lee,Fanghui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LeanMarathon is a multi-agent harness for reliable research-level Lean autoformalization that uses an evolving blueprint abstraction and a two-stage orchestrator to turn brittle multi-hour runs into many local, recoverable, parallel transactions. It formalized all seven target theorems across four Erdős problems from two research papers with no sorry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon autoformalization of research mathematics fails not only at hard lemmas but at scale due to statement drift, tangled dependencies, context decay, and local repairs corrupting distant work.

Method: The core abstraction is an evolving blueprint (a Lean file serving as formal proof skeleton, natural-language proof graph, and shared system of record). Four contract-scoped agents (construct, audit, prove, repair) are coordinated by a two-stage orchestrator that first stabilizes target fidelity through adversarial review and then discharges the proof DAG from dynamic leaves upward in parallel CI-gated rounds.

Result: Evaluated on two recent research papers spanning four Erdős problems (#1051, #1196, #164, #1217). Across three autonomous runs, it formalizes all seven target theorems with no sorry, proving 258 lemmas and theorems.

Conclusion: Reliable AI co-mathematics requires not only stronger provers, but durable harnesses that preserve target fidelity across long mathematical developments.

Abstract: Long-horizon autoformalization of research mathematics fails not only at hard lemmas, but at scale: statements drift, dependencies tangle, context decays, and local repairs corrupt distant work. We present LeanMarathon, a multi-agent harness for reliable research-level Lean autoformalization. Its core abstraction is an evolving blueprint: a Lean file that serves simultaneously as formal proof skeleton, natural-language proof graph, and shared system of record. Four contract-scoped agents construct, audit, prove, and repair this blueprint. These agents are coordinated by a two-stage orchestrator that first stabilizes target fidelity through adversarial review and then discharges the proof directed acyclic graph (DAG) from its dynamic leaves upward in parallel CI-gated rounds. LeanMarathon turns one brittle multi-hour run into many local, recoverable, parallel transactions. We evaluate LeanMarathon on two recent research papers spanning four Erdős problems (#1051, #1196, #164, #1217). Across three autonomous runs, it formalizes all seven target theorems with no sorry, proving 258 lemmas and theorems. These results show that reliable AI co-mathematics requires not only stronger provers, but durable harnesses that preserve target fidelity across long mathematical developments. The code can be found at https://github.com/YuanheZ/LeanMarathon.

</details>


### [12] [Harnessing Generalist Agents for Contextualized Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05404)
*Zihao Li,Kaifeng Jin,Yuanchen Bei,Jiaru Zou,Avaneesh Kumar,Xuying Ning,Yanjun Zhao,Mengting Ai,Baoyu Jing,Hanghang Tong,Jingrui He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TimeClaw is an agentic harness framework that equips generalist LLM agents with time series-native runtime support (executable temporal tools, experience-driven capability evolution, episodic multimodal memory) for contextualized temporal reasoning, demonstrating improved performance across energy, finance, weather, traffic, and other real-world domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series analysis requires rich contextual understanding and end-to-end workflows beyond isolated forecasting tasks. Generalist AI agents operate primarily in textual spaces that are not fully aligned with structured temporal signals, motivating the need for a framework that bridges this gap.

Method: TimeClaw integrates three components: (1) executable temporal tools for grounded and auditable analysis, (2) experience-driven capability evolution for creating reusable analytical routines, and (3) episodic multimodal memory for retrieving relevant reasoning traces. These enable harnessed open-ended temporal reasoning with contextual information.

Result: Extensive evaluation across multiple benchmarks covering diverse tasks (energy, finance, weather, traffic, and other real-world domains) demonstrates improved performance of TimeClaw over baselines.

Conclusion: TimeClaw effectively bridges the gap between generalist LLM agents and structured temporal signals, providing a practical framework for contextualized time series analysis with auditable, reusable, and memory-enhanced reasoning.

Abstract: Time series are often embedded in rich contexts that are essential for holistic modeling. Moreover, real-world practitioners often require end-to-end workflows for analyzing temporal dynamics, where widely studied tasks such as forecasting are only one step in a broader solution loop. While generalist AI agents offer a promising interface for such workflows under complex contexts, they still operate primarily in textual spaces that are not fully aligned with structured temporal signals. In this work, we introduce TimeClaw, an agentic harness framework for time series that equips generalist LLM agents with the time series-native runtime support needed for contextualized temporal reasoning. TimeClaw integrates executable temporal tools for grounded and auditable analysis, experience-driven capability evolution for creating reusable analytical routines, and episodic multimodal memory for retrieving relevant reasoning traces. Together, these components unlock harnessed open-ended temporal reasoning with contextual information. Extensive evaluation on multiple benchmarks covering diverse tasks across energy, finance, weather, traffic, and other real-world domains demonstrates improved performance of TimeClaw. Code is available at https://github.com/iDEA-iSAIL-Lab-UIUC/TimeClaw.

</details>


### [13] [Agents' Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05405)
*Yiyou Sun,Xinyang Han,Weichen Zhang,Yuanbo Pang,Tianyu Wang,Yuhan Cao,Yixiao Huang,Chris Duroiu,Haoyun Zhang,Jeffrey Lin,Weishu Zhang,Tyler Zeng,Ying Yan,Bo Liu,Hanson Wen,Mingyang Xu,Xiaoyuan Liu,Zimeng Chen,Weiyan Shi,Amanda Dsouza,Vincent Sunn Chen,Patrick Bryant,Carl Boettiger,Yamini Rangan,Bradley Rothenberg,Kyle Steinfeld,Arvind Rao,Tapio Schneider,Georgios Yannakakis,Laure Zanna,Kaan Spanos,Lichao Sun,Chenguang Wang,Orson Xu,Zhen Dong,Hector Gomez,Aylin Caliskan,Ali Emami,Haimin Hu,Zhi Li,Lihui Liu,Murphy Niu,Yi Shao,Jianxin Sun,Mikko Tolonen,Ting Wang,Sanjiv Das,Yanjun Gao,Wenbo Guo,Erika J Schneider,Zhiyong Lu,Mark Mueller,Radha Poovendran,Somayeh Sojoudi,Dawn Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Agents' Last Exam (ALE), a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on long-horizon, economically valuable real-world tasks. Developed with 250+ experts across 55 subfields and 13 industry clusters covering 1K+ tasks, ALE reveals that current AI systems achieve only a 2.6% average full pass rate on its hardest tier, highlighting a significant gap between benchmark performance and economically meaningful deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI benchmarks show strong performance gains that fail to translate into economically meaningful deployment across professional domains. This evaluation gap stems from the lack of sustained performance measurement on real, economically valuable workflows.

Method: ALE is developed in collaboration with 250+ industry experts, organized around a task taxonomy with 55 subfields grouped into 13 industry clusters covering 1K+ tasks. It focuses on non-physical industries defined with reference to O*NET/SOC 2018 (U.S. federal occupational taxonomy) and evaluates AI agents on long-horizon, real-world tasks with verifiable outcomes. It is designed as a living benchmark with continuously growing task pools.

Result: Across mainstream harness and backbone configurations, the average full pass rate on the hardest tier is only 2.6%, indicating that current AI systems are far from saturating economically meaningful benchmarks.

Conclusion: ALE is intended as an instrument for closing the gap between benchmark success and GDP-relevant impact, serving not merely as another leaderboard but as a tool for driving economically meaningful AI progress.

Abstract: Recent AI systems have achieved strong results on a wide range of benchmarks, yet these gains have not translated into economically meaningful deployment across many professional domains. We argue that this gap is largely an evaluation problem: widely used benchmarks lack sustained performance measurement on real and economically valuable workflows. This paper introduces Agents' Last Exam (ALE), a benchmark designed to evaluate AI agents on long-horizon, economically valuable, real-world tasks with verifiable outcomes. Developed in collaboration with 250+ industry experts, ALE covers non-physical industries defined with reference to O*NET / SOC 2018 (the U.S. federal occupational taxonomy). It is organized around a task taxonomy with 55 subfields grouped into 13 industry clusters covering 1K+ tasks. Current results show that the hardest tier remains far from saturated: across mainstream harness and backbone configurations, the average full pass rate is 2.6%. ALE is designed as a living benchmark: its task pool grows continuously as new workflows and industries are onboarded. More broadly, ALE is intended not merely as another leaderboard, but as an instrument for closing the gap between benchmark success and GDP-relevant impact.

</details>


### [14] [Mutation Without Variation: Convergence Dynamics in LLM-Driven Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05408)
*Can Gurkan,Forrest Stonedahl,Uri Wilensky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-driven program mutation, even without selection pressure, exhibits strong convergence toward restricted structural attractors, with 87% of chains showing over 93% revisitation of prior forms—revealing a systematic bias toward structural homogeneity unlike classical GP mutation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLM-based program mutation in the absence of selection pressure leads to exploration of novel program forms or convergence to repeated ones, uncovering potential limitations for open-ended program evolution.

Method: Analyzed LLM-driven mutation chains within a domain-specific language without selection pressure, varying prompt design, model family, and stochastic replication; compared against a classical GP subtree mutation operator.

Result: LLM-based mutation consistently converges toward attractor regions in program space; 87% of chains have over 93% structural revisitation; cycles are dominated by short loops and self-loops; convergence rate varies with prompt and model but is robust; GP mutation does not exhibit comparable convergence.

Conclusion: The semantics-aware capabilities of LLMs that enable program transformation also inherently bias toward structural homogeneity, posing a challenge for sustaining open-ended exploration in LLM-driven program evolution.

Abstract: When an LLM repeatedly mutates a program, does it explore new forms or circle back to the same ones? We study this question by analyzing LLM-driven mutation chains in the absence of selection pressure within a domain-specific language, varying prompt design, model family, and stochastic replication. We find that LLM-based mutation consistently converges toward restricted attractor regions in program space. Convergence is especially severe at the structural level: in 87% of chains, over 93% of mutations revisit a previously seen structural form, with most variation confined to terminal substitutions within recurring templates. Cycle analysis reveals short cycles and self-loops dominating the transition structure. The rate of convergence varies with prompt wording and model choice, but the phenomenon is robust across conditions. A classical GP subtree mutation operator does not exhibit comparable convergence, suggesting that the effect is intrinsic to the LLM mutation pipeline. These findings reveal a tension at the heart of LLM-driven program evolution: the same capabilities that enable semantics-aware program transformation also carry a systematic bias toward structural homogeneity that must be accounted for if such systems are to sustain open-ended exploration. Source code is available at https://github.com/can-gurkan/lmca.

</details>


### [15] [A Motivational Architecture for Conversational AGI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05411)
*Anna Mikeda,Ben Goertzel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reinterprets the OpenPsi motivational architecture for conversational agents, replacing biological homeostasis with dialogue-native motivational drives (competence, uncertainty reduction, affiliation, etc.) and proposes a ten-stage processing pipeline, a dual decision strategy (fast vs. deliberative), and a distinction between pre-action feelings and post-action emotions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motivational architectures in cognitive AI are designed for physical agents regulating bodily needs, but conversational agents operate in a fundamentally different regime where the sensorimotor loop is linguistic, the environment is a user's mental state, and actions are speech acts. Thus, a new motivational framework tailored for conversational agents is needed.

Method: The paper proposes (1) a ten-stage motivational processing pipeline that separates cognitive modulation from situational appraisal; (2) a dual decision strategy blending urgency-driven fast response with deliberative multi-goal optimization; and (3) a functional distinction between pre-action feelings and post-action emotions. The framework reinterprets OpenPsi homeostasis in dialogue-native terms (competence, uncertainty reduction, affiliation, affinity, legitimacy, nurturing, aesthetic coherence) and couples it with MetaMo's motivational scaffold on a modular execution substrate.

Result: The framework is specialized to two example agents (CompanionAgent and ResearchAgent), demonstrating its applicability in conversational domains. The paper also sketches extension pathways to social robotics and domain-generic human-level AGI.

Conclusion: A conversational reinterpretation of motivational architectures is both feasible and necessary for agents operating in linguistic sensorimotor loops. The proposed pipeline, dual decision strategy, and affect distinction provide a principled foundation for building socially aware, motivationally driven conversational agents.

Abstract: Motivational architectures in cognitive AI have largely been designed for physical agents regulating bodily needs. Conversational agents operate in a different regime: their sensorimotor loop is linguistic, their environment is a user's evolving mental state, and their consequential actions are speech acts, tool invocations, and strategic silences. This paper proposes a conversational reinterpretation of the OpenPsi motivational lineage, coupled to MetaMo's higher-level motivational scaffold, for agents built on a modular execution substrate. Homeostasis is recast in dialogue-native terms: the agent regulates competence, uncertainty reduction, affiliation, affinity, legitimacy, nurturing, and aesthetic coherence rather than bodily deficits. We propose three contributions: a ten-stage motivational processing pipeline that architecturally separates cognitive modulation from situational appraisal; a dual decision strategy blending urgency-driven fast response with deliberative multi-goal optimization; and an architecturally useful distinction between pre-action feelings and post-action emotions as functionally different forms of affect. We specialize the framework to two example agents -- CompanionAgent and ResearchAgent -- and sketch its extension to social robotics and domain-generic human-level AGI.

</details>


### [16] [Assessing the Carbon Emissions and Energy Consumption of U.S. Hyperscale Data Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05420)
*Gianluca Guidi,Francesca Dominici,Tiziano Squartini,Callaway Sprinkle,Jonathan Gilmour,Kevin Butler,Eric Bell,Scott Delaney,Falco J. Bargagli-Stoffi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 403 US hyperscale data centers (May 2024–April 2025), finding they consumed 68–99 TWh of electricity (≈1.8% of US total), with ~54% from fossil fuels, and a carbon intensity of 545 gCO2/kWh—48% above the national grid average of 370 gCO2/kWh—resulting in 37–54 million metric tons of CO2 emissions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid proliferation of hyperscale data centers (HDCs) in the US, driven by AI adoption, has raised concerns about this industry's environmental footprint, motivating the need for systematic assessment.

Method: Compiled facility-level information on 403 US HDCs operating between May 2024 and April 2025, estimated electricity consumption, electricity sources, and attributable CO2 emissions using EPA eGRID plant-level data under multiple facility-load scenarios.

Result: HDCs consumed ~68–99 TWh of electricity, associated with ~37–54 million metric tons of CO2. Under the central scenario, HDC electricity demand was ~1.8% of total US electricity consumption, with ~54% from fossil fuels. The HDC electricity-weighted average carbon intensity was ~545 gCO2/kWh, ~48% above the US grid average (370 gCO2/kWh).

Conclusion: The approach provides an attributional tool for assessing the environmental footprint of hyperscale data centers using the most recent EPA eGRID plant-level data, highlighting that HDCs have substantially higher carbon intensity than the national average.

Abstract: The rapid proliferation of hyperscale data centers (HDCs) in the US, mainly driven by the adoption of artificial intelligence, has raised concerns about this industry's environmental footprint. We compiled facility-level information on 403 US hyperscale data centers operating between May 2024 and April 2025 and estimated their electricity consumption, electricity sources, and attributable CO2 emissions. Across different facility-load scenarios, these HDCs consumed approximately 68-99 TWh of electricity and were associated with about 37-54 million metric tons of CO2. Under the central scenario, HDC electricity demand corresponded to approximately 1.8% of total US electricity consumption, with roughly 54% of attributed generation supplied by fossil-fuel sources. The HDC electricity-weighted average carbon intensity was approximately 545 gCO2/kWh, about 48% above the contemporaneous US national grid-average carbon intensity of 370 gCO2/kWh. Our approach provides an attributional tool for assessing the environmental footprint of hyperscale data centers using the most recent EPA eGRID plant-level data.

</details>


### [17] [Minimizing the Hidden Cost of Scales: Graph-Guided Ultra-Low-Bit Quantization for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05429)
*Rayyan Abdalla,Amir Hussein,Min Wu,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE-PTQ is a novel ultra-low-bit quantization framework for LLMs that uses saliency-aware graph modeling to separate salient and unsalient weights, achieving 1.03 weight bits and 0.004 scaling bits per matrix with superior perplexity and speed over SOTA methods like BiLLM and PB-LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ultra-low-bit PTQ methods rely on rigid weight-saliency assumptions or position heuristics, which introduce substantial hidden scaling overhead, motivating the need for a more efficient quantization framework.

Method: SAGE-PTQ separates salient and unsalient weights using distributional statistics, models subsampled unsalient weights as a sparse graph to estimate optimal groups per layer, applies dual-mode quantization (multi-bit for salient, binarization for unsalient), uses one per-channel scale for salient and one scalar per unsalient group, and implements adaptive saliency thresholding per matrix.

Result: Achieves 1.03 weight bits and 0.004 scaling bits per matrix; on LLaMA-3-8B achieves 6.74 WikiText2 perplexity vs 55.8 for BiLLM with <50% GPU memory; on LLaMA-2-70B provides 1.5x faster decoding on one L40 GPU.

Conclusion: SAGE-PTQ effectively minimizes hidden scaling cost while outperforming SOTA methods in both quantization efficiency and inference performance, making it practical for ultra-low-bit LLM deployment.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is critical for the efficient deployment of large language models (LLMs). Recent ultra-low-bit PTQ methods rely on rigid weight-saliency assumptions or position heuristics, introducing substantial hidden scaling overhead. We propose SAGE-PTQ (Saliency-Aware Graph-guided Efficient PTQ), a novel ultra-low-bit quantization framework for LLMs that minimizes hidden scaling cost. SAGE-PTQ separates salient and unsalient weights using distributional statistics, then models subsampled unsalient weights as a sparse graph to estimate the optimal number of groups per layer. SAGE-PTQ applies dual-mode quantization, assigning multi-bit precision to salient weights and binarizing unsalient weights. To reduce scaling overhead, SAGE-PTQ uses one per-channel scale for salient weights and one scalar per unsalient group. Finally, SAGE-PTQ implements adaptive saliency thresholding to select the optimal saliency ratio per matrix. SAGE-PTQ achieves 1.03 weight bits and only 0.004 scaling bits per matrix on average, outperforming state-of-the-art methods such as BiLLM and PB-LLM. On LLaMA-3-8B, SAGE-PTQ achieves 6.74 WikiText2 perplexity, compared to 55.8 for BiLLM, while using less than 50% of BiLLM's GPU memory. On LLaMA-2-70B, SAGE-PTQ provides 1.5x faster decoding on one NVIDIA L40 GPU, demonstrating practical inference efficiency.

</details>


### [18] [Zero knowledge verification for frontier AI training is possible](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05433)
*Pierre Peigné,Ky Nguyen,Paul Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a zero-knowledge proof-based verification architecture for frontier AI training that can generate proofs of correct training with single-digit-percent overhead, deployable within ~36 months, overcoming previous assumptions that ZK proofs were impractical for frontier-scale training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current frontier AI governance relies on self-reporting of training compute because no technical verification primitive exists for training. This undermines enforcement of any future international agreements, which historically require technical verification to be effective.

Method: Combines pre-committed training specifications, inter-node network observations, on-the-fly Merkle commitments of intermediate computation, verified through a zero-knowledge Virtual Machine (zkVM) with native BF16/FP32 precompiles. Produces three proof types: genesis proof, in-training step proofs, and ex-ante attestations enforcing policy-relevant invariants.

Result: Estimates proof-of-concept deployment within ~36 months at single-digit-percent training-side overhead, compared to 6-10 years for verification-grade custom silicon. Identifies 13 open research problems for external contribution.

Conclusion: The impracticality of ZK proofs for frontier training verification is paradigm-bound, not fundamental. The proposed architecture is feasible and could transform training records into governance-enforceable artefacts.

Abstract: Frontier AI governance frameworks increasingly use cumulative training compute as the primary criterion for designating high-impact models, but enforcement rests on self-reporting because no technical verification primitive for training exists. Any future international agreement on frontier AI faces the same problem at higher stakes: coordinated regulation of technologies with significant externalities has historically rested on technical verification, without which agreements are declaratory. Recent governance analyses judge zero-knowledge proofs a promising candidate but currently impractical at frontier scale [26, 4]. We argue the impracticality is paradigm-bound rather than fundamental, and propose a verification architecture for frontier dense pre-training combining a pre-committed training specification, inter-node network observations, and on-the-fly Merkle commitments of intermediate computation, verified through a zero-knowledge Virtual Machine (zkVM) with native BF16/FP32 precompiles. The proof checks the actual floating-point computation the GPU performed rather than a fixed-point approximation, and preserves model-architecture confidentiality through a private training specification. The protocol produces three proof types: a genesis proof at initialisation, in-training step proofs across the run, and ex-ante attestations enforcing policy-relevant claims as running invariants, turning the training record into a governance-enforceable artefact. We estimate a deployable proof of concept within approximately 36 months at single-digit-percent training-side overhead, against a six-to-ten-year cycle for verification-grade custom silicon. Thirteen open research and engineering problems are catalogued as a research agenda for external contribution

</details>


### [19] [Ten Headache Specialists versus Artificial Intelligence for Clinical Literature Summarization: A Critical Evaluation and Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05436)
*Alejandro Lozano,Keiko Ihara,Ping-Hao Yang,Carrie E. Robertson,Jennifer Stern,Allan Purdy,Hsiangkuo Yuan,Pengfei Zhang,Yulia Orlova,Olga Fermo,Jennifer Hranilovich,Fred Cohen,Todd J. Schwedt,Jenelle A. Jindal,Serena Yeung-Levy,Chia-Chun Chiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study comparing RAG-based LLM-generated (Sonnet, GPT-4o, Llama 3.1) and expert-written medical literature summaries for headache disorders found that expert-written summaries were preferred, though experts sometimes struggled to distinguish human from AI summaries, revealing key features valued by clinicians beyond standard metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evidence-based medicine requires synthesizing rapidly growing medical literature for clinical decision-making, but clinicians face time constraints. While RAG-based LLMs show promise, human evaluations comparing LLM-generated clinical summaries to expert-written syntheses remain scarce.

Method: A RAG-based agentic AI framework was built using Sonnet, GPT-4o, and Llama 3.1. A headache specialist created 13 questions (3 for prompt optimization, 10 for evaluation). Ten headache specialists each wrote one expert summary, yielding 4 summaries per question (expert + 3 LLMs). Blinded experts evaluated all summaries (excluding their own) on correctness, completeness, conciseness, and clinical utility (1-10 scale), ranked preferences, and judged if summaries were human- or AI-written.

Result: Expert-written summaries were preferred over LLM-generated ones, though experts sometimes found it challenging to distinguish between human- and AI-generated summaries.

Conclusion: Key expert-valued features beyond standard evaluation metrics were identified, which can guide future refinement of both human and AI literature summarization pipelines.

Abstract: Summarizing the latest medical literature to guide clinical decision-making is essential for evidence-based medicine and high-quality patient care. Yet clinicians face increasing challenges due to limited time with patients and a rapidly growing volume of published articles. Although retrieval-augmented large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in clinical summarization, human evaluations of their effectiveness in synthesizing broader scientific literature and direct comparisons to expert-written syntheses remain scarce. We constructed a RAG-based agentic AI framework using three state-of-the-art LLMs: Sonnet, GPT-4o, and Llama 3.1. A headache specialist created 13 questions, three for prompt optimization and ten for evaluation. Ten headache specialists across the United States and Canada each wrote a summary for one question, yielding four summaries per question (expert, Sonnet, GPT-4o, and Llama). The experts, blinded to authorship, critically evaluated the summaries, excluding the topic for which they wrote a summary, based on correctness, completeness, conciseness, and clinical utility, scoring each from 1 to 10 using standardized rubrics. They also ranked the summaries by preference and indicated whether they believed each summary was written by an expert or an LLM. Our study, comparing LLM- and expert-written literature summaries evaluated by headache specialists, showed that expert-written summaries were preferred, although experts sometimes found it challenging to distinguish between human- and AI-generated summaries. We also identified key expert-valued features beyond standard evaluation metrics that can guide future refinement of both human and AI literature summarization pipelines.

</details>


### [20] [Brick-Composer: Using MLLMs for Assembly with Diverse Bricks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05445)
*Jiateng Liu,Bingxuan Li,Zhenhailong Wang,Rushi Wang,Kaiwen Hong,Cheng Qian,Jiayu Liu,Denghui Zhang,Katherine Driggs-Campbell,Manling Li,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces BC-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating MLLMs on brick assembly, and proposes Brick-Composer, a learning framework using Human Design Sparks, World Feedback, and Synthetic Experience to improve MLLMs' assembly capabilities. Brick-Composer improves brick selection accuracy by 3x, reduces pose estimation errors, and raises step-level success from <1% to ~15%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) possess the visual grounding and spatial reasoning needed for constructing real-world objects from reusable building blocks (brick assembly), and to bridge the gap between current MLLMs and reliable assembly capabilities.

Method: Formulates brick assembly as sequential decision-making with two subtasks (brick selection and pose estimation). Introduces BC-Bench benchmark and Brick-Composer learning framework with three training signals: Human Design Sparks (affordance-rich demonstrations), World Feedback (visual/physical consequence grounding), and Synthetic Experience (scaling beyond existing designs).

Result: Current MLLMs struggle with fine-grained brick selection and precise pose estimation. Brick-Composer improves brick selection accuracy by over 3x, substantially reduces pose estimation errors, and raises step-level assembly success from <1% to ~15%.

Conclusion: MLLMs can acquire assembly capabilities through targeted, physically grounded learning. A Qwen-3-8B trained with Brick-Composer can correctly compose up to 42% of steps for a complete object, showing promise for AI agents that can read designs and construct real-world objects from reusable blocks.

Abstract: We dream of AI agents that can read arbitrary designs and construct real-world objects from reusable building blocks. As a first step toward this vision, we study whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) possess the visual grounding and spatial reasoning capabilities required for brick assembly. We formulate brick assembly as a sequential decision-making problem, where each step involves two subtasks: brick selection, identifying the target brick from candidate components, and brick pose estimation, predicting where and how the selected brick should be placed. To support this study, we introduce BC-Bench (Brick Construction Benchmark), the first benchmark for evaluating MLLMs on assembly with diverse bricks. Experiments show that current state-of-the-art MLLMs remain far from reliable builders, struggling with fine-grained brick selection and failing at precise pose estimation. To bridge this gap, we propose Brick-Composer, a learning framework that equips MLLMs with assembly skills through three complementary signals: Human Design Sparks, which provide affordance-rich construction demonstrations; World Feedback, which grounds predicted actions in visual and physical consequences; and Synthetic Experience, which scales learning beyond existing object designs. Brick-Composer improves brick selection accuracy by over three times, substantially reduces pose estimation errors, and raises strict step-level assembly success from less than 1% to around 15%. After training, a Qwen-3-8B can correctly compose up to 42% of the steps for a complete object, suggesting that MLLMs can acquire assembly capabilities through targeted, physically grounded learning.

</details>


### [21] [Insurance of Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05449)
*Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper examines the emerging insurance market for agentic AI systems, analyzing risk pathways (hallucinations, prompt-injection, autonomous decision errors, model drift, etc.), evaluating existing product adaptations, and proposing an actuarial framework and coordinated insurance architecture combining cyber, tech E&O, product liability, performance-warranty, and affirmative AI-liability coverages via explicit allocation mechanisms and dedicated AI aggregates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems extend beyond information generation to autonomous planning, tool invocation, and environment modification, creating novel exposures that traditional insurance categories (cyber, professional liability, product liability, D&O) cannot adequately address.

Method: Conceptual framework development characterizing agentic AI as a continuum of autonomy and delegated authority; analysis of major risk pathways; evaluation of existing insurance product adaptations; proposal of an actuarial framework based on exposure assessment, scenario analysis, dependency mapping, and accumulation-risk management; design of a coordinated insurance architecture with allocation mechanisms and dedicated AI aggregates.

Result: The paper identifies key risk pathways unique to agentic AI and proposes a layered insurance ecosystem rather than a single monoline product, supported by improved governance, transparency, telemetry, and regulatory clarity.

Conclusion: The future of agentic-AI insurance lies in a layered ecosystem of complementary coverages (cyber, tech E&O, product liability, performance-warranty, affirmative AI-liability) integrated through explicit allocation mechanisms and dedicated AI aggregates, rather than a single monoline product.

Abstract: Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) systems are transforming the risk landscape by extending beyond information generation to autonomous planning, tool invocation, decision execution, and persistent modification of digital and physical environments. These capabilities introduce novel exposures that do not fit neatly within traditional insurance categories such as cyber, professional liability, product liability, or directors and officers coverage. This paper examines the emerging insurance market for agentic AI and develops a framework for understanding its underwriting, pricing, reinsurance, and product-design implications. We characterize agentic AI as a continuum of autonomy and delegated authority, emphasizing the distinction between informational outputs and systems capable of independently generating insured events through external actions. We analyze major risk pathways, including hallucinations, prompt-injection attacks, autonomous decision errors, model drift, dependency failures, and cyber-physical harms, and evaluate how existing insurance products are adapting to address these exposures. The paper further proposes an actuarial framework based on exposure assessment, scenario analysis, dependency mapping, and accumulation-risk management, drawing parallels to the evolution of cyber insurance. Finally, we present a coordinated insurance architecture that integrates cyber, technology errors and omissions, product liability, performance-warranty, and affirmative AI-liability coverages through explicit allocation mechanisms and dedicated AI aggregates. The analysis suggests that the future of agentic-AI insurance lies not in a single monoline product but in a layered ecosystem of complementary coverages supported by improved governance, transparency, telemetry, and regulatory clarity.

</details>


### [22] [Output Type Before Quality: A Standards-Derived XAI Admissibility Rubric for Autonomous-Driving Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05461)
*Abhinaw Priyadershi,Mandar Pitale,Jelena Frtunikj,Maria Spence*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: There is a mismatch (evidence-type gap) between what safety standards require for ADS assurance (directed cause-and-effect chains) and what popular XAI methods like SHAP provide (e.g., ranked feature lists). From four safety standards, the authors derive 19 testable criteria and evaluate six XAI method classes. Causal XAI is structurally necessary for three lifecycle stages (hazard identification, incident investigation, data management) with large rubric gaps, while correlational/language methods suffice for others. A single-VLA proof-of-concept on ~80K real-world driving clips supports the rubric predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety standards for ML-based autonomous driving mandate specific evidence types (directed cause-and-effect chains, quantified interventional effects, named root-cause variables), but the XAI literature is organized by output type/technique families, creating a structural mismatch. This evidence-type gap means methods like SHAP—despite being most recommended for ADS—cannot produce the required evidence, potentially leading to non-compliant assurance cases.

Method: The authors derive 19 testable evidentiary criteria from four safety standards (AMLAS, ISO 26262, ISO 21448, ISO/PAS 8800) across 7 lifecycle stages with representative clause-cited derivations. They then structurally score six XAI method classes against these criteria, and validate with a single-VLA proof-of-concept on 1,996 real-world driving clips (79,840 rows, ten splits).

Result: Causal XAI is structurally required for hazard identification (+62% rubric gap), incident investigation (+50%), and data management (+50%). The verdict is stable across thresholds T in (0%, 50%] and survives worst-case single-cell flip down to T=25%. For the remaining four stages, correlational or language-based methods are comparable or sufficient. The proof-of-concept is consistent with each method's observed output type matching its rubric prediction.

Conclusion: XAI method selection for ADS safety assurance should be driven by lifecycle-stage evidence demand rather than method popularity. Structural admissibility via the rubric is necessary but not sufficient for compliance—the open challenge is validating output fidelity. Causal XAI is not universally required but is structurally necessary at three key stages.

Abstract: Safety standards for ML-based autonomous driving specify the kind of evidence an assurance case must contain (directed cause-and-effect chains, quantified interventional effects, named root-cause variables), yet the XAI literature is organised by output type and technique family (saliency maps, feature attribution, counterfactuals, causal graphs, language traces). SHAP, the most-recommended ADS XAI method, returns a ranked feature list that no implementation effort can convert into a directed chain (Fig.1). We name this mismatch the evidence-type gap.
  From AMLAS, ISO 26262, ISO21448, ISO/PAS 8800 we derive 19 testable evidentiary criteria across 7 lifecycle stages with representative clause-cited derivations and score six XAI method classes structurally.
  Causal XAI emerges as structurally required to satisfy the derived criteria at three stages: hazard identification (+62% rubric gap), incident investigation (+50%), and data management (+50%); the verdict set is stable across thresholds T in (0%, 50%]$ and survives a worst-case single-cell flip down to T = 25%. At the remaining four stages, correlational or language-based methods are comparable or sufficient. The rubric identifies structural admissibility (necessary but not sufficient for compliance): an admissible method's specific output content may still be wrong, and validating that fidelity (the edges a fitted SCM produces, the cause a trace names) is the open assurance challenge. A single-VLA proof of concept on 1,996 real-world driving clips (79,840 rows, ten splits) is consistent with each method's observed output type matching its rubric prediction. XAI method selection for ADS safety assurance should be driven by lifecycle-stage evidence demand, not by method popularity.

</details>


### [23] [PSEBench: A Controllable and Verifiable Benchmark for Evaluating LLMs in Patient Safety Event Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05463)
*Keqi Han,Ryan Young,Annabel Strauss,Lindsey Hughes,Katharine M. Nesbitt,Nicole Schueler,Che Ngufor,Carl Yang,Yuan Xue,Zhijun Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PSEBench is a 5,074-case benchmark for evaluating LLMs on patient safety event triage, built via a policy-grounded methodology using clause cards to factor regulatory text into auditable decision specs, enabling evidence-grounded reasoning, proactive information seeking, and principled abstention evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Patient safety event triage is high-stakes and manually performed; LLMs may help but lack reliable evaluation due to no benchmarks supporting evidence-grounded policy reasoning, proactive information seeking, and principled abstention in ambiguous cases.

Method: A scalable pipeline using clause cards (structured representations factorizing regulatory text), anchor-driven instantiation, and closed-loop verification to produce narratives with by-construction ground truth, supporting missing information and uncertain variants.

Result: PSEBench contains 5,074 cases based on Minnesota's 29 Reportable Adverse Health Events; evaluation on 15 LLMs shows consistent capability trends and identifies actionable gaps.

Conclusion: The benchmark demonstrates utility for evaluating LLM-based patient safety event triage and reveals gaps that need addressing for reliable deployment.

Abstract: Patient safety event triage, determining whether a clinical event is reportable under jurisdiction-specific policy, is a high-stakes task typically performed manually by patient safety experts. Although LLMs may support this workflow, reliable evaluation is limited by the lack of benchmarks to capture evidence-grounded policy reasoning, proactive information seeking for incomplete reports, and principled abstention in irreducibly ambiguous cases. We address this gap with a policy-grounded construction methodology centered on the clause card, a structured representation that factorizes regulatory text into auditable decision specifications. Combining clause cards with anchor-driven instantiation and closed-loop verification, our scalable pipeline produces narratives with by-construction ground truth and naturally supports generating missing information and uncertain variants. We instantiate this method on Minnesota's 29 Reportable Adverse Health Events, producing PSEBench, a 5,074-case benchmark with an agentic evaluation environment. Evaluation on 15 representative LLMs reveals consistent capability trends, demonstrates the benchmark's utility, and identifies actionable gaps toward reliable LLM-based patient safety event triage.

</details>


### [24] [Step-by-Step Optimization-like Reasoning in LLMs over Expanding Search Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05464)
*Nicolás Astorga,Nabeel Seedat,Mihaela van der Schaar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OPT* introduces scalable optimization-style tasks for training LLMs in step-by-step optimization reasoning, using solver-guided online policy optimization and search-based offline RL, showing improved reasoning on complex search spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing verifiable reward training (e.g., math, coding) captures only part of step-by-step decision making; many real-world tasks require finding high-value feasible plans among many valid alternatives, which current LLM training paradigms do not adequately address.

Method: OPT* defines tasks with a feasibility checker and evaluator, scaled by a complexity parameter. Two training regimes: (i) solver-guided online policy optimization using a solver as a value oracle with rank-based reward shaping; (ii) search-based offline RL when solvers are unavailable. Theoretical framing relates success to information extracted per unit of search budget.

Result: Training on OPT* improves step-by-step optimization-like reasoning; ablations identify key ingredients for efficient search on OPT* tasks.

Conclusion: OPT* provides a scalable benchmark for optimization reasoning in LLMs, and the proposed training methods effectively transfer to improved performance on optimization-like step-by-step reasoning.

Abstract: Verifiable reward training has improved mathematical and coding reasoning, but these domains capture only part of step-by-step decision making. Many real-world tasks require finding a high-value feasible plan among many valid alternatives. We introduce OPT*, a scalable family of optimization-style tasks for training and evaluating LLM step-by-step optimization-like reasoning along a complexity axis: each task provides a feasibility checker and evaluator, while a complexity parameter expands the search space without requiring new human labels. This motivates studying these tasks in two regimes: (i) solver-guided online policy optimization, which uses a solver as a value oracle for partial states and applies rank-based reward shaping to reinforce better next steps, and (ii) search-based offline RL when such solvers are unavailable. Theoretically, we relate success in large search spaces to the information a reasoner extracts per unit of search budget. Empirically, we ablate the ingredients that make search efficient on OPT* and show that training on OPT* improves step-by-step optimization-like reasoning.

</details>


### [25] [Severity-Aware Curriculum Learning with Multi-Model Response Selection for Medical Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05510)
*Ahmed Alansary,Molham Mohamed,Ali Hamdi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A severity-aware multi-model framework combining curriculum training (mild→moderate→critical) with multi-model response selection improves medical QA performance, achieving 86.71% (baseline) and 90.30% (fine-tuned) BERTScore on the MAQA dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telehealth systems need accessible medical information delivery, but existing LLMs struggle to provide consistent and contextually appropriate responses across varying levels of case severity, motivating the need for models that can adapt to progressive complexity in medical queries.
