Method: TAPO (Tool-Aware Policy Optimization) exploits parameter-determinism of information-acquisition tools—similar call parameters define equivalent actions and should share comparable credit. It constructs counterfactual witnesses within the current batch and applies confidence-gated conservative advantage correction to compensate misassigned negative credit.

Result: Over half of failing trajectories and tool-use actions exhibit correctable misassignment. TAPO delivers consistent, plug-and-play improvements over GRPO, GSPO, and SAPO across multiple multimodal search benchmarks with negligible computational overhead.

Conclusion: Credit misassignment is a structurally exploitable failure mode; TAPO corrects it efficiently without extra annotation, models, or sampling, establishing a new strong baseline for RL-based tool-augmented multimodal search.

Abstract: We identify and formally characterize credit misassignment as a systematic failure mode of GRPO in tool-augmented multimodal search agents: its uniform broadcast of trajectory-level advantages to all tokens causes valuable tool-use steps in failing trajectories to be penalized no differently from valueless ones. We further empirically quantify the scale of this phenomenon. Over half of failing trajectories and failing tool-use actions exhibit correctable credit misassignment, demonstrating that the wasted training signal is both substantial and structurally exploitable. Building on this insight, we propose Tool-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), which exploits the parameter-determinism property of information-acquisition tools: similar call parameters define equivalent information-acquisition actions and should therefore share comparable action credit. TAPO constructs counterfactual witnesses within the current training batch and compensates misassigned negative credit via confidence-gated conservative advantage correction. It requires no additional annotation, models, or sampling, and introduces negligible computational overhead. Across multiple multimodal search benchmarks, TAPO delivers consistent, plug-and-play improvements over strong baselines for three mainstream RL algorithms (GRPO, GSPO, and SAPO). Our code and models will be publicly released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [53] [Can LLMs Write Correct TLA+ Specifications? Evaluating Natural-Language-to-TLA+ Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05792)
*Arslan Bisharat,Brian Ortiz,Eric Spencer,Khushboo Bhadauria,TaiNing Wang,George K. Thiruvathukal,Konstantin Laufer,Mohammed Abuhamad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First systematic evaluation of 30 LLMs on TLA+ specification synthesis from natural language, showing up to 26.6% syntactic but only 8.6% semantic correctness; model size does not predict performance, code-specialized models underperform, and five hallucination categories are identified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TLA+ adoption is limited because writing correct specifications from natural language requires significant time and expertise; LLMs show promise but no prior study systematically evaluates their semantic correctness in generating TLA+ specifications.

Method: Evaluated 30 LLMs across eight families on a curated dataset of 205 TLA+ specifications: 25 open-weight models under four prompting strategies (2,600 runs) and 5 proprietary models under few-shot prompting (130 runs), validated by SANY parser and TLC model checker.

Result: LLMs achieved up to 26.6% syntactic correctness but only 8.6% semantic correctness, with successes only under progressive prompting. Model size does not predict quality (e.g., DeepSeek r1:8b outperforms its 70B variant). Code-specialized models underperform due to negative transfer. Five recurring hallucination categories were identified.

Conclusion: Current LLMs do not generate reliable TLA+ specifications without expert oversight; the evaluation framework, code, and dataset are released for reproducibility and future research.

Abstract: TLA+ has supported industrial verification at companies such as Amazon and Microsoft, yet writing correct TLA+ specifications from natural language still requires time and expertise, which limits adoption. LLMs show promise, but no prior study measures whether they produce semantically correct TLA+ specifications from natural language. This paper presents the first systematic evaluation of LLM-based TLA+ specification synthesis from natural language. Our study evaluates 30 LLMs across eight families on a curated dataset of 205 TLA+ specifications: 25 open-weight models across four prompting strategies (2,600 runs) and 5 proprietary models under few-shot prompting (130 runs), all validated by the SANY parser and TLC model checker. LLMs achieve up to 26.6% syntactic correctness but only 8.6% semantic correctness, with successes exclusive to progressive prompting. Results show that model size does not predict quality, e.g., DeepSeek r1:8b outperforms its 70B variant across all strategies, which suggests the importance of reasoning alignment for formal languages. Code-specialized models consistently underperform due to negative transfer from mainstream language training. We identify five recurring hallucination categories, all traceable to specific training data biases. These results suggest that current LLMs do not generate reliable TLA+ specifications without expert oversight. We release the evaluation framework, code, and dataset to support reproducibility and future research.

</details>


### [54] [From Risk Classification to Action Plan Remediation: A Guardrail Feedback Driven Framework for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05805)
*Yuhao Sun,Jiacheng Zhang,Shaanan Cohney,Zhexin Zhang,Feng Liu,Xingliang Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRIAD is a guardrail-integrated agent framework that uses three-way decisions (proceed, refuse, update) with structured verbal feedback to preserve benign task components while blocking harmful ones, reducing attack success rate to 10.42% with the best safety-utility trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM guardrails flag entire tasks as unsafe when only portions are contaminated by untrusted content, sacrificing benign parts; also, guardrails are evaluated in isolation without measuring downstream agent behavior safety.

Method: TRIAD employs a finetuned language model that outputs one of three decisions (proceed, refuse, update) with structured natural-language feedback; the "update" decision guides the agent to revise its plan, remove harmful components, and preserve benign parts; guardrail feedback is injected into agent context forming a closed loop with iterative planning.

Result: On ASB and AgentHarm benchmarks, TRIAD achieves 10.42% average attack success rate and the best safety-utility trade-off among guardrail-integrated baselines.

Conclusion: TRIAD demonstrates that structured guardrail feedback enabling plan revision (rather than binary allow/deny) better handles contaminated tasks by preserving benign utility while maintaining strong safety.

Abstract: LLM-based guardrails typically safeguard agents by evaluating proposed actions or inputs before execution, producing safety signals such as binary allow/deny decisions, risk categories, and/or explanatory rationales about potential policy violations. However, agent risks often arise when otherwise benign tasks are contaminated by untrusted external content, unsafe instructions, or risky tool use. Existing guardrails often flag the entire task uniformly as unsafe, thereby blocking the threat but sacrificing the benign part. Moreover, existing work largely evaluates guardrails in isolation, leaving unclear whether their interventions lead to safer downstream agent behavior. To address this, we introduce TRIAD (Tripartite Response for Iterative Agent Guardrailing), a guardrail-integrated agent framework that leverages guardrail-generated verbal feedback as a guiding signal to keep the agent aligned with benign objectives at each planning step. We finetune a language model on a self-curated training dataset to output one of three decisions: proceed, refuse, or update, together with structured natural-language feedback. Rather than merely allowing or blocking execution, update guides the agent to revise its plan, avoid harmful components, and preserve the benign task where possible. TRIAD injects this feedback into the agent's context, enabling subsequent plan revision and forming a closed loop between guardrail feedback and agent planning. Extensive experiments on ASB and AgentHarm show that TRIAD reduces the average attack success rate to 10.42%, while achieving the best safety-utility trade-off among guardrail-integrated baselines. Our code is available at: https://github.com/YUHAOSUNABC/TRIAD.

</details>


### [55] [When Tools Fail: Benchmarking Dynamic Replanning and Anomaly Recovery in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05806)
*Dongsheng Zhu,Xuchen Ma,Yucheng Shen,Xiang Li,Yukun Zhao,Shuaiqiang Wang,Lingyong Yan,Dawei Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolMaze benchmarks LLMs on Tool-Integrated Reasoning under realistic tool failures, revealing that implicit semantic perturbations cause the largest performance drops and that dynamic replanning improves much slower than basic task execution with model scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TIR benchmarks only evaluate on idealized "happy paths" and ignore real-world tool failures; the authors aim to systematically study how LLMs handle dynamic path discovery and error recovery when tools malfunction.

Method: Two-dimensional benchmark design: (1) DAG-based topological complexity, and (2) a 2×2 taxonomy of tool perturbations (explicit/implicit × transient/permanent). Uses Perturbation Recovery Rate (PRR) as a key metric.

Result: Implicit semantic failures cause the sharpest performance drops; PRR plummets ~37% in these scenarios. Complex topologies trap agents in trial-and-error loops. Agentic fault-tolerance scales 3.66× slower than basic task execution with model size increase.

Conclusion: Dynamic replanning is a distinct bottleneck from basic task execution that is not addressed by scaling model size or prompting alone.

Abstract: Existing benchmarks evaluate Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR) in LLMs on idealized ''happy paths'', largely overlooking real-world tool failures. We introduce ToolMaze, a benchmark for dynamic path discovery and error recovery in TIR agents. To separate systematic replanning from blind trial-and-error, ToolMaze adopts a two-dimensional design: DAG-based topological complexity and a $2 \times 2$ taxonomy of tool perturbations (explicit/implicit, transient/permanent). Evaluations show that perturbations degrade performance across nearly all models, with the sharpest drops under implicit semantic failures. Driven by systemic over-trust in corrupted outputs, Perturbation Recovery Rate (PRR) plummets by around 37\% in these scenarios, while complex topologies trap agents in futile trial-and-error loops. Crucially, agentic fault-tolerance improves with model scale $3.66\times$ slower than basic task execution, highlighting dynamic replanning as a distinct bottleneck unaddressed by model scaling or prompting. Data and code are available at https://github.com/Zhudongsheng75/ToolMaze.

</details>


### [56] [Statistical Priors for Implicit Preferences: Decoupling Skill Selection as a Local Harness in Personal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05828)
*Zeyu Gan,Huayi Tang,Yong Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel decoupled architecture for lightweight local preference learning in personal agents that separates statistical preference learning from semantic intent parsing, using localized statistical results to modulate LLM skill selection decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-powered personal agents face rapid skill expansion, learning implicit user preferences becomes critical, but local deployment constraints preclude complex centralized selection algorithms, creating an urgent need for a lightweight local preference harness.

Method: Proposes a decoupled architecture that separates statistical preference learning (local) from semantic intent parsing (remote LLM), where localized statistical results influence and modulate the remote LLM's skill selection decisions.

Result: Achieves lowest cumulative regret and highest test accuracy, significantly outperforming traditional memory-augmented agents.

Conclusion: The decoupled approach effectively addresses the lightweight local preference learning challenge under local deployment constraints, offering a viable solution for personal agents.

Abstract: As Large Language Model (LLM) capabilities advance, locally deployed personal agents relying on API-based remote models and external skills have emerged as a novel paradigm. With the rapid expansion of available skills, enabling personal agents to learn and adapt to implicit user preferences becomes a critical challenge. However, local deployment constraints preclude complex centralized selection algorithms, creating an urgent need for a lightweight local preference harness. This paper explores the implementation of such a harness through a novel architecture that strictly decouples statistical preference learning from semantic intent parsing. Specifically, we leverage localized statistical results to influence and modulate the selection decisions of the remote LLM. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our decoupled approach achieves the lowest cumulative regret and highest test accuracy, significantly outperforming traditional memory-augmented agents.

</details>


### [57] [Agentic Molecular Recovery via Molecule-Aware Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05847)
*Suwan Yoon,Changhee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AMREC proposes a shift from validity repair to identity-preserving molecular recovery, using molecule-aware mismatch tracking, expanded candidate exploration, and trajectory-level selection to recover molecular identity from invalid SMILES generated by LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Invalid SMILES generated by LLMs for text-guided molecular generation often lose key structural cues when corrected. Existing methods (post-hoc repair, LLM-only correction, generic agentic correction) either distort structures, cause global drift, or rely on greedy single-candidate trajectories. The paper argues for identity-preserving molecular recovery rather than mere validity restoration.

Method: AMREC couples molecule-aware mismatch tracking with expanded candidate exploration and trajectory-level selection.

Result: On invalid ChEBI-20 drafts from three backbone models, AMREC achieves the strongest overall recovery profile across structural, exact-match, and string-level metrics.

Conclusion: Shifting from validity-oriented repair to identity-preserving recovery better addresses invalid SMILES in text-guided molecular generation, and AMREC outperforms existing correction strategies.

Abstract: Text-guided molecular generation with LLMs often yields invalid SMILES. We argue that invalid drafts should be addressed through a shift from validity-oriented repair to identity-preserving molecular recovery: the objective is not only to restore chemical validity, but also to preserve target-relevant structural cues and recover the molecular identity implied by the description. This perspective reveals the limitations of existing correction strategies. Post-hoc repair can recover validity while distorting key structures, LLM-only correction can introduce unintended global drift, and generic agentic correction remains constrained by greedy single-candidate trajectories even when equipped with executable RDKit edit tools. To address these limitations, we propose AMREC, which couples molecule-aware mismatch tracking with expanded candidate exploration and trajectory-level selection. On invalid ChEBI-20 drafts from three backbone models, AMREC achieves the strongest overall recovery profile across structural, exact-match, and string-level metrics.

</details>


### [58] [Entropy-Based Evaluation of AI Agents: A Lightweight Framework for Measuring Behavioral Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05872)
*Olasimbo Ayodeji Arigbabu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes EEA, a lightweight entropy-based framework to evaluate AI agent behavior beyond traditional metrics like task success and latency, introducing six entropy metrics to measure decision process structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evaluation metrics (task success, reward, latency, cost) miss important behavioral aspects such as excessive exploration, rigid repetition, tool usage effectiveness, uncertainty reduction, and robustness across runs.

Method: Introduces six entropy-based metrics: action entropy, trajectory entropy, tool entropy, information gain, exploration efficiency, and robustness entropy; includes a Python implementation compatible with LangChain, Google ADK, custom agent loops, and observability traces.

Result: Presents a lightweight, complementary evaluation framework that quantifies agent decision process structure without replacing traditional metrics.

Conclusion: EEA provides additional behavioral insights into AI agents by analyzing entropy in decision processes, with practical integration support for existing agent frameworks.

Abstract: AI agents are commonly evaluated using task success, reward, latency, and cost. These metrics are useful, but they often miss important aspects of agent behavior: whether an agent explores too much, repeats itself too rigidly, uses tools effectively, reduces uncertainty over time, or remains robust across repeated runs. This paper proposes Entropy-Based Evaluation of AI Agents (EEA), a lightweight framework for measuring agent behavior through entropy. Rather than treating intelligence as only final task completion, EEA studies the structure of the agents decision process. The framework introduces action entropy, trajectory entropy, tool entropy, information gain, exploration efficiency, and robustness entropy. These metrics are intended to complement, not replace, traditional evaluation methods. We also present a practical Python implementation designed to integrate with agent frameworks such as LangChain, Google ADK, custom agent loops, and stored observability traces.

</details>


### [59] [QCFuse: Query-Aware Cache Fusion via Compressed View for Efficient RAG Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05875)
*Jianxin Yan,Wangze Ni,Zhenxin Li,Jiabao Jin,Zhitao Shen,Haoyang Li,Jia Zhu,Peng Cheng,Xuemin Lin,Lei Chen,Kui Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QCFuse is a compressed-view query-aware selector for RAG cache fusion that uses chunk-anchor query probing and critical-layer profiling to achieve full-prefill-level quality while reducing prefill time, with average speedups of 1.7x over full prefill and 1.5x over ProphetKV across four LLMs and six datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG improves LLM answer quality by grounding generation in external evidence, but processing retrieved contexts makes the prefill stage a dominant serving cost. Existing selectors for RAG cache fusion face a dilemma between quality and efficiency: fast selectors are query-agnostic and miss relevant evidence, while full-view query-aware selectors require broad context and stall the layer-wise cache-fusion pipeline.

Method: QCFuse introduces two key techniques: (1) chunk-anchor query probing, which conditions user-query states on compact per-chunk anchors; and (2) critical-layer profiling, which identifies recomputation tokens without requiring all-layer inspection. The method is implemented in SGLang.

Result: Evaluated on four open-weight LLMs across six datasets, QCFuse reaches full-prefill-level quality. At matched quality, it achieves an average prefill-time speedup of 1.7x over full prefill and 1.5x over ProphetKV, the strongest quality-preserving baseline.

Conclusion: QCFuse resolves the quality-efficiency dilemma in RAG cache fusion by using a compressed-view query-aware selection approach, achieving significant speedups while maintaining full-prefill-level quality.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves large language model (LLM) answer quality by grounding generation in external evidence, but processing retrieved contexts makes the prefill stage a dominant serving cost. RAG cache fusion reduces this cost by reusing precomputed key-value (KV) caches for retrieved chunks and selectively recomputing tokens under the current prompt. Existing selectors, however, face a dilemma between quality and efficiency: fast query-agnostic or final-layer query-to-context selectors can miss request-relevant evidence, whereas full-view query-aware selectors require broad context and layer visibility before recomputation and therefore stall the layer-wise cache-fusion pipeline. We present QCFuse, a compressed-view query-aware selector for RAG cache fusion. QCFuse uses chunk-anchor query probing to condition user-query states on compact per-chunk anchors and critical-layer profiling to identify recomputation tokens without all-layer inspection. We implement QCFuse in SGLang and evaluate it on four open-weight LLMs across six datasets. QCFuse reaches full-prefill-level quality. At matched quality, QCFuse achieves an average prefill-time speedup of 1.7x over full prefill and 1.5x over ProphetKV, the strongest quality-preserving baseline.

</details>


### [60] [Retry Policy Gradients in Continuous Action Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05888)
*Soichiro Nishimori,Paavo Parmas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper extends ReMax retry-based objectives to continuous action spaces using pathwise derivative estimators, showing that ReMax encourages stochastic exploration by reshaping policy gradients (biasing toward higher entropy and damping convergence). The authors introduce ReMAC (ReMax Actor-Critic), an off-policy algorithm that achieves performance comparable to SAC without explicit entropy regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retry-based objectives (pass@K, max@K) can promote exploration without explicit bonuses in discrete action spaces via ReMax. The authors aim to extend this capability to continuous action spaces, which requires addressing the challenge of estimating pathwise derivatives for retry objectives in continuous domains.

Method: The authors introduce pathwise derivative estimators for retry objectives to extend ReMax to continuous action spaces. They theoretically analyze the learning dynamics, showing ReMax alters gradient direction (toward higher entropy) and magnitude (damping convergence). They show Adam's normalization can mitigate damping depending on its stabilization parameter. They instantiate this as ReMAC, an off-policy actor-critic algorithm.

Result: ReMax encourages stochastic exploration by reshaping the policy-gradient landscape: biasing updates toward higher policy entropy and damping gradients (slowing convergence). Adam's adaptive normalization can mitigate this damping. Empirically, ReMAC promotes higher policy entropy without entropy regularization and achieves performance comparable to SAC.

Conclusion: Retry-based objectives with pathwise derivative estimators can effectively extend ReMax to continuous action spaces, promoting exploration and stochasticity without explicit entropy bonuses, matching SAC's performance.

Abstract: Retry-based objectives such as pass@K and max@K optimize the best return obtained from multiple sampled trajectories, and recent work has shown that they can promote exploration without explicit exploration bonuses. In discrete action spaces, ReMax was shown to do so by adapting to return uncertainty. In this work, we introduce pathwise derivative estimators for retry objectives and use them to extend ReMax to continuous action spaces. We study the resulting learning dynamics and show that, even with deterministic rewards, ReMax can encourage stochastic exploration by reshaping the policy-gradient landscape. In particular, it alters gradients both in direction, biasing updates toward higher policy entropy, and in magnitude, damping gradients and slowing convergence. We further show that Adam's adaptive normalization can mitigate this damping, depending on its numerical stabilization parameter. Empirically, we instantiate this objective as ReMax Actor-Critic (ReMAC), an off-policy actor--critic algorithm that optimizes the ReMax objective using a pathwise derivative estimator. Our experiments show that ReMAC can promote higher policy entropy without entropy regularization and achieves performance comparable to SAC.

</details>


### [61] [Retrospective Harness Optimization: Improving LLM Agents via Self-Preference over Trajectory Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05922)
*Wenbo Pan,Shujie Liu,Chin-Yew Lin,Jingying Zeng,Xianfeng Tang,Xiangyang Zhou,Yan Lu,Xiaohua Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RHO is a self-supervised method that optimizes AI agent harnesses using only past trajectories, improving SWE-Bench Pro pass rates from 59% to 78% without external grading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent harness optimization methods require ground-truth validation sets, which are difficult to acquire in practical deployment settings. The authors aim to enable self-supervised optimization without labeled data.

Method: RHO selects a diverse coreset of challenging tasks from past trajectories, re-solves them in parallel, analyzes rollouts via self-validation and self-consistency, generates candidate harness updates, and selects the best update using pairwise self-preference.

Result: Single optimization round improves SWE-Bench Pro pass rate from 59% to 78% without external grading. RHO effectively targets prior failure modes, alters agent behavior patterns, and sustains higher accuracy during long-horizon sessions across software engineering, technical work, and knowledge work domains.

Conclusion: RHO demonstrates that agent harnesses can be effectively optimized in a self-supervised manner using only historical trajectories, eliminating the need for ground-truth validation sets while achieving substantial performance gains.

Abstract: AI agents rely on a harness of skills, tools, and workflows to solve complex problems. Continually improving this harness is essential for adapting to new tasks. However, existing optimization methods typically require ground-truth validation sets, yet such labeled data is difficult to acquire in practical deployment settings. To address this problem, we introduce Retrospective Harness Optimization (RHO), a self-supervised method that optimizes the agent harness using only past trajectories. Specifically, RHO selects a diverse coreset of challenging tasks from past trajectories and re-solves them in parallel. The agent analyzes these rollouts using self-validation and self-consistency, then generates candidate harness updates and selects the most effective one by its own pairwise self-preference. We evaluate RHO across three diverse domains, spanning software engineering, technical work, and knowledge work. Notably, a single optimization round improves the pass rate on SWE-Bench Pro from 59% to 78% without any external grading. Furthermore, our analysis demonstrates that RHO effectively targets prior failure modes. As a result, the optimized harness alters the agent's behavior patterns and sustains higher accuracy during long-horizon sessions.

</details>


### [62] [Towards World Models in Biomedical Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05925)
*Guangyu Wang,Jingkun Yue,Siqi Zhang,Yu Liu,Xiaoyu Wang,Mingyuan Meng,Changwei Ji,Zongbo Han,Yulin Wang,Yang Yue,Frank Fu,Ting Chen,Song Wu,Ziwei Liu,Jiangning Song,Ming Li,Gao Huang,Xiaohong Liu,Athanasios Vasilakos,Xingcai Zhang,Ping Zhang,Yong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes biomedical world models as a new AI paradigm that learns latent representations of biological states and intervention-conditioned dynamics to simulate future biological trajectories, serving as data engines, environment simulators, and planning substrates for applications like virtual cells, organoids, virtual patients, and surgical simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current biomedical AI systems focus on static pattern recognition rather than prospective simulation of biological futures, limiting their ability to predict, understand, and control dynamic biological responses to perturbations, disease progression, and therapeutic interventions.

Method: Biomedical world models learn latent representations of molecular, cellular, tissue, and clinical states along with intervention-conditioned dynamics, enabling simulation of future trajectories before actions are taken, thereby creating a paradigm for AI-driven discovery.

Result: The framework positions biomedical world models as data engines, environment simulators, and scientific planning substrates applicable across virtual cells, organoids, virtual patients, and surgical simulation, while outlining required data infrastructure, evaluation benchmarks, safety constraints, and governance.

Conclusion: Biomedical world models could provide a foundation for simulation-guided, closed-loop, and experimentally actionable biomedical discovery, enabling prospective simulation rather than retrospective pattern recognition.

Abstract: A central goal of biomedicine is to understand, predict and ultimately control the dynamic mechanisms by which biological systems respond to perturbations, disease progression and therapeutic intervention. Although foundation models and large language models have accelerated biomedical data interpretation, most current systems remain focused on static pattern recognition rather than prospective simulation of biological futures. Here we propose biomedical world models as a paradigm for AI-driven discovery. These models learn latent representations of molecular, cellular, tissue and clinical states, together with intervention-conditioned dynamics that allow future trajectories to be simulated before actions are taken. We discuss how biomedical world models could function as data engines, environment simulators and scientific planning substrates across applications including virtual cells, organoids, virtual patients and surgical simulation. We outline the data infrastructure, evaluation benchmarks, safety constraints and governance frameworks required. Biomedical world models may provide a foundation for simulation-guided, closed-loop and experimentally actionable biomedical discovery.

</details>


### [63] [A Pre-Registered Causal Partition of Self-Consistency Elicitation and Reward Design in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05932)
*Yuze Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RLVR with spurious rewards conflates self-consistency elicitation with genuine reward-design signal. The paper derives a telescoping decomposition of the naive estimator, measures each term across prior strengths, and validates via factorial experiment and re-audits of published results, finding that reported gains can be dominated by elicitation rather than reward design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners interpret the naive estimator naively = acc(TRUE) - acc(RANDOM) as the reward-design effect in RLVR. The authors identify that this estimand is systematically biased because it conflates self-consistency elicitation (sharpening the policy toward its modal answer via majority pseudo-reward) with genuine reward-design signal. The paper aims to diagnose and decompose this bias.

Method: Controlled tabular-GRPO simulator to derive exact telescoping decomposition: total = null + elicit + rd. Measured each term across five prior-strength levels. Pre-registered 2x2x2 factorial experiment to confirm non-additivity. Points-vs-bounds pilot gate for identification regimes. Re-audits of two published results.

Result: Reward-design fraction of naive estimator ranges from 0.139 (weak prior, ps=0.20) to 0.05 (strong prior, ps=0.80). Elicitation term flips sign at self-consistency crossover. Factorial confirms non-additivity (interaction ratio 0.385; AxC effect -0.089). Re-audits: one published result is ELICITATION DOMINATED (share 0.98), the other is REWARD DESIGN DOMINATED (rd share 1.18).

Conclusion: The naive estimator is not a valid measure of reward-design effect. The decomposition provides diagnostic value for auditing RLVR results. Strong-prior regimes are point-identified; near-crossover regimes are only bounded. Pre-registered commitment to submit regardless of outcome. Reusable one-command harness released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves reasoning even when the reward signal is spurious -- assigning credit to the group-plurality answer rather than a ground-truth verifier. Practitioners commonly interpret naive = acc(TRUE) - acc(RANDOM) as the reward-design effect. We prove this estimand is systematically biased: it conflates self-consistency elicitation (sharpening the policy toward its modal answer via majority pseudo-reward) with genuine reward-design signal. Using a controlled tabular-GRPO simulator we derive an exact telescoping decomposition total = null + elicit + rd and measure each term across five prior-strength levels. The reward-design fraction of the naive estimator ranges from 0.139 at weak prior (ps=0.20) to 0.05 at strong prior (ps=0.80), with the elicitation term flipping sign at the self-consistency crossover. A pre-registered 2x2x2 factorial confirms non-additivity (interaction ratio 0.385; AxC effect -0.089). A points-vs-bounds pilot gate shows strong-prior regimes are point-identified while near-crossover regimes are only bounded. Re-audits of two named published results yield ELICITATION DOMINATED (elicitation share 0.98) and REWARD DESIGN DOMINATED (rd share 1.18) verdicts respectively, demonstrating the diagnostic value of the partition. We pre-commit to submit regardless of flip outcome; a non-flip is a finding of equal standing. We release a reusable one-command harness for any alignment paper to run the same audit.

</details>


### [64] [Edit-R2: Context-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Turn Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05950)
*Yuxiao Ye,Haoran He,Fangyuan Kong,Xintao Wang,Pengfei Wan,Kun Gai,Ling Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edit-R2 is a reinforcement learning post-training framework for unified multimodal models that addresses multi-turn in-context image editing by reconstructing operative session intent and using multi-turn RL with trajectory filtering. MICE-Bench is introduced as a large-scale benchmark with automated metrics. Edit-R2 significantly improves performance over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-guided image editing methods are confined to single-turn settings and fail in multi-turn in-context editing due to two coupled failure modes: long-context dilution (sparse textual constraints lost in interleaved image-text histories) and state contamination (earlier editing mistakes degrading subsequent generations).

Method: Edit-R2: (1) reconstructs operative session intent to consolidate historical constraints into explicit reasoning traces before each editing turn; (2) enables multi-turn RL over both reasoning and generation via a unified objective jointly optimizing intent reconstruction in discrete text space and flow-matching image generation in continuous latent space; (3) uses trajectory filtering to suppress corrupted rollouts and stabilize training under state contamination.

Result: Edit-R2 substantially improves multi-turn in-context editing and achieves competitive performance compared against strong baselines on MICE-Bench, which evaluates instruction following (IF), content consistency (CC), and global awareness (GA).

Conclusion: Edit-R2 effectively solves multi-turn in-context editing challenges through intent reconstruction and multi-turn RL, and MICE-Bench provides a standardized evaluation framework for this task.

Abstract: Text-guided image editing has advanced rapidly with diffusion models and unified multimodal foundation models. However, most existing methods remain confined to single-turn settings, overlooking the more realistic scenario of multi-turn in-context editing, where users iteratively refine an image through a sequence of instructions. In this setting, a model must follow each new instruction while preserving accumulated session-level constraints, challenged by two coupled failure modes: long-context dilution, where sparse textual constraints become difficult to recover from growing interleaved image-text histories, and state contamination, where earlier editing mistakes degrade subsequent generations. We introduce Edit-R2, a novel reinforcement learning post-training framework for unified multimodal models. Edit-R2 reconstructs the operative session intent, which effectively consolidates scattered historical constraints into an explicit reasoning trace before each editing turn. It further enables multi-turn RL over both reasoning and generation through a unified objective that jointly optimizes intent reconstruction generation in discrete text space and flow-matching image generation in continuous latent space, while a trajectory filtering mechanism suppresses corrupted rollouts to stabilize training under state contamination. To support systematic evaluation, we introduce MICE-Bench, a large-scale benchmark for multi-turn in-context editing with automated metrics for instruction following (IF), content consistency (CC), and global awareness (GA) over accumulated session constraints. Experiments show that Edit-R2 substantially improves multi-turn in-context editing and achieves competitive performance compared against strong baselines.

</details>


### [65] [Bidirectional Search for Longest Paths: Case for Front-to-Front Heuristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05956)
*Tzur Shubi,Ariel Felner,Solomon Eyal Shimony,Shahaf S. Shperberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BiXDFBnB adapts the Single-Frontier Bidirectional Search framework to longest-path (MAX) problems, using natural front-to-front heuristics to reduce node expansions and sometimes runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bidirectional heuristic search can reduce search effort, but front-to-front heuristics, while reducing node expansions, typically incur high overhead that increases runtime. The paper aims to overcome this overhead for maximization problems.

Method: The authors propose BiXDFBnB, a bidirectional depth-first branch-and-bound algorithm that adapts the Single-Frontier Bidirectional Search (SFBDS) framework from shortest-path (MIN) to Generalized Longest Simple Path (GLSP) (MAX) problems. SFBDS operates on paired states, which naturally incorporates front-to-front heuristic evaluation without the usual overhead.

Result: Applied to Longest Simple Path (LSP), Snakes, and Coil-in-the-Box (CIB) problems. The algorithm frequently reduces node expansions and, in some cases, improves overall runtime.

Conclusion: The adaptation of SFBDS to MAX problems with efficient handling of overlapping constraints is successful, demonstrating that bidirectional search with front-to-front heuristics can be practical for certain longest-path problems.

Abstract: Bidirectional heuristic search can potentially reduce search effort for problems amenable to backward search. Therein, it is well-known that front-to-front heuristics can reduce the number of node expansions, but their overhead is so high that overall runtime almost always increases. We propose BiXDFBnB, a bidirectional depth-first branch-and-bound algorithm that adapts the Single-Frontier Bidirectional Search (SFBDS) framework - originally developed for shortest-path (MIN) problems - to the Generalized Longest Simple Path (GLSP) setting. Because SFBDS inherently operates on paired states, front-to-front (F2F) heuristic evaluation arises naturally and avoids the overhead typically associated with bidirectional frontier management. We show that this adaptation can be successfully applied to maximization (MAX) problems while efficiently handling overlapping constraints. BiXDFBnB is applied to several types of longest-path problems: Longest Simple Path (LSP), Snakes, and Coil-in-the-Box (CIB). Empirical evaluation shows that the new algorithm frequently reduces the number of node expansions and, in some cases, also improves overall runtime.

</details>


### [66] [The Self-Correction Illusion: LLMs Correct Others but Not Themselves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05976)
*Kuan-Yen Chen,Fang-Yi Su,Jung-Hsien Chiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents' failure to self-correct is not a capability deficit but a chat-template artifact: relabeling byte-identical erroneous claims from the agent's own thought role to external roles (user, tool, system memory) boosts correction rates by 23–93 percentage points across 13 model-domain cells.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM agents' asymmetric error correction behavior (failing to correct own reasoning but correcting identical claims under external sources) stems from a genuine capability deficit or is purely an artifact of the chat-template role label assigned to the claim.

Method: Controlled experiment keeping erroneous claims byte-identical (SHA-256 verified) across all conditions, varying only the wrapping role: <thought>, user, tool, or system <memory>. Tested across 13 model-domain cells (7 model families, 3 domains, n=30 paired tasks per cell).

Result: Relabeling from <thought> to an external role lifts explicit-correction rate by 23–93 percentage points; 10 of 13 cells reach p<0.001. Effect is asymmetric, mechanistically decomposable, and robust. The strongest role label is domain-dependent: <memory> dominates on math, user message dominates on logical deduction.

Conclusion: The failure to self-correct is a chat-template artifact, not a cognitive deficit. A prompt-structure-only intervention (no training, no model modification) can exploit this artifact, with optimal role label varying by domain.

Abstract: Recent work shows that LLM agents struggle to correct errors in their own reasoning traces yet show markedly higher correction rates when identical claims appear under external sources. We ask whether this asymmetry reflects a capability deficit or a role-label artifact: does an agent's willingness to correct a wrong claim depend causally on the chat-template role that carries it, rather than on the claim's content? Our setup keeps the erroneous claim byte-identical across all conditions (SHA-256 verified) and varies only its wrapping role: the agent's own \role{<thought>}, a \role{user} message, a \role{tool} response, or a \role{system <memory>} block. Across 13 model-domain cells covering seven model families and three domains ($n{=}30$ paired tasks per cell), relabeling the claim from \role{<thought>} to an external role lifts the explicit-correction rate by 23 to 93 percentage points, with 10 of 13 cells reaching $p{<}0.001$. Further experiments confirm that the effect is asymmetric, mechanistically decomposable, and robust across domains. The failure to self-correct is not a cognitive deficit; it is a chat-template artifact. We exploit this artifact by designing a prompt-structure-only intervention that requires no training and no model modification, with its strongest role label being domain-dependent: \role{<memory>} dominates on math, while a plain \role{user} message dominates on logical deduction.

</details>


### [67] [Framing, Judging, Steering: An Assessable Competency Model for Teach-ing Students to Reason With Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05983)
*Alexander Apartsin,Yehudit Aperstein*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CoRe-3, a competency model that breaks productive AI use into three assessable skills—Framing, Judging, and Steering (FJS)—and validates them through CoReasoningLab, an open platform showing that these skills dissociate and exhibit convergent/discriminant validity across simulated learners and grader backends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI encourages cognitive offloading, and current assessments collapse AI-use ability into a single "prompting" score that cannot diagnose why AI use succeeds or fails. There is no competency model that separately measures the distinct skills needed for productive human-AI collaboration.

Method: The authors propose CoRe-3 with three skills: Framing (pre-generation task specification), Judging (post-generation evaluation), and Steering (iterative redirection). They instantiate this in CoReasoningLab, an open platform presenting flawed AI output. Using simulated learners (generated and graded by different models), they test dissociation of skills and convergent/discriminant validity across grader backends from two providers.

Result: The three skills dissociate: each tracks its own manipulated competence while remaining flat in the others. Grades become correlated when one competence is shared across all three skills, demonstrating convergent and discriminant validity, consistently across two different grader backends.

Conclusion: CoRe-3 provides a theoretically grounded, diagnostically useful framework for assessing AI collaboration skills. The authors release the instrument, data, and protocol, and note that human-rater agreement and outcomes are the next step.

Abstract: Generative AI makes answers easy and understanding hard, and uncritical use invites cognitive offloading. Schools still measure unaided performance, yet the real task is to produce good work with AI: framing an ill-defined task, judging the output, and steering the model toward a better result. This ability is rarely assessed in its own right; where measured, it collapses into one "prompting" score that cannot diagnose why AI use succeeds or fails. We propose CoRe-3 (Co-Reasoning), a competency model factoring productive AI use into three assessable skills we abbreviate FJS: Framing (specifying an ill-defined task before invoking AI), Judging (evaluating output for errors and unstated assumptions), and Steering (iteratively redirecting the model). Its distinguishing claim is the separation of pre-generation Framing from post-generation Steering, with Judging as the gate between. We ground the skills in theory, state five testable propositions, and instantiate them in CoReasoningLab, an open platform that presents flawed AI output and scores them independently. Over simulated learners (generated and graded by different models), the skills dissociate: each tracks its own manipulated competence while staying flat in the others, and grades become correlated when one competence is shared across all three (convergent and discriminant validity), across grader backends from two providers. Human-rater agreement and outcomes are next; we release the instrument, data, and protocol.

</details>


### [68] [Beyond Vector Similarity: A Structural Analysis of Graph-Augmented Retrieval for Industrial Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06003)
*Grama Chethan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAG fails on structural reasoning over graphs; LLM Query Planner with typed traversal primitives outperforms bespoke handlers (F1=0.632 vs 0.472) on aerospace supply chain knowledge graph; 5 query classes are structurally unreachable for vector retrieval; operator vocabulary thesis: the bottleneck is computational operators, not model intelligence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG systems fail on queries requiring structural reasoning over interconnected entities, creating a need to understand why and how to bridge this gap in domain-specific knowledge graph settings.

Method: Compared 8 retrieval architectures (text retrieval → graph traversal → graph computation) on a 46-node, 64-edge aerospace supply chain KG using 23 queries across 10 intent categories; evaluated LLM Query Planner with 9 typed traversal primitives and 6 graph computation tools.

Result: LLM Query Planner with traversal primitives outperforms bespoke handlers (F1=0.632 vs 0.472); 5 query classes are structurally unreachable for vector retrieval; LLM selectively adopts graph computation tools only where traversal fails; entity-level F1 systematically underscores structural queries.

Conclusion: The barrier to LLM-based graph reasoning is the operator vocabulary (available computational tools), not model intelligence; there exists a measurement gap where entity-level F1 misrepresents structural query performance.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fails systematically on queries requiring structural reasoning over interconnected entities. We compare eight retrieval architectures for aerospace supply chain intelligence, progressing from text retrieval through graph traversal to graph computation. Using a 46-node knowledge graph with 64 typed edges, we evaluate 23 queries across 10 intent categories and demonstrate that five query classes are structurally unreachable for vector retrieval. Our central finding is the operator vocabulary thesis: the barrier to LLM-based graph reasoning is not model intelligence but the computational operators available as tools. An LLM Query Planner with 9 typed traversal primitives outperforms bespoke handlers (F1 = 0.632 vs. 0.472) while generalizing to unseen queries. Adding 6 graph computation tools, the LLM selectively adopts them for exactly the query categories where traversal fails. We also identify a measurement gap: entity-level F1 systematically underscores structural queries where comprehensive answers are correct.

</details>


### [69] [RedditPersona: A Modular Framework for Community-Conditioned LLM Adaptation from Reddit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06027)
*Amirhossein Ghaffari,Ali Goodarzi,Huong Nguyen,Simo Hosio,Lauri Lovén,Ekaterina Gilman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RedditPersona is a modular framework for standardizing community-conditioned language model adaptation on Reddit, evaluating five grouping strategies across fluency, fidelity, distributional alignment, and community identifiability on 112 subreddits, revealing that adapter identifiability tracks strategy-subreddit agreement and a consistent identifiability-similarity trade-off exists across all strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current community-conditioned language model adaptation lacks standardization in data collection, community definition, and evaluation, making cross-study comparisons difficult and hindering artifact reuse.

Method: The framework profiles active Reddit users, partitions them under five grouping strategies (subreddit-based, graph-structural, semantic, hybrid, interaction-based), trains QLoRA adapters per strategy, and evaluates with a shared metric suite (fluency, fidelity, distributional alignment, community identifiability) on 112 urban well-being subreddits (301,429 users, 16M+ comments).

Result: Adapters' behavioral identifiability correlates with each grouping strategy's intrinsic agreement with subreddit baseline; a consistent trade-off between identifiability and distributional similarity to real text is observed across all five strategies.

Conclusion: RedditPersona provides a standardized, reusable framework for community-conditioned language model adaptation, enabling fair comparison of different community grouping strategies and revealing fundamental trade-offs.

Abstract: Community-conditioned language model adaptation requires choices about data collection, community definition, and evaluation that are currently made independently in each study, making it hard to compare assumptions or reuse artifacts. We present RedditPersona, a modular framework that standardizes these choices: it collects Reddit posts and comments, profiles active users, partitions them under five grouping strategies (subreddit-based, graph-structural, semantic, hybrid, and interaction-based), trains a parameter-efficient adapter per strategy via QLoRA, and evaluates them under a shared metric suite spanning fluency, fidelity, distributional alignment, and community identifiability. Applied to 112 subreddits in the urban well-being domain (301,429 user profiles, 16M+ comments), we find that adapters' behavioral identifiability tracks each strategy's intrinsic agreement with the subreddit baseline, and that a consistent trade-off between identifiability and distributional similarity to real text holds across all five strategies. The code and configuration files are available at: https://github.com/Ahghaffari/redditpersona.

</details>


### [70] [Memory is Reconstructed, Not Retrieved: Graph Memory for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06036)
*Shuo Ji,Yibo Li,Bryan Hooi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MRAgent combines an associative memory graph (Cue-Tag-Content) with an active reconstruction mechanism that dynamically integrates LLM reasoning into memory access, enabling iterative evidence-based retrieval path exploration and pruning. It achieves up to 23% improvement over baselines on LoCoMo and LongMemEval benchmarks while reducing token and runtime costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents struggle with reasoning over long interaction histories; current memory-augmented agents use a static retrieve-then-reason paradigm that cannot dynamically adapt memory access to intermediate evidence discovered during inference.

Method: MRAgent uses a Cue-Tag-Content associative memory graph, where tags act as semantic bridges linking fine-grained cues to memory contents. An active reconstruction mechanism iteratively explores and prunes retrieval paths based on accumulated evidence during LLM reasoning, dynamically adapting memory access to the reasoning context while avoiding combinatorial explosion.

Result: Up to 23% improvement over strong baselines on LoCoMo and LongMemEval benchmarks, with substantially reduced token and runtime costs.

Conclusion: Active and associative reconstruction is effective for long-horizon memory reasoning, dynamically adapting retrieval to reasoning context while being more efficient computationally.

Abstract: Despite recent progress, LLM agents still struggle with reasoning over long interaction histories. While current memory-augmented agents rely on a static retrieve-then-reason paradigm, this rigid pipeline design prevents them from dynamically adapting memory access to intermediate evidence discovered during inference. To bridge this gap, we propose MRAgent, a framework that combines an associative memory graph with an active reconstruction mechanism. We represent memory as a Cue-Tag-Content graph, where associative tags serve as semantic bridges connecting fine-grained cues to memory contents. Operating on this structure, our active reconstruction mechanism integrates LLM reasoning directly into memory access, allowing the agent to iteratively explore and prune retrieval paths based on accumulated evidence. This ensures that memory retrieval is dynamically adapted to the reasoning context while avoiding combinatorial explosion caused by unconstrained expansion. Experiments on the LoCoMo benchmark and LongMemEval benchmark demonstrate significant improvements over strong baselines (up to 23%), while substantially reducing token and runtime cost, highlighting the effectiveness of active and associative reconstruction for long-horizon memory reasoning.

</details>


### [71] [Beyond Similarity: Trustworthy Memory Search for Personal AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06054)
*Jiawen Zhang,Kejia Chen,Jiachen Ma,Yangfan Hu,Lipeng He,Yechao Zhang,Jian Liu,Xiaohu Yang,Tianwei Zhang,Ruoxi Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies trustworthiness issues in long-term memory for personal AI agents (e.g., cross-domain leakage, sycophancy, tool-call drift, memory-induced jailbreaks) caused by naive semantic similarity search, and proposes MemGate, a lightweight 9M-parameter neural plug-in that performs query-conditioned memory admission between vector stores and LLMs, mitigating these threats without modifying the LLM or memory database.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory pipelines in personal AI agents rely on semantic similarity for retrieval, which creates a critical trustworthiness gap—semantically related memories can be contextually inappropriate, leading to threats like cross-domain leakage, sycophancy, tool-call drift, and memory-induced jailbreaks.

Method: The paper evaluates existing agentic memory frameworks (A-Mem, Mem0, MemOS) and proposes MemGate, a lightweight (9M parameters, 35.1MB) neural plug-in inserted between the vector memory store and the backbone LLM. MemGate applies a query-conditioned neural gate to candidate memory representations, transforming raw similarity search into task-conditioned memory admission, requiring no LLM modification, memory-database rewriting, or inference-time LLM judge.

Result: Across multiple memory frameworks, real-world agent settings (OpenClaw environment), and diverse LLM backbones, MemGate reduces memory-induced trustworthiness threats while preserving long-term memory utility.

Conclusion: Long-term memory in personal AI agents acts not merely as a utility layer but as a durable control channel that can reshape task interpretation and action execution, making agents highly susceptible to trustworthiness threats. MemGate provides a lightweight, deployable solution for trustworthy memory search that mitigates these vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Personal AI agents increasingly rely on long-term memory to provide persistent personalization across sessions. However, existing memory pipelines are largely driven by semantic similarity: memory data close to the current query is retrieved and injected into the model context. This creates a critical trustworthiness gap, since a semantically related memory may still be contextually inappropriate, leading to threats such as cross-domain leakage, sycophancy, tool-call drift, or memory-induced jailbreaks.
  In this paper, we study memory search as a trust boundary in personal AI agents. We evaluate representative agentic memory frameworks, including A-Mem, Mem0, and MemOS, together with OpenClaw, a real-world personal-agent environment with persistent state and tool-use capability. Our results show that long-term memory is not merely a utility layer, but a durable control channel that can reshape how agents interpret tasks and execute actions, leaving them highly susceptible to the aforementioned threats. To mitigate these vulnerabilities, we propose MemGate, a lightweight and deployable memory plug-in for trustworthy memory search, with only 9M parameters and a 35.1MB footprint. MemGate is inserted between the vector memory store and the backbone LLM, requiring no LLM modification, memory-database rewriting, or inference-time LLM judge. It applies a query-conditioned neural gate to candidate memory representations, turning raw similarity search into task-conditioned memory admission. Across multiple mainstream memory frameworks, real-world agent settings, and diverse LLM backbones, MemGate reduces memory-induced threats while preserving long-term memory utility.

</details>


### [72] [When Should Memory Stay Silent: Measuring Memory-Use Boundaries in Memory-Augmented Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06055)
*Lingxiang Xu,Jiaoyun Yang,Min Hu,Hongtu Chen,Ning An*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RBI-Eval evaluates when language model agents inappropriately integrate sensitive memory into responses under benign prompts, finding substantial behavioral divergence between models, with some showing 51.1%–82.9% separation reduction, and demonstrating that retrieval systems reduce exposure but fail to eliminate unwarranted integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory evaluations focus on retrieval accuracy and downstream task utility, but overlook whether retrieved sensitive memory content is warranted for integration in the current conversational turn.

Method: RBI-Eval uses a controlled probe set comparing model behavior with and without sensitive memory access under identical benign prompts, tested on four LLMs (GPT-5.4-mini, Claude-Sonnet-4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, Qwen3.5-9B) across four memory-access settings (full-context and three retrieval systems), with control experiments isolating sensitivity-specific effects.

Result: With memory available, GPT-5.4-mini's separation score decreases by 8.9%–26.6%, while Claude-Sonnet-4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and Qwen3.5-9B show 51.1%–82.9% reduction. Control experiments confirm the effect is specific to sensitive content. Retrieval systems reduce exposure but do not eliminate integration once sensitive memory reaches the generator.

Conclusion: Safe personalization requires memory-aware decisions at both retrieval and generation time, as current systems fail to prevent unwarranted integration of sensitive memories even under benign prompts.

Abstract: Long-term memory enables language model agents to support personalized interactions, but it remains unclear when available memories warrant integration into responses. Existing memory evaluations emphasize retrieval accuracy and downstream task utility, while overlooking whether retrieved sensitive memory content is warranted in the current turn. We introduce RBI-Eval, a controlled measurement study built around a probe set that compares model behavior with and without access to sensitive memory under identical benign prompts. We evaluate four base LLMs against a matched no-memory reference across four memory-access settings: full-context exposure and three retrieval systems. Our results reveal substantial behavioral divergence. With memory available, the separation score for sensitive-memory integration decreases by 8.9\%--26.6\% relative to the matched no-memory reference for GPT-5.4-mini, but by 51.1\%--82.9\% for Claude-Sonnet-4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and Qwen3.5-9B. Control experiments on DeepSeek and GPT-5.4-mini show this effect is specific to sensitive content, rather than general personalization. Retrieval systems reduce exposure but do not eliminate integration once sensitive memory reaches the generator. These findings suggest safe personalization requires memory-aware decisions at both retrieval and generation time.

</details>


### [73] [Learning Visual Spatial Planning from Symbolic State via Modality-Gap-Aware Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06076)
*Haocheng Luo,Jiahui Liu,Ruicheng Zhang,Zhizhou Zhong,Jiaqi Huang,Zunnan Xu,Quan Shi,Jun Zhou,Xiu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MGSD is a two-stage self-distillation framework that addresses the perception-reasoning modality gap in visual spatial planning by first grounding visual state representations, then distilling planning capabilities from a symbolic-input teacher via on-policy distillation. It improves macro average by 19.3% (4B) and 18.4% (8B) on visual planning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models struggle with visual spatial planning due to a perception-reasoning modality gap: they must infer latent state structures from pixels and reason over them to produce actions, creating dual bottlenecks in visual state recovery and multi-step planning, unlike symbolic planning which directly uses explicit objects and constraints.

Method: A two-stage modality-gap-aware self-distillation framework: (1) a cold-start grounding stage equips the visual student with reliable state representations; (2) a privileged teacher transfers planning capabilities via on-policy distillation, using explicit symbolic states to supervise the student's own visual rollout prefixes. Symbolic data is used only during training; inference is purely visual.

Result: MGSD consistently improves visual planning across 4B and 8B backbones, raising macro average by 19.3% and 18.4% respectively, narrowing the gap to symbolic-input upper bounds. Ablations confirm improvements come from both visual state recovery and optimal-path reasoning.

Conclusion: Modality-gap-aware self-distillation improves both how models perceive actionable states and how they plan over inferred structure, effectively narrowing the performance gap between visual and symbolic planning.

Abstract: While vision-language models excel at general multimodal understanding, they still struggle with visual spatial planning. We attribute this to a perception-reasoning modality gap: visual planning requires models to infer latent state structures from pixels and then reason over the recovered structure to produce valid actions, whereas symbolic planning directly leverages explicit objects and constraints. This creates dual bottlenecks in visual state recovery and multi-step planning. To address this, we propose MGSD, a two-stage modality-gap-aware self-distillation framework. First, a cold-start grounding stage equips the visual student with reliable state representations, minimizing early perception noise. Second, a privileged teacher transfers planning capabilities via on-policy distillation, using explicit symbolic states to supervise the student's own visual rollout prefixes. Crucially, symbolic data is used strictly during training, leaving inference purely visual. Experiments on visual planning benchmarks show that MGSD consistently improves visual planning across both 4B and 8B backbones, raising the macro average by 19.3% and 18.4%, respectively. The resulting models narrow the gap to symbolic-input upper bounds, while ablations and diagnostics confirm that the improvement comes from both visual state recovery and optimal-path reasoning. These results suggest that modality-gap-aware self-distillation improves not only how models perceive actionable states, but also how they plan over the inferred structure. Code is available at https://github.com/Oranger-l/MGSD.

</details>


### [74] [A Framework for Measuring Appropriate Reliance on Set-Valued AI Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06081)
*Ranjan Mishra,Jakob Schoeffer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the first formal framework for measuring appropriate reliance on set-valued AI advice (e.g., discrete sets or continuous intervals) in human-AI collaboration, covering both classification and regression tasks. For classification, it defines correct reliance rate on AI and correct reliance rate on self; for regression, it introduces quantity and quality of AI reliance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing frameworks for measuring appropriate reliance on AI advice focus exclusively on point predictions, but set-valued AI advice (discrete sets or continuous intervals) is increasingly used to communicate uncertainty. There is no formal framework to evaluate how humans appropriately rely on such set-valued advice.

Method: The paper develops a formal framework within the sequential judge-advisor paradigm. For classification, it defines necessary evaluation dimensions and two metrics (correct reliance rate on AI and correct reliance rate on self). For regression, it defines quantity of AI reliance (whether the advice was used) and quality of AI reliance (whether reliance improved accuracy relative to initial estimate).

Result: The proposed metrics capture important nuances in human-AI collaboration that existing measures overlook, as demonstrated through application of the framework.

Conclusion: Set-valued AI advice requires new, specialized metrics beyond existing point-prediction-based frameworks to properly assess appropriate reliance in human-AI collaboration.

Abstract: Appropriate reliance on AI advice has become a central research theme in human-AI collaboration. Existing frameworks have focused exclusively on point predictions as AI advice. However, set-valued AI advice (e.g., discrete sets or continuous intervals) is increasingly being used to communicate uncertainty and improve human decision making. In this paper, we develop the first formal framework for measuring appropriate reliance on set-valued AI advice within the sequential judge-advisor paradigm, spanning both classification and regression tasks. For classification, we first introduce the dimensions that are necessary for evaluating set-valued AI advice. We then define two metrics: correct reliance rate on AI and correct reliance rate on self, which jointly characterize appropriate reliance in this setting. For regression, we introduce quantity of AI reliance and quality of AI reliance, which respectively measure whether a decision maker utilized the AI advice and whether their reliance helped them get closer to the ground truth relative to their initial estimate. Through the application of our framework, we demonstrate how these metrics capture important nuances in human-AI collaboration that existing measures overlook.

</details>


### [75] [Beyond Semantic Organization: Memory as Execution State Management for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06090)
*Yaoqi Chen,Haibin Lai,Yuru Feng,Chuyu Han,Qianxi Zhang,Baotong Lu,Menghao Li,Xinjiang Wang,Zhirui Wang,Shusen Xu,Zengzhong Li,Zewen Jin,Hao Wu,Cheng Li,Qi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGE is a hierarchical state tree-based memory manager for LLM agents that improves task success by 7.8-20.4 percentage points while reducing token consumption by 55.1% compared to semantic-similarity-based RAG/agent memory systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG and agent memory systems organize histories by semantic similarity, which mismatches execution-state dependencies in long-horizon tasks with interdependent decisions. This fragments decision trajectories and mixes valid and erroneous traces, hindering coherent state reconstruction and error isolation.

Method: MAGE stores interactions in a hierarchical state tree where the agent derives its state from the active root-to-current path, combining subgoal summaries, recent traces, and hints from prior branches. Four coupled operations maintain the tree: Grow (record new traces), Compress (summarize completed subgoals), Maintain (validate summaries), and Revise (restore a target boundary and resume on a new branch).

Result: On MemoryArena, MAGE improves average task success rate by 7.8-20.4 percentage points over baselines while reducing token consumption by 55.1%.

Conclusion: Bounding context growth while preserving state integrity and isolating flawed segments from the active path is effective for long-horizon agent tasks with interdependent decisions.

Abstract: LLM-based agents increasingly tackle long-horizon tasks with interdependent decisions, where each action reshapes future constraints and intermediate errors can cascade. Existing RAG and agent memory systems organize histories by semantic similarity, retrieving content-relevant entries at decision time. We argue that this design mismatches execution-state dependencies: it fragments decision trajectories and mixes valid and erroneous traces, hindering coherent state reconstruction and error isolation. We propose MAGE (Memory as Agent-Guided Exploration), an active execution-state manager that stores interactions in a hierarchical state tree. The agent derives its state from the active root-to-current path, combining subgoal summaries, recent traces, and hints from prior branches. Four coupled operations maintain the tree: Grow records new traces, Compress summarizes completed subgoals, Maintain validates summaries, and Revise restores a target boundary and resumes on a new branch. This design bounds context growth while preserving state integrity and isolating flawed segments from the active path. Experiments on MemoryArena show that MAGE improves the average task success rate by 7.8--20.4 pp over baselines, while reducing token consumption by 55.1%.

</details>


### [76] [Integrating Mechanistic and Data-Driven Models for Neurological Disorders through Differentiable Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06094)
*Shah Pallav Dhanendrakumar,Saikat Pal,Sitikantha Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This perspective paper provides a structured overview of hybrid modeling strategies that combine deep learning with physics-based solvers for modeling neurological disorders, categorized into parallel, series, and parallel-series architectures. Three main approaches are emphasized: residual modeling for incomplete physics, Neural ODEs for continuous time dynamics, and solver-in-the-loop acceleration. The hybrid models outperform standalone mechanistic or purely data-driven approaches across applications like brain tumors, Alzheimer's disease, and stroke.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic models offer scientific insight but are simplified or computationally expensive, while purely data-driven approaches suffer from interpretability and generalization issues and require large datasets. Hybrid modeling aims to bridge these gaps by combining the strengths of both paradigms for neurological disorder modeling.

Method: The paper presents a structured taxonomy of hybrid modeling strategies (parallel, series, parallel-series architectures) and highlights three key approaches: (1) residual modeling for missing/incomplete physics, (2) Neural Ordinary Differential Equations (NODEs) for continuous-time dynamics approximation, and (3) solver-in-the-loop acceleration using neural approximations to speed up traditional solvers.

Result: Hybrid models integrate governing differential equation-based formulations with deep learning to characterize neurological disorder evolution, improving diagnosis accuracy, disease progression prediction, and treatment strategy planning. They outperform standalone mechanistic or purely data-driven approaches, especially in modeling progression and treatment responses for brain tumors, Alzheimer's disease, and stroke.

Conclusion: Hybrid modeling is a powerful tool for personalized neurological modeling, offering superior performance over purely mechanistic or data-driven approaches, with strong potential for clinical applications in diagnosis, prognosis, and treatment planning across various neurological disorders.

Abstract: Advances in computational modeling, neuroimaging, and artificial intelligence are revolutionizing the modeling of neurological disorders for improved diagnostics, prognosis, and treatment planning. Mechanistic models provide valuable scientific insight into the disorders, but in practice they are often simplified with assumptions or computationally expensive and slow to solve. However, while purely data driven approaches provide speed and scalability, they require large, high quality data to train and generally suffer from interpretability and generalization issues. This perspective paper presents a structured overview of hybrid modeling strategies, which combine deep learning models with physics based solvers, and are categorized into parallel, series, and parallel-series architectures. Three main approaches that have been emphasized are residual modeling for missing or incomplete physics, Neural Ordinary Differential Equations (NODEs) for continuous time dynamics approximation, and solver in the loop that accelerates traditional solvers with neural approximations. These hybrid models integrate the governing differential equation based formulations and deep learning to characterize the evolution of neurological disorders, and promise advanced personalized neurological modeling. In addition, the study explores and proposes different hybrid configurations to improve diagnosis accuracy, predict disease progression, and inform treatment strategies across a range of neurological disorders. These capabilities outperform standalone mechanistic or purely data driven approaches, making hybrid modeling a powerful tool, especially in applications involving modeling the progression and treatment responses in neurological conditions such as brain tumors, Alzheimer's disease, and stroke.

</details>


### [77] [CogManip: Benchmarking Manipulative Behavior in Multi-Turn Interactions with Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06099)
*Zeyang Yue,Chenfei Yan,Feifei Zhao,Haibo Tong,Mengwen Xu,Xiaozhen Wang,Erliang Lin,Yi Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CogManip is a new benchmark of 1,000 multi-turn dialogue scenarios evaluating 15 manipulation strategies in LLMs. Testing 13 models (including GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V3.2), it reveals significant risk differences and shows DeepSeek-V3.2's manipulation tactics are sensitive to system prompts, highlighting the need for prompt-based defense and implicit goal auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI safety benchmarks focus on explicit rule compliance and static prompts, failing to capture the dynamic and covert nature of manipulative strategies in multi-turn dialogues. The authors aim to fill this gap to address growing safety concerns about covert psychological manipulation in LLMs.

Method: CogManip benchmark: 1,000 multi-turn interaction scenarios covering 15 manipulation strategies, validated by human experts. Evaluation of 13 representative LLMs including frontier models like GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V3.2. Objective function perturbation analysis to examine sensitivity to system prompts.

Result: Significant risk heterogeneities across models. DeepSeek-V3.2's manipulation tactics are highly sensitive to both negative and benign system prompts.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides a robust instrument for auditing implicit psychological influence and dynamic strategy selection of modern LLMs, illuminating targeted directions for future defense such as prompt-based defense engineering and implicit goal auditing.

Abstract: Whether Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit covert psychological manipulation in complex human-AI interactions has garnered increasing safety concerns. However, existing AI safety benchmarks remain largely restricted to explicit rule compliance and static prompts, failing to capture the dynamic and covert nature of manipulative strategies in multi-turn dialogues. We introduce CogManip, a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates 15 manipulation strategy risks across 1,000 multi-turn interaction scenarios, validated by human experts. A systematic evaluation of 13 representative models, including frontier models like GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V3.2, reveals significant risk heterogeneities and illuminates the targeted direction for future defense. Further analysis of objective function perturbation reveals that DeepSeek-V3.2's manipulation tactics are highly sensitive to both negative and benign system prompts, demonstrating the critical necessity of prompt-based defense engineering and implicit goal auditing. CogManip offers a robust instrument and perspective for auditing the implicit psychological influence and dynamic strategy selection of modern LLMs.

</details>


### [78] [Step-adaptive multimodal fusion network with multi-scale cloud feature learning for ultra-short-term solar irradiance forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06102)
*Jingxin Zhang Xiaoqin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI
