TL;DR: The paper proposes a multi-source data fusion model for ultra-short-term solar irradiance prediction that uses InceptionNeXt for multi-scale spatial feature extraction from cloud images, a step-adaptive low-frequency compensation unit, and TempAttnLSTM for temporal dependency modeling, outperforming state-of-the-art methods on NREL and real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches have three key shortcomings: (1) single time-series models cannot capture spatial cloud dynamics under complex conditions, (2) standard convolutions inadequately represent multi-scale cloud features, and (3) fixed low-frequency compensation strategies fail to adapt to different prediction steps.

Method: The model has three components: (1) InceptionNeXt extracts multi-scale, multi-directional spatial features from ground-based cloud images; (2) a step-adaptive low-frequency compensation unit dynamically modulates global low-frequency information based on the prediction step; (3) enhanced image features are combined with meteorological time-series features, and TempAttnLSTM captures global temporal dependencies for multi-step prediction.

Result: Experiments on the public NREL dataset and practical photovoltaic stations in Shandong demonstrate the proposed method outperforms several state-of-the-art approaches.

Conclusion: The multi-source data fusion model with step-adaptive compensation effectively addresses the limitations of existing methods for ultra-short-term solar irradiance prediction.

Abstract: Ultra-short-term solar irradiance prediction is critical for photovoltaic system dispatch and power grid stability. Existing approaches suffer from three key shortcomings: single time-series models cannot capture the spatial dynamics of clouds under complex conditions, standard convolutions inadequately represent multi-scale cloud features, and fixed low-frequency compensation strategies fail to adapt to different prediction steps. To address these issues, this proposes a multi-source data fusion model for ultra-short-term irradiance prediction. The model first employs InceptionNeXt to extract multi-scale, multi-directional spatial features from ground-based cloud images. A step-adaptive low-frequency compensation unit is then introduced to dynamically modulate global low-frequency information based on the prediction step. Eventually, the enhanced image features are combined with meteorological time-series features, and a TempAttnLSTM network captures global temporal dependencies for multi-step prediction. Experiments on the public NREL dataset and practical photovoltaic stations in Shandong illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed method compared with several state-of-the-art approaches.

</details>


### [79] [Towards Healthy Evolution: Exploring the Role and Mechanisms of Human-Agent Interaction in Self-Evolving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06114)
*Dianxing Shi,Junqi He,Junhao Chen,Bowen Wang,Yuta Nakashima*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ANCHOR is an LLM-based framework that simulates human supervision feedback to mitigate capability degradation and safety drift in self-evolving agent systems, showing that limited supervision during output verification effectively preserves safety and performance stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving agents can experience capability degradation and safety drift during autonomous evolution, and the role of human feedback in such self-evolving systems remains underexplored.

Method: The authors propose ANCHOR, an LLM-based framework that simulates human-like oversight and delivers feedback at various phases of self-evolution. They evaluate it on two open-source self-evolving agent systems across coding, mathematical reasoning, and safety tasks.

Result: Limited supervision significantly mitigates safety degradation while preserving stable performance on core evolutionary objectives. Supervision over the output verification phase is the most effective, while increasing supervision frequency yields diminishing returns.

Conclusion: These findings provide empirical evidence and practical guidance for designing more stable, controllable, and human-aligned self-evolving agent systems.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents improve through continual self-play and self-generated learning signals, but autonomous evolution can also cause capability degradation and safety drift. Although human feedback has proven effective for static and post-trained agents, its role in self-evolving systems remains underexplored. We introduce Agent Norm Correction through Human-like Oversight and Review (ANCHOR), an LLM-based framework that simulates human supervision and delivers feedback at various phases of self-evolution. With ANCHOR, we evaluate two representative open-source self-evolving agent systems across coding, mathematical reasoning, and safety. Our results show that even limited supervision substantially mitigates safety degradation while preserving stable performance on core evolutionary objectives. Further analysis shows that supervision over the output verification phase is the most effective for intervention, whereas increasing supervision frequency yields diminishing returns. These findings provide empirical evidence and practical guidance for designing more stable, controllable, and human-aligned self-evolving agent systems.

</details>


### [80] [WorldFly: A World-Model-Based Vision-Language-Action Model for UAV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06147)
*Shengtao Zheng,Kai Li,Weichen Zhang,Yu Meng,Chen Gao,Xinlei Chen,Yong Li,Xiao-Ping Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorldFly introduces a world-model-based VLA framework with dual-branch coupled flow matching for joint future video prediction and navigation action generation, significantly improving UAV navigation in dense urban environments with severe occlusions and sharp turns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA models rely on historical observations to directly predict actions, which fails in dense urban environments where severe occlusions and drastic viewpoint transitions (e.g., sharp turns) create partial observability. The authors argue that the ability to "imagine" future states—inherent in World Models—is critical for robust decision-making under such conditions.

Method: WorldFly is a world-model-based VLA framework that uses a dual-branch coupled flow matching mechanism to jointly generate future video predictions and navigation actions, explicitly guiding the agent's policy via spatial imagination. A challenging Urban Canyon Traversal Benchmark was constructed to evaluate spatial understanding in occlusion-heavy, viewpoint-transition scenarios.

Result: WorldFly outperforms other baselines, particularly in unseen environments, on the Urban Canyon Traversal Benchmark, validating the effectiveness of integrating world models into embodied aerial agents.

Conclusion: Integrating world models into VLA frameworks improves spatial imagination and robust decision-making for UAV navigation in challenging urban settings, especially under partial observability.

Abstract: End-to-end Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown promise in UAV navigation. However, existing approaches typically rely on historical observations to directly predict actions, often struggling in dense urban environments where severe occlusions and sharp turns result in drastic viewpoint transitions. We argue that the ability to "imagine" future states -- inherent in World Models -- is critical for robust decision-making under such partial observability. To address this, we construct a challenging Urban Canyon Traversal Benchmark, specifically designed to evaluate spatial understanding in scenarios characterized by severe occlusions and drastic viewpoint transitions. To this end, we propose WorldFly, a novel world-model-based VLA framework that employs a dual-branch coupled flow matching mechanism to jointly generate future video predictions and navigation actions, thereby explicitly guiding the agent's policy via spatial imagination. Extensive evaluations on our benchmark demonstrate that WorldFly outperforms other baselines, particularly in unseen environments, validating the effectiveness of integrating world models into embodied aerial agents.

</details>


### [81] [Amortizing Federated Adaptation: Hypernetwork Driven LoRA for Personalized Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06154)
*Sunny Gupta,Shambhavi Shanker,Amit Sethi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperLoRA addresses structural aggregation bias and client-side initialization lag in federated LoRA by using a hypernetwork for amortized LoRA generation and product space aggregation, achieving faster convergence and better personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing federated LoRA methods suffer from structural aggregation bias (averaging low-rank factors independently fails to approximate true combined updates) and client-side initialization lag (repeated reinitialization of LoRA parameters slows convergence).

Method: HyperLoRA uses (1) a learned generator mapping client distribution signatures to LoRA initializations (amortized adaptation), (2) a server-side learned aggregation module that synthesizes updates in low-rank product space to avoid factor-wise averaging inconsistencies, and (3) a lightweight residual correction module for stability under non-IID client distributions.

Result: Experiments on federated vision and vision-language benchmarks show HyperLoRA achieves improved convergence speed, greater robustness to distribution shift, and stronger personalization compared to prior federated LoRA methods.

Conclusion: By replacing iterative optimization and heuristic averaging with learned operators, HyperLoRA jointly enables efficient personalization, unbiased aggregation, and faster convergence in federated fine-tuning.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning of foundation models using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) offers a communication efficient solution for distributed learning. However, existing federated LoRA methods suffer from two fundamental limitations: (1) structural aggregation bias, where independently averaging low rank factors fails to approximate the true combined update, and (2) client side initialization lag, as clients repeatedly reinitialize LoRA parameters across communication rounds, slowing convergence. We propose HyperLoRA, a unified framework that addresses both issues through amortized federated adaptation through hypernetwork-driven LoRA generation and product space aggregation. Instead of iterative per-client optimization, HyperLoRA employs a learned generator that maps client distribution signatures to LoRA initializations, effectively amortizing per client adaptation. On the server side, we introduce a learned aggregation module that directly synthesizes updates in the low-rank product space, eliminating the inconsistencies of factor-wise averaging. A lightweight residual correction module further improves stability under heterogenous (non-IID) client distributions.By replacing iterative optimization and heuristic averaging with learned operators, HyperLoRA jointly enables efficient personalization, unbiased aggregation, and faster convergence. Experiments on federated vision and vision-language benchmarks show that HyperLoRA achieves improved convergence speed, greater robustness to distribution shift, and stronger personalization performance compared to prior federated LoRA methods.

</details>


### [82] [Where does Absolute Position come from in decoder-only Transformers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06160)
*Valeria Ruscio,Umberto Nanni,Fabrizio Silvestri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RoPE-trained transformers exhibit absolute position leakage in attention patterns due to two causes: the causal mask's query-dependent softmax denominator and the residual stream's closed dynamical system at position 0. This leakage varies by architecture (NTK scaling, sliding-window attention, standard RoPE) and can be partially reduced by removing the BOS embedding. Attention sinks act as token-anchored stabilizers carrying a deterministic fingerprint of the first token.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why RoPE-trained transformers, which theoretically encode only relative position information, nonetheless exhibit absolute position sensitivity in their attention patterns.

Method: Analyze two architectural sources of absolute position leakage: (1) the causal mask's per-query softmax denominator, which inherently depends on absolute query position, and (2) the residual stream dynamics at position 0 under causal attention. Compare three architectures (NTK scaling, sliding-window attention, standard RoPE) and test BOS embedding replacement.

Result: Both architectural components cause absolute position leakage, with architecturally specific balance: NTK scaling suppresses residual-stream component, sliding-window attention allows accumulation with depth, standard RoPE sits between. Replacing BOS embedding removes 40% of residual-stream component at early queries.

Conclusion: Absolute position leakage in RoPE transformers stems from causal masking and residual stream dynamics; attention sinks are token-anchored stabilizers carrying a deterministic fingerprint of the first token.

Abstract: RoPE-trained transformers distinguish absolute position in their attention patterns, even though RoPE encodes only relative offsets in the inner product. We trace this leakage to two architectural components, The causal mask is responsible for the first: its per-query softmax denominator depends on the absolute query position by construction. The residual stream supplies the second. Under causal attention the activation at position $0$ attends only to itself and runs as a closed dynamical system from the embedding of the token at that position; downstream attention reads this trajectory through sink-reading heads. Both components appear in all three architectures we study, in architecturally specific balance: NTK scaling suppresses the residual-stream component, sliding-window attention allows it to accumulate with depth, and standard RoPE sits between. Replacing the \texttt{BOS} embedding before the forward pass removes $40\%$ of the residual-stream component at early queries. Attention sinks are token-anchored stabilizers that pass forward a deterministic fingerprint of the token at position $0$, constant across inputs when that token is the auto-prepended \texttt{BOS} and varying with it otherwise.

</details>


### [83] [ProSarc: Prosody-Aware Sarcasm Recognition Framework via Temporal Prosodic Incongruity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06168)
*Prathamjyot Singh,Ashima Sood,Sahil Sharma,Jasmeet Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProSarc is an audio-only sarcasm detection framework that models temporal prosodic incongruity using dual encoders (Global Emotion + Temporal Prosody) and an incongruity analyzer, achieving state-of-the-art results on MUStARD++ (F1=75.3) and generalizing to spontaneous (PodSarc, F1=62.9) and cross-lingual (MuSaG, F1=65.6) speech, with uncertainty estimation and sarcasm onset localization capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sarcasm detection in speech is challenging because sarcasm often manifests through subtle prosodic mismatches—where local vocal patterns deviate from the overall emotional tone of an utterance. Existing audio-only methods lack explicit modeling of this incongruity, limiting their accuracy and interpretability.

Method: ProSarc uses a dual-encoding architecture: a Global Emotion Encoder captures utterance-level emotional baseline, while a Temporal Prosody Encoder (BiLSTM + multi-head attention) models local prosodic dynamics. A Prosodic Incongruity Analyzer computes a scalar incongruity score between the two representations for binary classification. Monte Carlo dropout estimates prediction uncertainty, and attention weights localize sarcastic onset regions without frame-level supervision.

Result: ProSarc achieves F1=75.3 on MUStARD++, surpassing prior audio-only methods. It generalizes to spontaneous speech (PodSarc, F1=62.9) and cross-lingual data (MuSaG, F1=65.6). Statistical validation (Wilcoxon p=0.002, Cohen's d=1.51) confirms incongruity modeling's contribution. Human evaluation shows uncertainty correlates with perceptual ambiguity and predicted onsets align with human annotations.

Conclusion: Modeling temporal prosodic incongruity is an effective and generalizable approach for audio-only sarcasm detection. ProSarc not only improves classification accuracy but also provides interpretable uncertainty estimates and fine-grained temporal localization of sarcastic speech segments.

Abstract: We present ProSarc, an audio-only framework that detects sarcasm by modelling temporal prosodic incongruity, that is, the mismatch between local prosodic dynamics and the utterance-level emotional baseline. Dual encoding paths, a Global Emotion Encoder and a Temporal Prosody Encoder (BiLSTM + multi-head attention), feed a Prosodic Incongruity Analyzer that produces a scalar incongruity score for classification. Monte Carlo dropout provides uncertainty estimates, and an attention-based mechanism localises sarcastic onset without frame-level labels. ProSarc outperforms prior audio-only methods on MUStARD++ (F1=75.3) and generalises to spontaneous (PodSarc, F1=62.9) and cross-lingual speech (MuSaG, F1=65.6). Ten-run validation confirms the contribution of incongruity modelling (Wilcoxon p=0.002, Cohen's d=1.51). Human evaluation shows that model uncertainty tracks perceptual ambiguity and predicted onsets align with human-annotated temporal windows.

</details>


### [84] [Learning to replenish: A hybrid deep reinforcement learning for dynamic inventory management in the pharmaceutical supply chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06201)
*Amandeep Kaur,Gyan Prakash*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a deep reinforcement learning framework (hybrid A3C DPPO) to optimize pharmaceutical inventory replenishment under stochastic demand and variable lead times, formulated as a Markov decision process. The algorithm outperforms benchmarks in cost reduction and is validated with real-world data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pharmaceutical supply chains face complex inventory management challenges due to unpredictable demand, variable lead times, and finite product shelf lives, requiring sophisticated strategies to balance stock availability with waste minimization.

Method: Formulates inventory replenishment as a Markov decision process and proposes a hybrid asynchronous advantage actor-critic distributed proximal policy optimization (A3C DPPO) algorithm designed for continuous action spaces in inventory management.

Result: The A3C DPPO algorithm adaptively updates replenishment strategies under dynamic scenarios, achieving lower inventory costs compared to various benchmark methods. Real-world pharmaceutical data validates practical feasibility.

Conclusion: The proposed DRL-based approach effectively addresses stochasticity in pharmaceutical supply chains, maximizing profitability while maintaining high patient service levels, with demonstrated practical applicability.

Abstract: Pharmaceutical supply chains (PSCs) struggle with inventory management (IM) due to unpredictable demand patterns and variable lead times associated with restocking. This complexity is further compounded by the finite shelf lives of pharmaceutical products, which necessitate a delicate balance between adequate stock and minimal waste. These intertwined factors create a complex optimization problem that requires sophisticated inventory strategies to ensure both product availability and PSC efficiency. This study aims to develop an optimal inventory replenishment policy for pharmaceutical products that can handle the stochasticity arising from uncertain demand and variable PSC conditions. The objective is to maximize the profitability of the PSC while maintaining a high patient service level. We formulate the problem as a Markov decision process and propose a deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approach, specifically, a hybrid asynchronous advantage actor critic distributed proximal policy optimization (A3C DPPO)algorithm. The A3C DPPO algorithm is tailored to handle the continuous action space inherent in IM. The numerical results demonstrate that the proposed algorithm adaptively updates the inventory replenishment strategy under dynamic scenarios, resulting in lower inventory costs compared to various benchmarks. We also conduct numerical validation using real-world pharmaceutical inventory data to confirm the practical feasibility of the proposed algorithm.

</details>


### [85] [Unsupervised Pattern Analysis in Japanese Veterinary Toxicology: A Regulatory-Compliant Framework for Cross-Species Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06207)
*Yukiko Kawakami,Mohammad Shirazi,Ryo Shimizuwa,Saito Shinoda,Alireza Mortazavi,Matsumoto Kawahara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a regulation-integrated unsupervised framework for discovering adverse drug event patterns in Japanese veterinary pharmacovigilance data, identifying species-specific toxicity clusters (hepatic in companion animals, renal in ruminants, dermatological in sheep) with strong alignment to pharmacological classes and regulatory standards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing veterinary pharmacovigilance systems fail to capture region-specific toxicity patterns shaped by local biological and regulatory contexts (e.g., Japan's species-specific metabolic differences and MAFF reporting practices), and prior work relies on prediction-oriented models that lack mechanistic interpretability.

Method: An unsupervised framework using the NVAL database: ADEs are encoded into organ system-aligned representations, adjusted for species-specific reporting biases, followed by similarity-based clustering (cosine similarity) and dimensionality reduction to identify latent toxicity structures.

Result: Analysis of 4,120 ADE reports (9,080 drug-ADE combinations) identified three significant species clusters (p<0.01): hepatic-dominant in companion animals (0.42±0.06), renal toxicity in ruminants (0.39±0.07), and dermatological sensitivity in sheep (0.35±0.07). Drug-level clustering achieved 83% alignment with pharmacological classes; cosine similarity outperformed alternatives (silhouette: 0.48; precision: 87%).

Conclusion: Regulation-aligned unsupervised analysis can uncover biologically meaningful, region-specific toxicity patterns, providing an interpretable and scalable framework for veterinary drug safety assessment.

Abstract: Veterinary pharmacovigilance systems are essential for monitoring adverse drug events (ADEs), yet existing approaches often fail to capture region-specific toxicity patterns shaped by local biological and regulatory contexts. In Japan, these challenges are amplified by species-specific metabolic differences and reporting practices defined by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry, and Fisheries (MAFF). Most prior work relies on prediction-oriented models, limiting mechanistic interpretability. This study proposes a regulatory-integrated unsupervised framework for pattern discovery using the National Veterinary Assay Laboratory (NVAL) database. ADEs are encoded into organ system-aligned representations and adjusted for species-specific reporting biases, enabling cross-species comparison. Similarity-based clustering and dimensionality reduction are applied to identify latent toxicity structures. Analysis of 4,120 high-confidence ADE reports (9,080 drug-ADE combinations) identified three significant species clusters (p < 0.01), including hepatic-dominant patterns in companion animals (0.42 $\pm$ 0.06), renal toxicity in ruminants (0.39 $\pm$ 0.07), and dermatological sensitivity in sheep (0.35 $\pm$ 0.07). Drug-level clustering achieved 83% alignment with pharmacological classes, while cosine similarity outperformed alternative metrics (silhouette score: 0.48; cluster precision: 87%). Regulatory validation showed strong agreement with established classifications. These findings demonstrate that regulation-aligned unsupervised analysis can uncover biologically meaningful, region-specific toxicity patterns, providing an interpretable and scalable framework for veterinary drug safety assessment.

</details>


### [86] [Evaluating Agentic Configuration Repair for Computer Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06212)
*Rufat Asadli,Benjamin Hoffman,Ioannis Protogeros,Laurent Vanbever*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs augmented with formal network verification and context retrieval tools (agentic architectures) outperform base LLMs in repairing network misconfigurations by 12% on average and improve safety by 17% on average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Misconfigurations in computer networks remain a major source of critical Internet outages, and current LLMs fail to resolve misconfigurations in large-scale, complex scenarios while often introducing new errors.

Method: Benchmark open- and closed-source LLMs augmented with formal network verification and context retrieval tools using agentic architectures that enable dynamic context management and iterative validation of configuration repairs.

Result: Agentic architectures outperform base LLMs in repair efficacy by 12% on average and safety by 17% on average.

Conclusion: Agentic architectures with formal verification and context retrieval tools significantly improve LLM-based network configuration repair performance and safety.

Abstract: Misconfigurations in computer networks remain a major source of critical Internet outages. Research is turning to Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate the complex, error-prone task of network configuration. However, even state-of-the-art models fail to resolve misconfigurations in large-scale, complex scenarios and often introduce new errors. In this work, we benchmark open- and closed-source LLMs augmented with formal network verification and context retrieval tools. We demonstrate that agentic architectures outperform base LLMs in repair efficacy (by 12% on average) and safety (by 17% on average), enabled by the ability to dynamically manage context and iteratively validate configuration repairs.

</details>


### [87] [From Reward-Hack Activations to Agentic Risk States: Context-Calibrated Mechanistic Monitoring in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06223)
*Patrick Wilhelm,Odej Kao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies reward-hacking monitors in ReAct-style agents (ALFWorld and WebShop), finding that reward-hack tendencies can transfer via adapters but mitigation requires context-calibrated monitoring combining activation signals with entropy and decision-context features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety monitoring in language-model agents is complex due to the cyclical observation-reasoning-action loop, making it necessary to understand how internal model states and environmental context jointly influence reward-hacking behavior.

Method: Agents are instrumented with activation-based reward-hack scores, token-level entropy, and decision-context features. Adapters fine-tuned on School-of-Reward-Hacks dataset transfer reward-hack tendencies. Activation-direction steering is used to mitigate proxy-exploit behavior.

Result: Reward-hack activation identifies latent policy states but does not guarantee immediate exploits. Entropy and context-calibrated features improve risk estimation. Activation-direction steering reduces proxy-exploit behavior in mixed-adapter regimes.

Conclusion: Context-calibrated internal monitoring is necessary: reward-hack activation identifies latent risky policy states, while entropy and decision context help determine when that state translates into risky action.

Abstract: Language-model agents act through repeated cycles of observation, reasoning, and action selection, making safety monitoring depend on both internal model state and environment context. We study reward-hacking monitors in ReAct-style agents acting in Gameable ALFWorld and WebShop. Agents are instrumented with activation-based reward-hack scores, token-level entropy, and decision-context features. We find that adapters fine-tuned on \textit{School-of-Reward-Hacks} dataset can transfer reward-hack tendencies into agentic action selection, especially when the environment exposes proxy-reward affordances. However, mitigating such behavior cannot rely on activation dynamics alone. High reward-hack activation identifies a latent policy state, but does not necessarily imply an immediate exploit action. Across next-step prediction tasks, entropy and context-calibrated internal features improve risk estimation over reward-hack activation alone. Activation-direction steering further reduces proxy-exploit behavior in selected mixed-adapter regimes. Overall, our results support context-calibrated internal monitoring for agents: reward-hack activation identifies a latent policy state, while entropy and decision context help determine when that state becomes risky action.

</details>


### [88] [Closing the Loop on Latent Reasoning via Test-Time Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06252)
*Xiaopeng Yuan,Haibo Jin,Ye Yu,Peng Kuang,Lijun Yu,Yushun Dong,Haohan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReLAT is a self-supervised test-time training method that closes the open-loop problem in latent reasoning by ensuring latent states can reconstruct the original query. It improves accuracy across math, QA, and code benchmarks, notably raising AIME 2024 accuracy from 56.7% to 73.3% on Qwen3-8B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent reasoning reduces token overhead but operates in an open loop — intermediate states are not inspectable, making it impossible to verify whether they preserve original query constraints. This lack of an input-anchored fidelity check is the core problem addressed.

Method: ReLAT constructs a differentiable Question → Latent Thought → Question cycle. It applies query reconstruction loss through the latent state before answer generation, using the original query as the reference to anchor latent computation to the problem specification.

Result: ReLAT consistently outperforms single-model inference, text-based collaboration, open-loop latent collaboration, and alternative test-time training objectives across math, knowledge QA, and code benchmarks. On Qwen3-8B, AIME 2024 accuracy improves from 56.7% to 73.3% (16.6-point gain).

Conclusion: Closing the loop with query reconstruction is an effective self-supervised signal for improving latent reasoning fidelity, making opaque latent states reliably anchored to the original problem constraints.

Abstract: Recent work moves intermediate reasoning from natural-language traces into latent or cache-level representations to reduce token overhead and avoid a discrete communication bottleneck. However, this shift also removes a key advantage of textual reasoning: intermediate states are no longer inspectable, making it difficult to determine whether a latent state still preserves the constraints of the original query. As a result, latent reasoning typically operates in an open loop, where a latent state is produced and consumed without an input-anchored fidelity check. We propose ReLAT (Reconstruction-Guided Latent Reasoning At Test Time), a self-supervised test-time training method that closes this loop using the query itself as the reference. Our key observation is that if a latent state faithfully represents a query, the query should be recoverable from it; if the query cannot be recovered, the latent state has lost task-relevant information. ReLAT operationalizes this principle by constructing a differentiable Question -> Latent Thought -> Question cycle and optimizing query reconstruction loss through the latent thought before answer generation. This anchors opaque latent computation to the problem specification it is supposed to represent. Across mathematical reasoning, knowledge QA, and code generation benchmarks on the Qwen family, ReLAT consistently improves over single-model inference, text-based collaboration, open-loop latent collaboration, and alternative test-time training objectives. On Qwen3-8B, ReLAT raises AIME 2024 accuracy from 56.7% to 73.3%, a 16.6-point gain over the strongest open-loop latent baseline.

</details>


### [89] [RedKnot: Efficient Long-Context LLM Serving with Head-Aware KV Reuse and SegPagedAttention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06256)
*Yang Liu,ZhaoKai Luo,HuaYi Jin,ZhiYong Wang,RuoZhou He,BoYu Wang,Guanjie Chen,Junhao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RedKnot is a head-aware KV cache management system for LLM serving that decomposes the monolithic KV cache along KV heads, enabling position-independent reuse, prefix compression, hot/cold separation, and distributed management without model retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV cache has become the dominant bottleneck in LLM serving as input lengths grow, limiting GPU memory, concurrency, cache reuse, and scalability. Existing systems treat the KV cache as a monolithic homogeneous sequence, ignoring structured differences across KV heads in functional roles, attention distances, and runtime importance.

Method: RedKnot decomposes the KV cache along KV heads rather than treating it as a monolithic tensor, recognizing that different heads exhibit varying importance and effective attention ranges across serving scenarios. This head-level decomposition transforms the KV cache into a structured memory object.

Result: RedKnot uniformly supports position-independent KV reuse, prefix KV compression, hot/cold KV separation, and distributed KV placement while preserving output fidelity and improving resource efficiency, without requiring model retraining or fine-tuning.

Conclusion: RedKnot establishes a new foundation for AI infrastructure by transforming the KV cache from a monolithic, passive runtime artifact into a dynamic, model-aware runtime substrate for scalable LLM serving.

Abstract: As the input length of large language model (LLM) serving continues to grow, the KV cache has become a dominant bottleneck in AI infrastructure. It limits GPU memory capacity, serving concurrency, cache reuse, and distributed scalability. Several important problems, including position-independent KV cache, prefix KV cache compression, hot/cold KV cache separation, and distributed KV cache management, all depend on how the KV cache is represented and managed. However, existing serving systems largely rely on a monolithic KV cache abstraction, where the KV cache is treated as a homogeneous sequence of token-level memory blocks and managed with similar policies across attention heads and serving scenarios. We observe that KV cache utility is highly structured across KV heads: different heads exhibit different functional roles, attention distances, and runtime importance. Therefore, a full KV cache is not always necessary for every head, token range, or serving scenario.
  We present RedKnot, a head-aware KV cache management system for LLM serving. RedKnot breaks the conventional monolithic KV cache abstraction by decomposing the KV cache along KV heads, whose importance and effective attention ranges vary significantly across serving scenarios. This head-level decomposition turns the KV cache from a monolithic tensor abstraction into a structured memory object, enabling RedKnot to uniformly support position-independent KV reuse, prefix KV compression, hot/cold KV separation, and distributed KV placement while preserving output fidelity and improving resource efficiency, without requiring model retraining or fine-tuning. RedKnot establishes a new foundation for AI infrastructure by transforming the KV cache from a monolithic, passive runtime artifact into a dynamic, model-aware runtime substrate for scalable LLM serving.

</details>


### [90] [ToolChoiceConfusion: Causal Minimal Tool Filtering for Reliable LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06284)
*Rahul Suresh Babu,Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CMTF selects tools by causal sufficiency rather than semantic relevance, reducing visible tools from 100 to 1 per step and token usage by ~90% while maintaining task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-selection methods optimize for semantic relevance, which is insufficient because a tool may be related to a task yet unnecessary or premature at the current step, leading to wrong-tool calls, premature actions, and high token costs.

Method: CMTF is a training-free method that uses lightweight precondition-effect contracts to expose only the minimal causal next-step tool frontier needed to advance from the current state toward the user goal.

Result: On a benchmark with 102 tasks, 100 tools, 4 LLM backends, and 2448 runs, CMTF matches the strongest causal baseline in success rate while reducing visible tools from 100 to 1 per step and reducing token usage by ~90% compared to all-tools exposure.

Conclusion: Causal sufficiency, not just relevance, is the right criterion for tool selection in multi-step agentic tasks; CMTF is a lightweight, training-free method that achieves dramatic efficiency gains without sacrificing task success.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on external tools, but larger tool menus can reduce reliability and efficiency by increasing wrong-tool calls, premature actions, and token cost. Existing tool-selection methods often optimize semantic relevance, exposing tools whose names or descriptions match the user request. We argue that relevance is insufficient: a tool may be related to the task while still being unnecessary or premature at the current step.
  We propose Causal Minimal Tool Filtering (CMTF), a training-free method that selects tools by causal sufficiency. CMTF uses lightweight precondition-effect contracts to expose only the minimal next-step tool frontier needed to advance from the current state toward the user goal. Across multi-step tool-use tasks, we compare CMTF with all-tools exposure, keyword retrieval, state-aware filtering, and causal-path ablations, measuring task success, wrong-tool calls, premature actions, tool exposure, and token cost. In the main benchmark with 102 tasks, 100 tools, four LLM backends, and 2448 task-method-model runs, CMTF matches the strongest causal baseline in aggregate success while reducing visible tools from 100 to one per step and reducing token usage by about 90% relative to all-tools exposure.

</details>


### [91] [TRACE: A Temporal Conditional Estimation for Multimodal Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06285)
*Ziwen Kan,Yishuo Chen,Kecheng Li,Andrew Wen,Xiaomeng Wang,Liwei Wang,Jihao Duan,Song Wang,Hongfang Liu,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE introduces a conditional estimation paradigm for multimodal time series foundation models that systematically infers incomplete target modalities from available auxiliary modalities, outperforming prior fusion approaches under missingness and irregular sampling on healthcare and affective computing benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series foundation models in multimodal settings suffer from temporal misalignment and partial modality missingness, where existing naive imputation or masking strategies fail to capture cross-modal dependencies, leading to degraded representations.

Method: TRACE proposes a conditional estimation paradigm for multimodal TS-FM pipelines that systematically infers incomplete target modalities from available auxiliary modalities, handling missingness and irregular sampling without relying on naive imputation.

Result: TRACE consistently outperforms prior multimodal fusion approaches across MIMIC-IV, CMU-MOSI, and CMU-MOSEI benchmarks, demonstrating improved robustness to severe modality missingness and more reliable cross-modal representations.

Conclusion: Conditional estimation from available modalities provides a more robust and reliable approach for multimodal time series foundation models under realistic missingness and irregular sampling conditions.

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TS-FMs) aim to learn generalizable temporal representations that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks. In real-world multimodal settings, time series are frequently affected by temporal misalignment and partial modality missingness, where different modalities are observed at heterogeneous time scales or are partially absent. Existing approaches typically rely on naive imputation or masking strategies, which fail to account for cross-modal dependencies and often lead to misaligned or degraded representations. We propose TRACE, a conditional estimation paradigm for multimodal time series foundation model pipelines under missingness and irregular sampling, allowing incomplete target modalities to be systematically inferred from available auxiliary modalities. We evaluate TRACE on diverse multimodal benchmarks spanning healthcare and affective computing, including the MIMIC-IV clinical dataset and the CMU-MOSI and CMU-MOSEI benchmarks for multimodal sentiment analysis. Across a range of downstream prediction tasks and missing-modality settings, TRACE consistently outperforms prior multimodal fusion approaches, demonstrating improved robustness to severe modality missingness and more reliable cross-modal representations.

</details>


### [92] [Multi-ResNets for Subspace Preconditioning in Constrained Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06300)
*Merve Karakas,Christopher J. Williams,Emmanuel O. Balogun,Sadegh Sadeghi Tabas,Christian Brown,Nikhil Rao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MResOpt is a staged residual neural network architecture for constrained optimization that decomposes constraint satisfaction by priority, behaves like sequential Gaussian Process regression in infinite width, and achieves lower high-priority violations on AC optimal power flow problems compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address constrained optimization problems where constraint satisfaction needs to be prioritized (e.g., engineering systems where some constraints are more critical than others), and existing neural network approaches do not effectively leverage ordinal structure among constraints.

Method: A staged residual neural network architecture with intermediate re-completion and stage-aware losses, fitting within predict-complete-correct pipelines. It decomposes constraint satisfaction by priority and uses domain-informed constraint ordering. Theoretical analysis shows equivalence to sequential Gaussian Process regression under infinite-width regime.

Result: On synthetic QP, QCQP, and SOCP benchmarks, the staged architecture improves high-priority constraint satisfaction in both convex and non-convex settings. On AC optimal power flow with physics-motivated ordering, MResOpt achieves substantially lower high-priority violation than reprojected baselines while remaining computationally efficient.

Conclusion: The staged residual architecture enables learned division of labor that keeps iterates on the equality manifold, demonstrating that domain-informed ordered constraint satisfaction is effective for practical constrained optimization problems.

Abstract: We propose MResOpt, a staged residual neural network architecture for constrained optimization problems. Our architecture fits within predict-complete-correct pipelines and decomposes constraint satisfaction by priority through intermediate re-completion and stage-aware losses. The framework enables domain-informed ordered constraint satisfaction which allows the network to utilize ordinal structure when present. Under an idealized infinite-width regime, we show that our design behaves as sequential Gaussian Process regression. On synthetic QP, QCQP, and SOCP benchmarks, the staged architecture improves high-priority constraint satisfaction across convex and non-convex settings. On line-flow-constrained AC optimal power flow, we introduce a physics-motivated constraint ordering and show that MResOpt supports a learned division of labor that keeps iterates on the equality manifold, achieving substantially lower high-priority violation than reprojected baselines while remaining computationally efficient.

</details>


### [93] [AIS-Based Vessel Trajectory Prediction Using Memory-Augmented Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06311)
*Wonmo Koo,Sanha Chang,Heeyoung Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper empirically investigates memory-augmented neural networks for vessel trajectory prediction using AIS data, showing consistent performance improvements over deep learning baselines without external memory on two real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented neural networks have shown promise in pedestrian and road-vehicle trajectory prediction but remain underexplored for vessel trajectory prediction, which is critical for maritime safety and efficiency.

Method: Empirical investigation of memory-based trajectory prediction using AIS data, comparing memory-augmented networks against standard deep learning baselines without external memory on datasets from the Gulf of Mexico and New York Bight.

Result: Consistent and substantial performance gains over a range of deep learning baselines that do not incorporate an external memory.

Conclusion: Memory-augmented neural networks are effective for vessel trajectory prediction, outperforming conventional deep learning approaches on real-world AIS data.

Abstract: Accurate vessel trajectory prediction is essential for safe and efficient maritime operations, enabling collision avoidance and supporting route optimization. Although memory-augmented neural networks have recently shown strong performance in pedestrian and road-vehicle trajectory prediction by selectively retrieving relevant information from an external memory, their potential for vessel trajectory prediction remains underexplored. This paper presents an empirical investigation of memory-based trajectory prediction using Automatic Identification System (AIS) data. Experiments on data from the Gulf of Mexico and the New York Bight demonstrate consistent and substantial performance gains over a range of deep learning baselines that do not incorporate an external memory.

</details>


### [94] [LLM Self-Recognition: Steering and Retrieving Activation Signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06315)
*Thibaud Ardoin,Jonas Schäfer,Gerhard Wunder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can self-recognize their outputs via internal activation signals; steering residual streams with random sparse vectors creates a detectable fingerprint achieving >98% attribution accuracy without quality loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI-generated content proliferates, there is a need for reliable attribution methods to identify which LLM produced a given text, without relying on external watermarking or degrading output quality.

Method: Steering the internal residual stream during generation with a random sparse vector creates a detectable fingerprint; a detector LLM recovers this signal from its activations to attribute text to a specific model.

Result: Achieves over 98% accuracy across multiple detection settings while preserving the quality of generated text.

Conclusion: Activation spaces contain exploitable structure for encoding signals without semantic interference, offering a practical alternative to traditional detectors by leveraging the model's natural representation for attribution.

Abstract: Recent advances in interpretability suggest that large language models (LLMs) implicitly encode signals in their generated text that enable self-recognition of their outputs. We demonstrate that this capability is reliable, even in low-entropy scenarios, and that it can be amplified through targeted intervention. By steering the internal residual stream during generation with a random sparse vector, we create a detectable fingerprint that enables attribution of a given text to a specific LLM. This signal is recoverable from the activations of an LLM used as a detector, achieving over 98% accuracy across multiple detection settings while preserving the quality of generated text. As AI-generated content proliferates, this approach offers a practical alternative to traditional detectors by leveraging the model's natural representation structure for attribution rather than embedding a signal externally. Our contributions include: (i) establishing reliable self-recognition capabilities in LLMs, (ii) a simple steering mechanism enabling multi-LLM identification with no quality degradation, (iii) demonstrating that activation spaces contain exploitable structure for encoding signals without semantic interference.

</details>


### [95] [DragOn: A Benchmark and Dataset for Drag-Based GUI Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06322)
*Nathan Bout,Maxime Langevin,Ronan Riochet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DragOn is a drag grounding benchmark and training dataset (286K screenshots, 3.5M tasks) covering four drag-based interaction domains. It evaluates SOTA models and shows fine-tuning on this data can improve performance on downstream computer-use tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GUI agents struggle with drag-based interactions (drag-and-drop, swipe, highlight) because existing datasets for drag grounding are an order of magnitude smaller than click-grounding datasets, limiting progress on complex drag-based tasks.

Method: DragOn dataset includes 286K training screenshots and 3.5M training tasks across four domains (text highlighting, cell selection, element resizing, slider manipulation), plus a 2,000-example evaluation suite. The authors evaluate proprietary (GPT, Claude) and open-weight (Qwen, Kimi, Holo) models, and fine-tune a Qwen VLM on the training data.

Result: The fine-tuned Qwen VLM on DragOn data shows improved performance on downstream computer-use tasks compared to baselines, suggesting the dataset effectively addresses the drag grounding gap.

Conclusion: DragOn provides a much-needed large-scale drag grounding resource, and incorporating it into model training can enhance GUI agent capabilities for complex drag-based interactions.

Abstract: GUI agents - vision-based models that control desktops, web browsers, and mobile devices through graphical user interfaces - promise to automate a wide range of digital tasks. While million-scale datasets have enabled substantial progress on click-grounding, drag grounding (e.g. drag-and-drop, swipe, highlight) data remains an order of magnitude smaller and current models fall short on complex drag-based interactions. We introduce DragOn, a drag grounding benchmark and training dataset covering four domains: text highlighting, cell selection, element resizing and slider manipulation. The dataset comprises 286K training screenshots and 3.5M training tasks, plus a 2000-example held-out evaluation suite. We evaluate proprietary (GPT, Claude) and open-weight (Qwen, Kimi, Holo) models, as well as a Qwen VLM fine-tuned on our training data. Results suggest that our dataset could improve performance of state-of-the-art models on downstream computer-use tasks.

</details>


### [96] [TokenMizer: Graph-Structured Session Memory for Long-Horizon LLM Context Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06337)
*Shweta Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TokenMizer is an open-source proxy system that models LLM session history as a typed knowledge graph (14 node types, 7 edge types) with hybrid extraction, three-tier checkpointing, and 8-layer compression, producing resume blocks averaging 78 tokens (2x smaller than baselines) while achieving higher decision recall (+9-17 pp).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM deployments for long-horizon tasks face the constraint that context windows are finite while work sessions are not; when history exceeds Maximum Effective Context Window (MECW), critical structured information is silently discarded, and existing mitigations treat history as flat text, destroying relational structure needed for session resumability.

Method: TokenMizer models session history as a typed knowledge graph (14 node types, 7 edge types) using a hybrid extraction pipeline that populates the graph incrementally. It employs a three-tier checkpoint system to serialize the graph into compact resume blocks, an 8-layer compression pipeline to reduce context overhead, and a semantic cache for repeated-query latency reduction.

Result: On 21 sessions across 5 domains, TokenMizer produces resume blocks averaging 78 tokens (range: 42-124), 2x smaller than baselines (159-170 tokens), with higher decision recall (+9-17 pp). Mean task recall 51.0%, decision recall 46.6%, file recall 58.7%. Fuzzy label matching contributed +33 pp to task recall. Heuristic compression achieved 47.3% token reduction with zero external dependencies.

Conclusion: TokenMizer provides a queryable alternative to text-retention baselines at half the token cost, preserving not just that a technology was mentioned (as baselines do) but the rationale behind decisions, while outperforming baselines across recall metrics.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) deployments for long-horizon tasks face a fundamental constraint: context windows are finite while productive work sessions are not. When history exceeds the Maximum Effective Context Window (MECW), critical structured information - architectural decisions, task transitions, file histories - is silently discarded. Existing mitigations treat history as flat text, destroying the relational structure that makes sessions resumable. We present TokenMizer, an open-source proxy system that models LLM session history as a typed knowledge graph. The schema defines 14 node types and 7 edge types. A hybrid extraction pipeline populates the graph incrementally, while a three-tier checkpoint system serializes it into compact resume blocks. An 8-layer compression pipeline reduces context overhead, and a semantic cache reduces repeated-query latency. Evaluated on a controlled benchmark of 21 sessions spanning 5 domains, TokenMizer demonstrates significant token economy. It produces resume blocks averaging 78 tokens (range: 42-124) - 2x smaller than evaluated baselines (159-170 tokens) - while achieving higher decision recall (+9-17 percentage points). Crucially, baselines only preserve that a technology was mentioned; TokenMizer preserves the rationale. Across all sessions, TokenMizer achieves mean task recall 51.0%, decision recall 46.6%, and file recall 58.7%. Variance reflects domain heterogeneity: explicit imperative phrasing (software engineering) scores higher than implicit reasoning (research). Ablation studies show fuzzy label matching is the dominant improvement factor (+33 pp task recall). The heuristic compression achieves 47.3% token reduction with zero external dependencies. TokenMizer provides a queryable alternative to text-retention baselines at half the token cost.

</details>


### [97] [Boosting Brain-to-Image Decoding with TRIBE v2 Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06345)
*Yohann Benchetrit,Marlène Careil,Simon Dahan,Hubert Banville,Stéphane d'Ascoli,Jean-Rémi King*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether augmenting small fMRI datasets with synthetic data from TRIBE v2 (a large pretrained encoding model) can improve brain decoding performance in low-data regimes, achieving up to 68% improvement in Top-10 image-retrieval accuracy, with synthetic-only training even enabling above-chance zero-shot decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brain decoding is hindered by limited labeled neural data; the authors investigate whether synthetic fMRI data augmentation can overcome this low-data regime challenge.

Method: They use TRIBE v2, a large encoding model pretrained on 1000+ hours of fMRI responses to video, audio, and language, to generate synthetic fMRI data. Systematic grids evaluate image decoder performance across varying amounts of synthetic data on two datasets (7T NSD and 3T BOLD5000).

Result: Up to 68% improvement in Top-10 image-retrieval accuracy over real-data-only training; optimal synthetic-to-real data ratios depend on the data source; synthetic-only training yields above-chance performance in some settings.

Conclusion: Large-scale models of fMRI responses (sight, sound, language) can serve as a foundation to improve data efficiency for image decoding, with potential for zero-shot decoding.

Abstract: Brain decoding is limited by the availability of labeled neural data, and remains challenging in low-data regimes. To address this issue, we investigate whether and when brain decoding can be boosted by augmenting small fMRI datasets with synthetic data generated by a pretrained model of fMRI responses to stimuli. We use TRIBE v2, a large encoding model pretrained on more than 1000 hours of fMRI responses to video, audio and language. For each dataset, we evaluate systematic grids that show how the performance of image decoders varies with the amount of synthetic data used for training. Our results, based on two datasets (the 7T fMRI Natural Scenes Dataset and 3T fMRI BOLD5000), show up to 68% improvement in Top-10 image-retrieval accuracy compared to decoders trained only on real data. Importantly, the proportion of augmented data required to reach a given image decoding performance needs to be adjusted depending on the data source. Surprisingly, image decoders trained exclusively on synthetic fMRI can perform above chance in some settings, suggesting that TRIBE v2 can support zero-shot brain-to-image decoding. Together, these results show how large-scale models of the fMRI responses to sight, sound and language may provide a foundation to improve the data efficiency for image decoding.

</details>


### [98] [Where Should Knowledge Enter? A Layered Framework for Knowledge Infusion in Multimodal Iterative Generative Mo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06356)
*Renjith Prasad,Chathurangi Shyalika,Anushka Pawar,Amit Sheth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Knowledge infusion in iterative generative models is an intervention-layer problem with four layers (surface, trajectory, latent, parametric). Multi-layer composition reduces knowledge-violating outputs by 70.97% in diffusion models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for incorporating knowledge into multimodal generative models are categorized by technique rather than by which component of the generative process they modify, hindering systematic understanding and composition of complementary approaches.

Method: Proposes a framework identifying four structurally distinct intervention layers in iterative generative models: surface (input/output boundary), trajectory (transition function), latent (intermediate state), and parametric (model parameters). Instantiates this in diffusion models and validates via a controlled safety-alignment experiment with multimodal KG and two diffusion backbones, implementing surface (input+output), trajectory-latent (mid-generation) layers cumulatively.

Result: Each additional intervention layer addresses failure classes unreachable by prior layers; multi-layer composition reduces knowledge-violating outputs by 70.97% compared to vanilla generation, empirically confirming the framework's complementarity prediction.

Conclusion: The intervention-layer framework provides a principled taxonomy for knowledge infusion in generative models, and multi-layer composition yields complementary benefits that single-layer approaches cannot achieve.

Abstract: Multimodal generative models produce fluent outputs but remain unreliable when generation must respect structured, domain-specific, or safety-critical knowledge. Existing methods incorporate knowledge through mechanisms such as prompt augmentation, guidance, latent editing, or fine-tuning, yet they are typically categorized by technique rather than by the component of the generative process they modify. We argue that knowledge infusion in iterative generative models is fundamentally anintervention-layer problem. Since thegenerative process unfolds as a trajectory of internal states, knowledge can act on four structurally distinct components of this process: the input/output boundary, the transition function, the intermediate state, and the model parameters. This maps to four intervention layers: surface, trajectory, latent, and parametric infusion. We instantiate the framework in diffusion models, map representative methods to all four layers, and derive design principles for multi-layer composition. In a controlled safety-alignment experiment using a multimodal knowledge graph with two diffusion backbones, we implement three of the four layers cumulatively, surface (input-side and output-side) and trajectory--latent (mid-generation). We show empirically that each additional layer addresses failure classes that prior layers cannot reach, reducing knowledge-violating outputs by 70.97% compared to vanilla generation and empirically confirming the framework's complementarity prediction.

</details>


### [99] [An Infectious Disease Spread Simulation Based on Large Language Model Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06360)
*Yonchanok Khaokaew,Ruochen Kong,Andreas Zufle,Hao Xue,Taylor Anderson,Chandini Raina MacIntyre,Matthew Scotch,Flora D. Salim,David J Heslop*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a spatially grounded, agent-based simulation framework that uses LLMs to model individual decisions about self-reporting influenza-like illness, integrating census-based synthetic populations in US cities. Results show income and education are the main drivers of reporting variation, with smaller effects from geography, LLM choice, and message framing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modelling individual decision-making during infectious disease outbreaks is crucial for understanding behavioural dynamics and informing effective public health interventions. Prior work has shown LLMs can simulate realistic human behaviour, but lacked spatial grounding.

Method: A spatially grounded, agent-based simulation framework integrating LLM-generated decisions about self-reported influenza-like illness into a census-based synthetic population. Agents are assigned to spatial units within cities using real-world census data. Three decision scenarios are compared: independent reasoning, household influence, and message framing, simulated in San Francisco and Atlanta.

Result: Income and education are the dominant drivers of reporting rate variation, with smaller but consistent effects from geography, LLM model choice, and message framing.

Conclusion: The framework generates synthetic data capturing both social and geographic heterogeneity, supporting spatial epidemiological modelling and bias-aware behavioural analysis.

Abstract: Modelling individual decision-making during infectious disease outbreaks is crucial for understanding behavioural dynamics and informing effective public health interventions. Prior work has shown that large language models can simulate realistic human behaviour by generating agent decisions based on demographic prompts and situational context. We build on this foundation with a spatially grounded, agent-based simulation framework that integrates LLM-generated decisions about self-reported influenza-like illness into a census-based synthetic population of agents. Location is treated as a central feature: agents are assigned to spatial units within cities, capturing the spatial distributions of different demographic groups using real-world census data and enabling geographically diverse behavioural modelling. We implement and compare three decision scenarios, independent reasoning, household influence, and message framing, and simulate self-reporting outcomes in San Francisco and Atlanta. Results reveal that income and education are the dominant drivers of reporting rate variation, with smaller but consistent effects from geography, LLM model choice, and message framing. Our framework generates synthetic data that captures both social and geographic heterogeneity, supporting spatial epidemiological modelling and bias-aware behavioural analysis.

</details>


### [100] [Rethinking Infrastructure Inspection as Image Difference Classification: A Traffic Sign Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06375)
*Ching Yau Fergus Mok,Lavindra de Silva,Varun Kumar Reja,Ioannis Brilakis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes image difference classification (IDC) as an alternative to defect detection for digital twin road infrastructure inspection, reducing reliance on annotated data. Using instruction-based classifiers with reference image comparison, the approach outperforms encoder-based methods in low-resource traffic sign inspection tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital twins enable digitalization of road infrastructure inspection but are hindered by limited annotated data. The authors aim to reduce data reliance in defect detection through a relational task reformulation.

Method: Defect detection is reformulated as image difference classification (IDC), leveraging the relational nature of continuous asset condition monitoring. Evaluated on a newly-curated high-quality traffic sign dataset with different IDC classifiers (instruction-based vs. encoder-based).

Result: Instruction-based classifiers outperform encoder-based ones, and performance gains are achieved through comparison with reference images.

Conclusion: IDC is an effective task modeling approach for overcoming data constraints in infrastructure inspection and digital twin asset condition updating.

Abstract: Digital twins (DTs) allow the digitalization of road infrastructure inspection, though this is hindered by limited annotated data. This work exploits the relational nature of continuous asset condition monitoring to reformulate image-based defect detection as image difference classification (IDC) to reduce data reliance. This was evaluated in a case study on low-resource traffic sign inspection with different IDC classifiers using a newly-curated, high quality dataset. Results indicate that the instruction-based classifier outperforms encoder-based ones and gains from comparison with reference images. This shows that IDC can be an effective task modeling for tackling data constraints in infrastructure inspection and DT asset condition updating.

</details>


### [101] [Humans' ALMANAC: A Human Collaboration Dataset of Action-Level Mental Model Annotations for Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06388)
*Jiaju Chen,Yuxuan Lu,Jiayi Su,Chaoran Chen,Songlin Xiao,Zheng Zhang,Yun Wang,Yunyao Li,Jian Zhao,Tongshuang Wu,Toby Jia-Jun Li,Dakuo Wang,Bingsheng Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ALMANAC is a dataset of 2,987 action-level mental model annotations for agent collaboration, built from the Map Task, enabling evaluation of LLMs' ability to simulate human collaborative behaviors and infer mental models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents lack collaborative competence because they are optimized for task completion rather than process-level collaboration, and there is no authentic human collaboration data with action-level mental model annotations to guide such development.

Method: Built from the Map Task (a dyadic routing task), ALMANAC contains 2,987 collaboration actions annotated with theory-informed mental models recording self-reasoning, perceived partner intent, and perceived team goal. Six LLMs were benchmarked on predicting next-turn behavior and mental models.

Result: The dataset enables evaluation of LLMs' ability to simulate human collaborative behaviors and infer their underlying mental models.

Conclusion: ALMANAC demonstrates utility in benchmarking LLMs on collaborative competence, highlighting the need for process-level mental model alignment in LLM agents.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLM agents have enabled complex cognitive capabilities, such as multi-step reasoning, planning, and tool use, that increasingly position these agents as human collaborators. Effective collaboration, however, requires collaborators to continuously maintain and align mental models of their own reasoning,partners' intentions, and shared goals during the collaborative process. Today's agents rarely develop such capabilities since they are primarily optimized for task completion, and the community lacks authentic human collaboration data with action-level mental model annotations that could guide agents toward process-level collaborative competence. To bridge this gap, we present ALMANAC, a dataset of Action-Level Mental model ANnotations for Agent Collaboration built from the Map Task, a classic dyadic routing task from social science. ALMANAC contains 2,987 collaboration actions, each paired with theory-informed mental model annotations that record the participants' self-reasoning, perceived partner intent, and perceived team goal. We benchmark six LLMs on predicting humans' next-turn behavior and mental models. Our results demonstrate ALMANAC's utility in evaluating models' ability to simulate human collaborative behaviors and infer their underlying mental models.

</details>


### [102] [Risk Assessment of Autonomous Driving: Integrating Technical Failures, Ethical Dilemmas, and Policy Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06396)
*Boyi Chen,Shengqin Chu,Zicheng Wang,Brian Baetz,Zhen Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes autonomous driving risks across technology, ethics, and regulation using NHTSA crash data, CA DMV disengagement reports, MIT Moral Machines dataset, and a five-jurisdiction regulatory comparison. It finds perception/classification errors as dominant technical failures, varied ethical frameworks, and inconsistent regulations, recommending an adaptive governance approach combining engineering standards, ethics, and institutional oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous driving promises to reduce human-error-related accidents but introduces new risks. There is a need to systematically evaluate these risks from technical, ethical, and regulatory perspectives using real-world data.

Method: The study uses four data sources: NHTSA public crash data, CA DMV disengagement reports, MIT Moral Machines dataset, and a comparative regulatory analysis across five jurisdictions.

Result: Perception and classification errors are the primary technical failure modes accounting for a large proportion of reported accidents. Different ethical frameworks exist for AV decision-making, and inconsistent regulations across jurisdictions increase uncertainty for widespread deployment.

Conclusion: Technology, ethics, and regulation are closely interrelated problems that must be addressed collectively. The paper recommends an adaptive and cooperative governance approach integrating engineering standards, ethical discussion, and institutional supervision.

Abstract: Autonomous driving technology has the potential to reduce the large number of road traffic accidents caused by human error each year, but it also brings new types of risks that need to be evaluated from the aspects of technology, ethics and regulations. Based on public crash data from the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA), disengagement reports from the California Department of Motor Vehicles (DMV), the MIT Moral Machines dataset, and a comparative regulatory analysis of five jurisdictions, we have found that the main types of technical failure modes are perception and classification errors. These account for a relatively large proportion of the reported accidents, and it can be concluded that there are different ethical frameworks for autonomous vehicle decision-making, and inconsistent regulations in different areas increase the uncertainty of widespread application. Generally speaking, the problems of technology, ethics and regulation are closely related and need to be solved together. Therefore, this paper recommends a more adaptive and cooperative governance approach that combines engineering standards, ethical discussion, and institutional supervision.

</details>


### [103] [Unsupervised Skill Discovery for Agentic Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06416)
*Zhisong Qiu,Kangqi Song,Shengwei Tang,Shuofei Qiao,Lei Liang,Huajun Chen,Shumin Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DataCOPE is an unsupervised framework that discovers reusable data-analysis skills by using verifier signals from exploration trajectories, improving performance by 9.71% on report-style and 32.30% on reasoning-style tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time skill augmentation is lightweight but discovering effective skills for data analysis is challenging due to expensive supervision and varying success criteria across analytical formats.

Method: DataCOPE uses an unsupervised verifier-guided skill discovery framework with three components: a Data-Analytic Agent for trajectory generation, an Unsupervised Verifier for signal extraction (Adaptive Checklist Verifier for reports, Answer Agreement Verifier for reasoning), and a Skill Manager for contrastive skill distillation.

Result: DataCOPE consistently improves held-out performance over baselines, with mean score improvements of 9.71% on report-style (Deep Data Research) and 32.30% on reasoning-style (DABStep) tasks across four model settings.

Conclusion: Unsupervised verifier-guided skill discovery effectively identifies reusable data-analysis skills from unlabeled exploration, significantly improving agent performance across both report-style and reasoning-style analytical formats.

Abstract: Inference-time skill augmentation provides a lightweight way to improve data-analytic agents by injecting reusable procedural knowledge without updating model parameters. However, discovering effective skills for data analysis remains challenging, as reliable supervision is expensive and success criteria vary across analytical formats. This raises the key question of how to discover reusable data-analysis skills from unlabeled exploration alone. We propose DataCOPE, an unsupervised verifier-guided skill discovery framework for data-analytic agents. DataCOPE derives verifier signals from the exploration trajectories and uses them to characterize relative quality or aggreement among trajectories. It iteratively coordinates a Data-Analytic Agent for trajectory generation, an Unsupervised Verifier for signal extraction, and a Skill Manager for contrastive skill distillation. For report-style analysis, we instantiate the verifier as an Adaptive Checklist Verifier that derives task-specific criteria, scores reports by verifiable coverage, and iteratively refines the checklist. For reasoning-style analysis, we instantiate it as an Answer Agreement Verifier that groups trajectories by answer agreement and uses self-consistency as an auxiliary signal. We evaluate DataCOPE on report-style analysis from Deep Data Research and reasoning-style analysis from DABStep. Across both settings, DataCOPE consistently improves held-out performance over baselines. Averaged across four model settings, DataCOPE improves the mean score by 9.71% and 32.30% on report-style and reasoning-style tasks respectively.

</details>


### [104] [Agent Memory: Characterization and System Implications of Stateful Long-Horizon Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06448)
*Yasmine Omri,Ziyu Gan,Zachary Broveak,Robin Geens,Zexue He,Alex Pentland,Marian Verhelst,Tsachy Weissman,Thierry Tambe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First systems characterization of LLM agent memory, proposing a taxonomy, profiling harness, and 10 design recommendations after analyzing 10 systems across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are deployed on long-horizon tasks requiring persistent memory across sessions, yet the system-level behavior of existing agent memory systems remains uncharacterized, hindering informed design choices.

Method: (1) A system-oriented taxonomy classifying agent memory along four axes; (2) a phase-aware profiling harness that attributes costs to construction, retrieval, and generation phases; (3) empirical characterization of 10 representative systems across two benchmark suites.

Result: Uncovered how design choices shift cost across write and read paths; derived 10 system recommendations covering construction scheduling, capability floors, amortization via query volume, freshness-latency tradeoffs, and fleet-scale management.

Conclusion: Agent memory systems have distinct system-level cost profiles that can be optimized via structured recommendations; the paper provides the first systematic framework for understanding and improving these systems.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly deployed on long-horizon tasks requiring sustained reasoning over extended interaction histories. Realizing this at scale requires agents to persistently store, retrieve, and update their own memory across sessions. A rich ecosystem of agent memory systems has emerged spanning flat retrieval, LLM-mediated extraction, consolidating fact stores, and agentic control flows. Yet, their system-level behavior remains uncharacterized. We present the first systems characterization of agent memory. First, we introduce a system-oriented taxonomy classifying agent memory systems along four axes. Second, we build a phase-aware profiling harness attributing cost to construction, retrieval, and generation. Third, we characterize ten representative systems across two benchmark suites, uncovering how design choices shift cost across the write and read paths. Finally, we derive 10 system recommendations covering construction scheduling, capability floors, amortization via query volume, freshness-latency tradeoffs, and fleet-scale management.

</details>


### [105] [Vortex: Efficient and Programmable Sparse Attention Serving for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06453)
*Zhuoming Chen,Xinrui Zhong,Qilong Feng,Ranajoy Sadhukhan,Yang Zhou,Michael Qizhe Shieh,Zhihao Jia,Beidi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Vortex is a system with a Python-embedded frontend and page-centric tensor abstraction that enables rapid prototyping, deployment, and evaluation of sparse attention algorithms for LLMs, achieving up to 3.46× throughput improvement over full attention via AI agent-generated algorithms and up to 4.7× on MLA-based models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying and evaluating new sparse attention algorithms at scale remains highly engineering-intensive, slowing both human researchers and AI agents in exploring sparse attention designs.

Method: Vortex combines a Python-embedded frontend language with a page-centric tensor abstraction for expressing sparse attention algorithms, paired with an efficient backend integrated into modern LLM serving stacks.
