Result: AI agents using Vortex generate algorithms reaching up to 3.46× higher throughput than full attention; Vortex achieves up to 4.7× throughput on MLA-based GLM-4.7-Flash and 1.37× on MiniMax-M2.7 (229B parameters) on NVIDIA B200 GPUs.

Conclusion: Vortex substantially accelerates the design and iteration of sparse attention algorithms, effectively translating theoretical efficiency gains into real-world throughput improvements.

Abstract: Sparse attention is becoming increasingly important for serving large language models (LLMs) as generation lengths continue to grow. However, deploying and evaluating new sparse attention algorithms at scale remains highly engineering-intensive, slowing both human researchers and AI agents in exploring the sparse attention design. To address this challenge, we present Vortex, a system that combines a Python-embedded frontend language atop a page-centric tensor abstraction for expressing a broad range of sparse attention algorithms, with an efficient backend tightly integrated into modern LLM serving stacks. Vortex enables rapid prototyping, deployment, and evaluation of sparse attention algorithms, effectively translating their theoretical efficiency gains into real-world throughput improvements. As a result, Vortex substantially accelerates the design and iteration of sparse attention algorithms. First, AI agents use Vortex to automatically generate and refine diverse algorithms, the best reaching up to $3.46\times$ higher throughput than full attention while preserving accuracy. Second, Vortex extends sparse attention to emerging architectures and very large models that are otherwise hard to experiment with, reaching up to $4.7\times$ higher throughput on the MLA-based GLM-4.7-Flash and $1.37\times$ on the 229B-parameter MiniMax-M2.7 on NVIDIA B200 GPUs.

</details>


### [106] [Benchmark Everything Everywhere All at Once](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06462)
*Shiyun Xiong,Dongming Wu,Peiwen Sun,Yuang Ai,Bokang Yang,Wencheng Han,Xiao-Hui Li,Xiangyu Yue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark Agent is a fully autonomous system for building LLM/MLLM benchmarks, generating 15 diverse benchmark datasets with minimal human involvement, and revealing that current models struggle with domain-specific reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional benchmark construction is labor-intensive, hard to reuse, unsustainable, and quickly reaches performance saturation after release, making it difficult to discriminate among state-of-the-art models.

Method: The paper proposes Benchmark Agent, an autonomous agentic system that orchestrates the full benchmark construction pipeline including user query analysis, subtask design, data annotation, and quality control.

Result: Implemented 15 representative benchmarks across text understanding, multimodal understanding, and domain-specific reasoning. Evaluations via human evaluation, LLM-as-a-judge, and consistency checks show high-quality benchmark samples with minimal human involvement. Continual evaluation reveals current models struggle with certain domain-specific reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Benchmark Agent enables sustainable, scalable, and rapidly evolving benchmarks that can avoid saturation issues and better discriminate model capabilities, benefiting the research community.

Abstract: Benchmarks are fundamental for evaluating and advancing LLMs and MLLMs by providing standardized and explicit measures of performance. However, their construction is labor-intensive and hard to reuse, raising concerns about sustainability and scalability. Moreover, existing benchmarks often quickly reach performance saturation after their release, resulting in insufficient discrimination among state-of-the-art models. To address these challenges, we introduce Benchmark Agent, a fully autonomous agentic system designed for benchmark building. Our framework orchestrates the complete benchmark construction pipeline, from user query analysis and subtask design to data annotation and quality control. To assess Benchmark Agent, we implement it to produce 15 representative benchmarks, spanning diverse evaluation scenarios, including text understanding, multimodal understanding, and domain-specific reasoning. Extensive experiments, including human evaluation, LLM-as-a-judge assessment, and consistency checks, demonstrate Benchmark Agent can generate high-quality benchmark samples with minimal human involvement. More importantly, through continual evaluation, we observe several insightful findings, including that current models struggle with certain domain-specific reasoning tasks. We believe that rapidly evolving benchmarks can contribute significantly to the research community. The preview and code will be publicly available at the demo page and code repository.

</details>


### [107] [Goedel-Architect: Streamlining Formal Theorem Proving with Blueprint Generation and Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06468)
*Jui-Hui Chung,Ziyang Cai,Zihao Li,Qishuo Yin,Rohit Agarwal,Simon Park,Rodrigo Porto,Narutatsu Ri,Ziran Yang,Shange Tang,Xingyu Dang,Hongzhou Lin,Mengdi Wang,Danqi Chen,Chi Jin,Liam H Fowl,Sanjeev Arora*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Goedel-Architect is an agentic framework for formal theorem proving in Lean 4 that uses blueprint generation and refinement to achieve state-of-the-art results (99.2% on MiniF2F-test, 75.6% on PutnamBench) with open-weight models at up to 500x lower cost than comparable pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for formal theorem proving often rely on recursive lemma decomposition, which can inefficiently loop on dead-end strategies. The authors aim to overcome this by introducing a blueprint-based approach that uses a dependency graph of definitions and lemmas, enabling parallel proof attempts and global refinement from failures.

Method: Goedel-Architect first generates a blueprint (dependency graph of formally stated lemmas/definitions) optionally guided by a natural language proof. A tool-equipped Lean prover then attempts to prove each open lemma in parallel using relevant dependencies. Failed lemmas drive refinement of the global blueprint in an iterative loop.

Result: With DeepSeek-V4-Flash, achieves 99.2% pass@1 on MiniF2F-test and 75.6% on PutnamBench. With natural-language proof seeding, reaches 100% on MiniF2F-test, 88.8% on PutnamBench, solves 4/6 IMO 2025, 11/12 Putnam 2025, and 3/6 USAMO 2026. State-of-the-art for open-source at up to 500x lower cost.

Conclusion: The blueprint generation and refinement strategy is highly effective for formal theorem proving, outperforming recursive decomposition methods and achieving strong results with open-weight models at dramatically reduced cost.

Abstract: We introduce Goedel-Architect, an agentic framework for formal theorem proving in Lean 4 centered on blueprint generation and refinement. A blueprint is a dependency graph of definitions and lemmas that builds up to the main theorem. First, Goedel-Architect generates a blueprint of formally stated definitions and lemmas, along with declared dependencies. This blueprint is optionally guided by a natural language proof. Then, a tool-equipped Lean prover component closes each open lemma node in parallel using relevant dependencies. Failed lemmas in turn drive refinement of the global blueprint. This strategy contrasts with other mainstream approaches which use recursive lemma decomposition, and can inefficiently loop on dead-end strategies. Using the open-weight DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B-A13B) as the backbone, Goedel-Architect attains 99.2% pass@1 on MiniF2F-test and 75.6% pass@1 on PutnamBench. With an optional natural-language proof seeding the initial blueprint on the harder problems, we additionally close the remaining two MiniF2F-test problems (reaching 100%), lift PutnamBench to 88.8% (597/672), and solve 4/6 on IMO 2025, 11/12 on Putnam 2025, and 3/6 on USAMO 2026. This represents state-of-the-art performance for an open-source pipeline at a price point up to 500x less than comparable open-source pipelines.

</details>


### [108] [MLEvolve: A Self-Evolving Framework for Automated Machine Learning Algorithm Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06473)
*Shangheng Du,Xiangchao Yan,Jinxin Shi,Zongsheng Cao,Shiyang Feng,Zichen Liang,Boyuan Sun,Tianshuo Peng,Yifan Zhou,Xin Li,Jie Zhou,Liang He,Bo Zhang,Lei Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MLEvolve is an LLM-based self-evolving multi-agent framework for long-horizon machine learning algorithm discovery. It introduces Progressive MCGS for graph-based tree search with cross-branch information flow, Retrospective Memory for experience accumulation and reuse, and decoupled strategic planning from code generation. It achieves SOTA on MLE-Bench and outperforms specialized methods like AlphaEvolve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLE agents suffer from inter-branch information isolation, memoryless search, and lack of hierarchical control, which hinder sustained self-evolution and long-horizon optimization in tasks like scientific discovery and ML engineering.

Method: (1) Progressive MCGS: extends tree search with graph-based reference edges enabling cross-branch information flow, using an entropy-inspired schedule to shift from exploration to exploitation. (2) Retrospective Memory: combines a cold-start domain knowledge base with dynamic global memory for task-specific experience retrieval and reuse. (3) Decoupled strategic planning from code generation with adaptive coding modes for stable long-horizon iteration.

Result: SOTA on MLE-Bench across multiple dimensions including average medal rate and valid submission rate under a 12-hour budget (half standard runtime). Also outperforms AlphaEvolve on mathematical algorithm optimization tasks.

Conclusion: MLEvolve effectively addresses key limitations of MLE agents through cross-branch information flow, memory-augmented evolution, and hierarchical control, demonstrating strong cross-domain generalization.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to long-horizon tasks such as scientific discovery and machine learning engineering (MLE), where sustained self-evolution becomes a key capability. However, existing MLE agents suffer from inter-branch information isolation, memoryless search, and lack of hierarchical control, which together hinder long-horizon optimization. We present MLEvolve, an LLM-based self-evolving multi-agent framework for end-to-end machine learning algorithm discovery. By extending tree search to Progressive MCGS, MLEvolve enables cross-branch information flow through graph-based reference edges and gradually shifts the search from broad exploration to focused exploitation with an entropy-inspired progressive schedule. To allow the agent to evolve with accumulated experience, we introduce Retrospective Memory, which combines a cold-start domain knowledge base with a dynamic global memory for task-specific experience retrieval and reuse. For stable long-horizon iteration, we further decouple strategic planning from code generation with adaptive coding modes. Evaluation on MLE-Bench shows that MLEvolve achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple dimensions including average medal rate and valid submission rate under a 12-hour budget (half the standard runtime). Moreover, MLEvolve also outperforms specialized algorithm discovery methods including AlphaEvolve on mathematical algorithm optimization tasks, demonstrating strong cross-domain generalization. Our code is available at https://github.com/InternScience/MLEvolve.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [109] [A New Quaternion-Joint Cable-Driven Redundant Manipulator Configuration and its Control Through FABRIK and Residual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05236)
*Tanapath Pornthisan,Thanapat Kemthong,Thanyapisit Kangsathien,Pasut Aranchaiya,Paulo Garcia,Viboon Sangveraphunsiri*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A 4-segment, 8-joint quaternion-joint cable-driven redundant manipulator achieves broader workspace at lower hardware cost, and Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms FABRIK by three orders of magnitude in positional and orientational accuracy for controlling such manipulators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic arms capable of traversing arbitrary spatial paths in highly obstructed workspaces are highly desired across industries, but quaternion-joint kinematic complexity challenges configuration design and control, while fabrication imprecision amplifies discrepancies.

Method: Proposes a 4-segment, 8-joint quaternion-joint manipulator configuration; applies Residual Reinforcement Learning for control, benchmarked against the FABRIK algorithm.

Result: The 4-segment, 8-joint configuration achieves broader workspace at lower hardware cost; Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms FABRIK by three orders of magnitude in positional and orientational accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed configuration is more workspace-effective than prior designs, and Residual Reinforcement Learning enables precise control with simpler implementation; the methodology generalizes to designing new manipulator systems and control systems.

Abstract: Robotic arms capable of traversing arbitrary spatial paths, especially in highly obstructed workspaces, are highly desired across several industries. Quaternion-joints have recently empowered a specific class of robotic arms -- cable-driven redundant manipulators -- beyond its prior capabilities. Specifically, quaternion-joints reduce the number of required motors per degree of freedom, paving the way for more compact solutions.An ongoing challenge is that the complexity of the kinematic model of quaternion joints challenges a priori decisions on manipulator configurations and imposes higher computational demands on the control system and its non-linearities amplify all discrepancies between design and physical artifact arising from fabrication imprecision. Here we show a that a 4-segment, 8-joint manipulator can achieve a broader workspace than extant configurations, at lower hardware cost, and that Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms extant state-of-the-art methods -- specifically, the FABRIK algorithm -- on the control of such manipulator. Our results show that this configuration is more workspace-effective than prior designs, and that Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms FABRIK by three orders of magnitude on positional and orientational accuracy, effecting precise control of the novel 4-segment, 8-joint manipulator. Additionally, the control implementation is simpler: we describe the complete FABRIK process for control and corresponding learning implementation. Our methodology is applicable to the design of new systems, providing designers with further tools for the development of this class of manipulators and corresponding control systems for novel configurations.

</details>


### [110] [Efficient Computation of Distance Functions for Navigation Vector Fields in Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05372)
*Vinicius M. Gonçalves,João Baião,Felipe Bartelt,Douglas G. Macharet,Gustavo M. Freitas,Héctor Azpúrua,Luciano C. A. Pimenta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an efficient method for computing the distance between a point and a G-polynomial curve on matrix Lie groups, reducing the problem to polynomial root-finding to achieve significant speedups for robot control applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vector-field-based methods for robot path-tracking on Lie Groups require repeatedly computing the distance between a robot configuration and a curve, which is computationally expensive, especially at high control frequencies on embedded platforms.

Method: The approach exploits the structure of G-polynomial curves (polynomial curves generalized to matrix Lie groups) to reduce the distance computation problem to a small number of polynomial root-finding computations, avoiding expensive optimization-based searches.

Result: Simulation results show significant reduction in computation time while maintaining accuracy compared to existing optimization-based approaches. The method was validated experimentally on a robotic manipulator for the SE(3) group.

Conclusion: The proposed method enables efficient distance computation for G-polynomial curves on Lie Groups, making vector-field-based path tracking feasible for high-frequency control on embedded platforms. An open-source computational package is provided.

Abstract: Vector-field-based methods are widely used for robot control and are often applied to the path-tracking problem. Some vector field approaches require repeatedly computing the distance between the robot configuration and the curve, as well as the corresponding closest point. Recently, vector fields have been extended to Lie Groups. In this case, this computation can be expensive, especially when performed at high control frequencies on embedded platforms. This paper proposes a method for efficiently computing the distance between a point and a curve represented as what is called a G-polynomial curve, which is a curve representation that generalizes polynomial curves to matrix Lie groups. The proposed approach exploits the structure of these curves to reduce the problem to a small number of polynomial root-finding computations. Simulation results show that the method significantly reduces computation time while maintaining accuracy compared to existing optimization-based approaches. Practical formulas are also provided for the case of the group SE(3), and the method is validated experimentally on a robotic manipulator. The methodology is implemented in a computational package, available online.

</details>


### [111] [VASO: Formally Verifiable Self-Evolving Skills for Physical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05395)
*Yunhao Yang,Neel P. Bhatt,Kevin Wang,Samuel Tetteh,Zhangyang Wang,Ufuk Topcu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VASO is a framework for verification-guided self-evolution of LLM-generated robot skill contracts, using formal model checking to filter inconsistent skills and convert counterexample traces into textual gradients for skill refinement, achieving 97.2% formal-specification compliance on robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill-evolution loops rely on execution feedback, unit tests, environment reward, or LLM self-critique, which provide only trace-level evidence (skill worked on sampled executions) rather than guaranteeing that skill-induced plans satisfy temporal safety contracts under untested conditions.

Method: VASO represents each skill as a semantic contract with two coupled interfaces: a formal interface aligning robot states/observations/commands with logical propositions for model checking, and a planner-facing interface for executable behavior generation. A model checker filters logically inconsistent contracts and verifies plans against temporal specifications. When verification fails, counterexample traces are translated into textual gradients to update the skill contract while keeping foundation-model weights frozen.

Result: On Clearpath Jackal and PX4 quadcopter tasks, VASO reaches 97.2% formal-specification compliance using fewer than 100 optimization samples, outperforming execution-feedback, prompt-optimization, and fine-tuning baselines.

Conclusion: VASO is the first framework that closes the loop between formal verification and self-evolving LLM-generated skills for physical AI agents, where formal counterexamples serve as optimization feedback for reusable robot skill contracts rather than merely verifying one-off plans, tuning planner prompts, or fine-tuning model weights.

Abstract: Reusable robot skills are becoming the basic units through which embodied agents turn open-ended instructions into long-horizon physical behavior. We argue that, while foundation models have collapsed the cost of creating these skills, the cost of trusting them has not. Existing skill-evolution loops refine skills through execution feedback, unit tests, environment reward, or LLM self-critique, but these signals provide only trace-level evidence: they show that a skill worked on sampled executions, not that skill-induced plans satisfy temporal safety contracts under untested conditions. We introduce VASO, a framework for verification-guided self-evolution of LLM-generated robot skill contracts. In VASO, each skill is represented as a semantic contract with two coupled interfaces: a formal interface that aligns robot states, observations, and control commands with logical propositions for model checking, and a planner-facing interface that guides executable behavior generation. A model checker first filters logically inconsistent skill contracts, then verifies plans induced by the skill against global and local temporal specifications. When verification fails, VASO translates the counterexample trace into a textual gradient that updates the reusable skill contract while keeping foundation-model weights frozen. On Clearpath Jackal and PX4 quadcopter tasks, VASO reaches 97.2% formal-specification compliance using fewer than 100 optimization samples, outperforming execution-feedback, prompt-optimization, and fine-tuning baselines. To our knowledge, VASO is the first framework that closes the loop between formal verification and self-evolving LLM-generated skills for physical AI agents: formal counterexamples become optimization feedback for reusable robot skill contracts, rather than merely verifying one-off plans, tuning planner prompts, or fine-tuning model weights.

</details>


### [112] [MoDex: A Diffusion Policy for Sequential Multi-Object Dexterous Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05407)
*Haofei Lu,Hongjia Liu,Yifei Dong,Florian T. Pokorny,Jens Lundell,Danica Kragic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MoDex is a diffusion policy that enables a dexterous hand to sequentially grasp multiple objects without releasing previously held ones, using opposition space conditioning to allocate only necessary fingers per grasp, trained via two-stage imitation+RL and achieving 2.92-17.92% (sim) and 6.67-17.78% (real) improvements over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dexterous grasping methods commit all hand DOFs to a single object, underutilizing dexterity and leaving no redundancy for subsequent grasps, limiting the ability to sequentially grasp multiple objects without releasing those already held.

Method: MoDex is a diffusion policy that predicts the next gripper pose from observations, conditioned on an opposition space (specifying which fingers participate) and point cloud. Training is two-stage: imitation learning on expert demonstrations followed by reinforcement learning fine-tuning for sim-to-real transfer.

Result: In both simulation (MuJoCo-based Franka Emika Panda + Allegro Hand) and real-world experiments, MoDex outperforms learning-based baselines by 2.92-17.92% (sim) and 6.67-17.78% (real) in success rates.

Conclusion: MoDex effectively enables sequential multi-object grasping with a single dexterous hand by selectively allocating finger DOFs per grasp, with RL fine-tuning consistently improving success rates and strong sim-to-real transfer demonstrated.

Abstract: This work addresses sequentially grasping multiple objects with a single dexterous hand without releasing those already held. Most dexterous grasping methods commit all of the hand's degrees of freedom to a single object, underutilizing its dexterity and leaving no redundancy for subsequent grasps. The proposed solution, MoDex, is a diffusion policy that predicts the next gripper pose directly from observations, conditioned on an opposition space and point cloud. The opposition space condition specifies which fingers participate in the current grasp, enabling the gripper to use only a subset of its available degrees of freedom while reserving the remaining degrees of freedom for subsequent grasps. To facilitate sim-to-real transfer, MoDex is trained in two stages: first through imitation learning on expert demonstrations, and subsequently through reinforcement learning fine-tuning, which consistently improves success rates over the pre-trained policy. We evaluate MoDex in simulation on a MuJoCo-based Franka Emika Panda robot equipped with an Allegro Hand and on the corresponding real-world hardware platform. Across both simulation and real-world experiments, MoDex achieves higher success rates than the evaluated learning-based baselines, improving performance by 2.92-17.92% and 6.67-17.78%, respectively. Project page: https://modex2026.github.io/.

</details>


### [113] [Uncertainty-Aware Adaptive Sensor Fusion for Autonomous Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05437)
*Simegnew Yihunie Alaba,Yuichi Motai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hybrid deep learning approach combining Vision Transformer (ViT) and Multiscale CNN (MCNN) with an Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) for enhanced VIO pose estimation. An adaptive sensor fusion module with uncertainty-aware loss improves robustness under noisy conditions. The method achieves state-of-the-art ATE/RPE on KITTI at 155 FPS on an A100 GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve pose estimation accuracy and robustness in Visual-Inertial Odometry (VIO) for autonomous navigation, especially under challenging environmental conditions with noisy, incomplete, or unreliable sensor inputs.

Method: A hybrid deep learning framework integrating: (1) Vision Transformer (ViT) for capturing temporal dependencies from IMU data; (2) Multiscale CNN (MCNN) for learning optical flow-based motion cues from visual data; (3) Adaptive sensor fusion module for dynamically weighting IMU and visual features based on estimated uncertainty; (4) Uncertainty-aware loss function incorporating prediction uncertainty into training; all coupled with an Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF).

Result: The method outperforms baselines on the KITTI dataset in terms of Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) and Relative Pose Error (RPE). The model achieves 155 FPS on an NVIDIA A100 GPU, demonstrating computational efficiency suitable for resource-constrained systems.

Conclusion: The proposed hybrid ViT-MCNN-UKF approach with adaptive fusion and uncertainty-aware learning significantly enhances VIO pose estimation accuracy and robustness, while maintaining a lightweight and computationally efficient design suitable for real-time autonomous navigation deployment.

Abstract: This work introduces a hybrid deep learning approach integrated with an Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) to enhance pose estimation accuracy in Visual-Inertial Odometry (VIO) for autonomous navigation. The proposed model employs a Vision Transformer (ViT) network to effectively capture temporal dependencies from inertial measurement unit (IMU) data and utilizes a Multiscale Convolutional Neural Network (MCNN) to learn optical flow-based motion cues from visual data. An adaptive sensor fusion module dynamically weights IMU and visual features by leveraging estimated uncertainty, thus improving robustness in diverse and challenging environmental conditions. Additionally, a novel uncertainty-aware loss function is proposed to explicitly incorporate prediction uncertainty into the learning process, enabling robust and accurate navigation under noisy, incomplete, or unreliable sensor inputs. Comprehensive evaluations of the KITTI dataset demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms baseline approaches, achieving superior performance in terms of Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) and Relative Pose Error (RPE). The lightweight and computationally efficient model processes data at 155 FPS on an NVIDIA A100 GPU, making it highly suitable for deployment in resource-constrained autonomous systems.

</details>


### [114] [FlowPRO: Reward-Free Reinforced Fine-Tuning of Flow-Matching VLAs via Proximalized Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05468)
*Yihao Wu,He Zhang,Junbo Tan,Xueqian Wang,Zhengyou Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FlowPRO is a reward-free offline reinforced fine-tuning framework for flow-matching VLAs, featuring RPRO (a preference-optimization objective with proximal regularization to prevent reward hacking), and a teleoperated intervention-and-rollback data collection paradigm with Smooth Interpolation. It achieves state-of-the-art success rates on four long-horizon bimanual tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training VLA models for reliable real-world robot deployment remains difficult: SFT/DAgger exploit failure signals only indirectly, and reward-based RL is bottlenecked by real-world reward design and training reliable critics.

Method: FlowPRO consists of (1) RPRO: a preference-optimization objective for flow-matching action heads combining contrastive optimization with explicit proximal regularization to anchor implicit reward magnitude and prevent reward hacking; (2) a teleoperated intervention-and-rollback paradigm producing naturally paired positive/negative trajectories; (3) Smooth Interpolation with batch mixing converting sparse corrections into dense per-state supervision while preserving base policy capabilities.

Result: On four long-horizon bimanual tasks, FlowPRO achieves the highest success rate, outperforming four representative baselines. Ablations confirm each loss component contributes positively.

Conclusion: FlowPRO provides an effective reward-free offline reinforced fine-tuning framework for flow-matching VLAs, eliminating the need for reward engineering while addressing reward hacking via proximal regularization, and enabling practical real-robot data collection with minimal human effort.

Abstract: Post-training Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models into policies that can be reliably deployed on real robots remains a major bottleneck. SFT and DAgger exploit failure signals only indirectly, and reward-based RL is bottlenecked by the difficulty of real-world reward design and of training reliable critics. We present FlowPRO, a reward-free offline reinforced fine-tuning framework for flow-matching VLAs. Algorithmically, we propose RPRO (Robotic Flow-matching Proximalized Preference Optimization), a preference-optimization objective tailored to the flow-matching action head of VLA models. RPRO pairs a contrastive optimizer with an explicit proximal regularizer that anchors the absolute magnitude of the implicit reward, thereby eliminating the reward-hacking failure mode of plain Flow-DPO. On the data side, a teleoperated intervention-and-rollback paradigm produces naturally paired positive and negative trajectories $(τ^w, τ^l)$ on a real robot from a single operator action; a Smooth Interpolation procedure, combined with batch mixing, then converts these sparse corrections into dense per-state supervision while preserving the base policy's capabilities. On four long-horizon bimanual tasks, FlowPRO attains the highest success rate, outperforming four representative baselines, and ablations confirm the contribution of each loss component.

</details>


### [115] [Learning Contact Representation for Leg Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05501)
*Emre Girgin,Cagri Kilic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A self-supervised representation learning framework for legged robot contact detection that uses only joint encoder data, without force sensors, achieving superior performance compared to supervised and baseline probabilistic methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional legged robot odometry relies on foot contact detection, which typically requires force sensors at the foot tip. These sensors are not universally available and fail under disturbances like slippage. The authors aim to develop a contact detection method that works without force sensor augmentation.

Method: The authors propose a self-supervised representation learning framework that uses only standard joint encoder data. Learned representations probabilistically model stance and swing phases without requiring labeled data or force sensor inputs.

Result: The proposed self-supervised contact detector demonstrated superior performance compared to supervised methods that require sensor augmentation and labeling, as well as baseline probabilistic approaches.

Conclusion: Self-supervised learning from joint encoders alone is sufficient for accurate contact detection in legged robots, eliminating the need for specialized force sensors while outperforming existing methods.

Abstract: The estimation of odometry in legged robots depends on the assumption that the velocity of the foot with respect to the world remains zero during the stance phase. Feedback for the main body velocity is derived from the kinematic serial chain of the feet making accurate leg phase detection is a critical subproblem. A considerable number of studies employ ground reaction force sensors mounted at the tip of the foot to classify, yet these sensors may not be universally available for all legged robots. Additionally, these sensors are often unresponsive to unaccounted disturbances, such as slippage, while the foot remains in contact with the ground. In this study, we propose a self-supervised representation learning framework for contact detection that utilizes the standard sensor set of joint encoders without reliance on force sensor augmentations. We employ learned representations to model the stance and swing phases probabilistically. The experimental results obtained confirm the efficacy of the proposed self-supervised contact detector. Our framework exhibited superior performance in comparison to supervised methods which necessitate sensor set augmentation and labeling, as well as baseline probabilistic approaches. Additionally, we make our code available to the public.

</details>


### [116] [Auditing Demonstration Curation Metrics: Action-Only Scorers Fail on the Structural Defects That Degrade Imitation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05588)
*Aarav Bedi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper builds a controlled testbed to audit seven demonstration curation metrics, finding that only state-trajectory metrics detect structural errors (wrong actions at key moments), while action-only metrics fail or are inverted for such defects; high detection accuracy does not guarantee downstream policy improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation-learning policies suffer from low-quality demonstrations, and while many curation metrics claim to filter these automatically, they are validated on different data and protocols, making it unclear which metrics actually identify harmful demonstrations.

Method: A controlled testbed is constructed where demonstration defects (subtle perturbations and structural errors) are injected with known types. Seven curation metrics are audited along two axes: (1) separation of defective vs. clean demonstrations, and (2) downstream behavior-cloning policy success after curation.

Result: Subtle perturbations (correlated action noise, tremor, truncation) are detectable by multivariate outlier scoring and removal recovers full downstream gap. Structural errors (wrong action at key moment) are invisible to action-only metrics; two metrics are inverted (score defects higher). Only state-trajectory metrics detect structural errors, but even the best recovers only a third of the downstream gap.

Conclusion: High detection accuracy does not guarantee downstream improvement; action-only metrics are insufficient for structural defects, and the community needs better metrics for such errors.

Abstract: Imitation-learning policies inherit the quality of the demonstrations they are trained on, and a growing set of curation metrics promise to score and filter low-quality demonstrations automatically. These metrics are each validated on different data with different protocols, so it is unclear which of them actually identify the demonstrations that harm a policy. We build a controlled testbed in which demonstration defects are injected with known type, and audit seven curation metrics along two axes: how well each separates defective from clean demonstrations, and whether training a behavior-cloning policy on each metric's curated subset improves task success. We study two defect regimes. Subtle perturbations (correlated action noise, tremor, truncation) are detectable by multivariate outlier scoring and, once removed, recover the full downstream gap. Structural errors, where the demonstration executes a wrong action at a key moment, are invisible to every action-only metric we test, and two of them are inverted: they score defective demonstrations as higher quality and, used for curation, tend to leave the policy at or below the uncurated baseline rather than above it. Only metrics that examine the state trajectory detect structural errors, and even the best of them recovers just a third of the downstream gap. High detection accuracy does not guarantee downstream improvement. We release the testbed and all curation implementations.

</details>


### [117] [Discrete-WAM: Unified Discrete Vision-Action Token Editing for World-Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05645)
*Ziyang Yao,Haochen Liu,Yuncheng Jiang,Zeyu Zhu,Zibin Guo,Jingru Wang,Tianle Liu,Jianwei Cui,Kuiyuan Yang,Hongwei Xie,Jingwei Zhao,Guang Chen,Hangjun Ye*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Discrete-WAM unifies latent vision and action tokens through a shared discrete diffusion framework, enabling compositional causal reasoning and world-action policy for autonomous driving, achieving competitive performance with controllable generation and counterfactual reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end autonomous driving methods lack explicit modeling of action-conditioned dynamics and compositional causal reasoning across counterfactual futures, limiting reliable decision-making.

Method: Discrete-WAM aligns future visual states and ego actions as discrete tokens within a shared discrete diffusion framework, unifying world modeling, world-action policy, and hierarchical decision-enabled policy.

Result: Competitive performance on large-scale autonomous driving benchmarks with support for controllable generation and counterfactual reasoning.

Conclusion: Discrete-WAM provides a principled path toward more reliable autonomous driving decision-making through compositional causal reasoning over alternative futures.

Abstract: Autonomous driving requires reasoning about how ego actions shape the evolution of the surrounding world. However, most end-to-end methods rely on direct state-to-action mappings, capturing correlations without explicitly modeling action-conditioned dynamics. Conversely, continuous-latent world models often lack compositional structure for causal reasoning across counterfactual futures. We introduce Discrete-WAM, a unified latent vision-action world policy that represents future visual states and ego actions as aligned discrete tokens, enabling compositional causal reasoning across alternative futures. Built upon this unified discrete alignment, Discrete-WAM establishes a shared discrete diffusion framework with unified generative tasks, jointly formulating world modeling, world-action policy, and hierarchical decision-enabled policy, supporting compositional generalization across diverse driving scenarios. Experiments on large-scale autonomous-driving benchmarks show that Discrete-WAM achieves competitive performance while supporting controllable generation and counterfactual reasoning, offering a principled path toward more reliable decision-making.

</details>


### [118] [Safe Embodied AI for Long-horizon Tasks: A Cross-layer Analysis of Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05660)
*Dabin Kim,Daemin Park,Sangyub Lee,Jinsik Kim,Yeongtak Oh,Jongho Shin,Sungroh Yoon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey reviews safety in long-horizon robotic manipulation from an embodied AI perspective, organizing approaches by intervention locus (planning-time, policy-time, execution-time) and analyzing the strength of evidence (formal guarantees, statistical support, empirical heuristics), identifying gaps in policy-time safety, contact-rich manipulation, uncertainty-triggered intervention, and benchmark availability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied AI systems are increasingly expected to operate over extended horizons in physical environments, where failures can cause real-world harm. The literature on safe embodied AI is fragmented across planning, policy, and execution, and long-horizon robotic manipulation exposes unique failure modes (semantic misgrounding, subtask error propagation, execution drift, contact-rich physical risk) that demand a structured review.

Method: The authors organize the literature by intervention locus (planning-time, policy-time, execution-time safety) and analyze evidence strength (formal guarantees, statistical support, empirical safety heuristics), while distinguishing backbone capability papers, direct safety mechanisms, and benchmark/evaluation studies.

Result: Identified persistent gaps: limited evidence for policy-time safety, weak formal support for contact-rich long-horizon manipulation, immature uncertainty-triggered intervention, and a shortage of manipulation-specific safety benchmarks.

Conclusion: The survey concludes by outlining research directions for cross-layer assurance, evaluation design, and safer deployment of long-horizon robotic agents in real-world settings.

Abstract: Embodied AI systems are increasingly expected to reason and act over extended horizons in physical environments. This growing capability brings safety to the foreground, because failures in the physical world can harm people, damage objects, and disrupt workplaces. Although safe embodied AI has attracted substantial attention, the literature remains fragmented across planning, policy design, and runtime execution. Long-horizon robotic manipulation is a particularly revealing anchor domain for this problem because semantic misgrounding, subtask-level error propagation, execution drift, and contact-rich physical risk can accumulate within the same closed-loop system. This survey therefore provides a structured review of safety in long-horizon robotic manipulation from an embodied AI perspective. We organize the literature by intervention locus, covering planning-time, policy-time, and execution-time safety, and we analyze the strength of the evidence that each line of work provides, distinguishing formal guarantees, statistical support, and empirical safety heuristics. This framework clarifies the distinct roles of backbone capability papers, direct safety mechanisms, and benchmark or evaluation studies, while exposing where current safety claims are well supported and where they remain indirect. We identify persistent gaps, including limited evidence for policy-time safety, weak formal support for contact-rich long-horizon manipulation, immature uncertainty-triggered intervention, and a shortage of manipulation-specific safety benchmarks. We conclude by outlining research directions for cross-layer assurance, evaluation design, and safer deployment of long-horizon robotic agents in real-world settings.

</details>


### [119] [Preserving Full 6-DOF Actuation Under Abrupt Total Rotor Failures: Passive Fault-Tolerant Flight Control Using a Biaxial-Tilt Hexacopter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05663)
*Yipeng Yang,Yiqiao Tang,Hao Zhang,Jinqi Jiang,Jianfeng He,Rumo Chen,Xinghu Yu,Zhan Li,Huijun Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents two passive fault-tolerant control schemes (HOFA+LESO at controller layer, MRAC allocation with momentum-based wrench estimation at allocator layer) for a biaxial-tilt overactuated hexacopter (BTO) under abrupt total rotor failures, without requiring fault detection or mode switching. They extend the AWS metric with a transient-wrench-jump term and validate through simulations and experiments including indoor/outdoor tracking, narrow-frame traversal, and aerial writing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional multirotors experience rapid collapse of attainable wrench space (AWS) under abrupt total rotor failures, making full 6-DOF recovery physically impossible. The authors aim to address passive fault-tolerant flight under rotor failures that are unknown a priori, without relying on explicit fault detection, isolation, or mode switching.

Method: (1) Extended the inscribed-sphere metric of AWS by incorporating a transient-wrench-jump term for quantitative feasibility assessment. (2) Developed two passive control schemes: (a) HOFA controller combined with LESO for lumped-disturbance rejection at the controller layer; (b) Model-reference adaptive control allocation with momentum-based wrench estimation at the allocator layer. Both avoid fault detection and online optimization.

Result: Simulations and flight experiments validated stable hovering and 6-DOF trajectory tracking under single and multiple rotor failures. BTO provides larger recovery margins than uniaxial-tilt and coplanar designs. Additional onboard-sensor-only experiments (indoor tracking under wind, outdoor extreme tracking, narrow-frame traversal, contact-based aerial writing) confirmed robustness.

Conclusion: The proposed passive fault-tolerant framework enables BTO hexacopters to sustain full 6-DOF control after abrupt total rotor failures without fault detection or mode switching, and outperforms alternative multirotor configurations in recovery margins and robustness.

Abstract: Conventional multirotors suffer from a rapid collapse of attainable wrench space (AWS) under abrupt total rotor failures, rendering full 6-DOF recovery physically impossible. This paper addresses passive fault-tolerant flight of a biaxial-tilt overactuated hexacopter (BTO) under abrupt total rotor failures that are a priori unknown to the controller. The control design and analysis focus on representative abrupt rotor-failure cases for which the post-failure system remains fully actuated, while no explicit fault detection, isolation, or fault-mode switching is assumed. First, we extend the inscribed-sphere metric of the AWS by incorporating the transient-wrench-jump term, enabling quantitative feasibility assessment under up to three simultaneous rotor failures and benchmarking against uniaxial-tilt and coplanar hexacopters. Second, we develop two computationally efficient passive schemes without relying on fault detection or online optimization. One scheme operates at the controller layer by combining a high-order fully actuated (HOFA) controller with a linear extended state observer (LESO) for lumped-disturbance rejection. The other scheme operates at the allocator layer by using model-reference adaptive control allocation with momentum-based wrench estimation to compensate for control-allocation biases. Simulations and flight experiments validate stable hovering and 6-DOF trajectory tracking under single and multiple rotor failures. Further systematic comparisons confirm that the BTO provides larger recovery margins than uniaxial-tilt and coplanar designs. Additional onboard-sensor-only experiments, including indoor tracking under wind disturbance, outdoor tracking under extreme conditions, narrow-frame traversal, and contact-based aerial writing, further validate the robustness of the proposed framework in complex operational environments.

</details>


### [120] [Dynamic Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery in Robotic Cellular Warehousing Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05669)
*Cheng Ren,Ming Li,Xinping Guan,George Q. Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Dynamic Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery problem with internal order evolution for robotic warehouses, proposing two event-triggered replanning algorithms (Dynamic Token Passing and Cooperative Token Passing) that handle dynamic SKU additions to orders, achieving reduced order flowtime compared to static baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real warehouse operations involve dynamic order evolution where new SKUs may be appended to orders during execution, but existing MAPD formulations assume static tasks. This gap motivates the need for algorithms that handle internal order evolution in RCWS.

Method: Two event-triggered online replanning algorithms based on token passing: (1) Dynamic Token Passing performs localized replanning via add-order decomposition and priority-based token scheduling; (2) Cooperative Token Passing enables idle robots to opportunistically assist newly added pickups.

Result: Simulation results in RCWS environments show significant reduction in order flowtime compared with static and non-cooperative baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed event-triggered token passing methods effectively handle dynamic order evolution in multi-agent warehouse systems, improving efficiency over static approaches.

Abstract: Robotic Cellular Warehousing Systems (RCWS) give rise to multi-agent pickup and delivery (MAPD) processes in which robots sequentially collect multiple stock-keeping units (SKUs) for each order. Unlike classical MAPD formulations that assume static tasks, real warehouse operations often involve dynamic order evolution, where new SKUs may be appended to an order while it is being executed. Motivated by this practical requirement, this letter formulates the Dynamic Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery problem considering internal order evolution for the first time. Building on the token passing paradigm, we propose two event-triggered online replanning algorithms. The first, Dynamic Token Passing, performs localized replanning upon order updates through add-order decomposition and priority-based token scheduling while preserving collision-free execution. The second, Cooperative Token Passing, further enables idle robots to opportunistically assist newly added pickups, improving system-level efficiency. Simulation results in RCWS environments demonstrate that the proposed methods significantly reduce order flowtime compared with static and non-cooperative baselines.

</details>


### [121] [Accelerating and Scaling MPC-Guided Reinforcement Learning for Humanoid Locomotion and Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05687)
*Junheng Li,Liang Wu,Sergio A. Esteban,Lizhi Yang,Ján Drgoňa,Aaron D. Ames*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces MPC-RL, a framework that integrates model predictive control (MPC) as training-time guidance for reinforcement learning (RL) in humanoid locomotion and manipulation. It proposes a centroidal-dynamics MPC reward formulation and develops π^nMPC, a parallel-in-horizon, construction-free batched GPU MPC solver that avoids high memory usage and pre-compilation, enabling efficient massively parallel RL training. The method achieves superior performance in both simulation and hardware validations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Using MPC inside RL typically requires time-consuming problem construction or excessive training overhead, making existing frameworks difficult to justify in practice. The goal is to develop an efficient training-time MPC guidance approach for humanoid locomotion and manipulation.

Method: 1) A centroidal-dynamics MPC reward formulation that leverages guidance from MPC trajectories during RL training time. 2) π^nMPC, a parallel-in-horizon and construction-free batched GPU MPC solver that operates on time-varying dynamics to avoid high memory usage and pre-compilation, enabling practicality in massively parallel RL.

Result: Superior performance in locomotion and manipulation skills compared to alternative methods, validated through comparative studies and hardware experiments.

Conclusion: MPC-RL provides an efficient and practical framework for integrating MPC guidance into RL training for humanoid control, achieving state-of-the-art results with a publicly available codebase.

Abstract: In humanoid motion control, model predictive control (MPC) offers physically grounded prediction and constraint handling, while reinforcement learning (RL) enables robust whole-body skills through large-scale simulation. However, using MPC inside RL often requires time-consuming problem construction or excessive training overhead, making such frameworks difficult to justify in practice. This work studies efficient training-time MPC guidance for humanoid locomotion and manipulation, termed MPC-RL. We introduce a centroidal-dynamics MPC reward formulation that leverages guidance from MPC trajectories in training time. To make this practical in massively parallel RL, we develop $π^n$MPC, a parallel-in-horizon and construction-free batched GPU MPC solver that operates directly on time-varying dynamics to avoid high memory usage and pre-compilation. Through a variety of comparative studies and hardware validations, we have found that MPC-RL achieves superior performance in locomotion and manipulation skills. The code base is available at https://github.com/junhengl/mpc-rl.

</details>


### [122] [DexFuture: Hierarchical Future-State Visuomotor Targeting for Bimanual Dexterous Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05699)
*Runfa Blark Li,Kuang-Ting Tu,Nikola Raicevic,Dwait Bhatt,Xinshuang Liu,Keito Suzuki,Ki Myung Brian Lee,Nikolay Atanasov,Truong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexFuture is a hierarchical system for bimanual dexterous tool use that uses a high-level future-state predictor (horizon-conditioned transformer) to generate multi-step target trajectories from egocentric RGB and proprioceptive history, and a low-level target-conditioned per-link transformer policy to track them—achieving 90% of privileged-oracle performance at 60Hz, ~250x faster than CEM planning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bimanual dexterous tool use is challenging due to high-dimensional hand configurations and complex hand-tool-object dynamics; existing methods rely on demonstration-provided references or slow online planning over high-dimensional action sequences, lacking a way to generate dynamically consistent future references without privileged state information.

Method: A hierarchical system: (1) High-level Future-State Visuomotor Target Predictor uses egocentric RGB, proprioceptive, and geometric history with a horizon-conditioned transformer to generate structured hand-tool-object visuomotor embeddings and multi-step future target trajectories. (2) Low-level Target-Conditioned Structured Dexterous Policy tracks these targets with a target-conditioned per-link transformer. The hierarchy decouples coarse future reference generation from fine-grained control and slow semantic prediction from fast execution.

Result: On OakInk2 bimanual tool-use tasks, DexFuture achieves 90% of privileged-oracle performance (vs. 7% for no-reference policy), operates at 60 Hz, and is ~250x faster than DexWM-style CEM planning with a future action-conditioned world model.

Conclusion: DexFuture demonstrates that effective bimanual dexterous manipulation can be achieved without privileged demonstration references or slow planning, by hierarchically decoupling future-state prediction from action execution. The approach yields near-oracle performance with a significant speed advantage.

Abstract: Bimanual dexterous tool use remains challenging for robots due to high-dimensional hand configurations and complex hand-tool-object dynamics and contact. Most existing control policies depend on future configuration references provided from demonstrations, while future action-conditioned world models require slow online planning over high-dimensional action sequences. A significant challenge is generating a dynamically consistent future reference trajectory without relying on privileged states from demonstrations or slow counterfactual planning. We propose DexFuture, a hierarchical system that couples a high-level Future-State Visuomotor Target Predictor with a low-level Target-Conditioned Structured Dexterous Policy. Conditioned on egocentric RGB, proprioceptive and geometric history, the high-level predictor constructs structured hand-tool-object visuomotor embeddings and uses a horizon-conditioned transformer to generate a multi-step future target trajectory. Then, the low-level policy tracks them with a target-conditioned per-link transformer. This hierarchy decouples coarse future reference generation from fine-grained action control, and slow long-horizon semantic prediction from high-frequency execution. On OakInk2 bimanual tool-use tasks, DexFuture achieves 90% of the privileged-oracle performance, compared to 7% for a no-reference policy. DexFuture operates at 60 Hz, approximately 250 times faster than DexWM-style Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) planning with a future action-conditioned world model.

</details>


### [123] [PiL-World: A Chunk-Wise World Model for VLA Policy-in-the-Loop Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05773)
*Chong Ma,Taiyi Su,Jian Zhu,Jianjun Zhang,Zitai Huang,Yi Xu,Hanli Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PiL-World is a chunk-wise world model enabling closed-loop evaluation of vision-language-action (VLA) policies by generating multi-view future observations conditioned on policy rollouts, reducing the gap between simulated and real-world VLA success rate error from 63.2% to 12.0% on dual-arm manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models for robot action evaluation are limited to open-loop prediction along pre-collected trajectories, preventing closed-loop VLA evaluation where each action chunk depends on the observation generated by previous execution.

Method: PiL-World generates multi-view future observations conditioned on action rollouts from VLA policies, using head-view motion-derived visual control and latent history encoding for rollout fidelity, while jointly predicting complementary multi-view observations and learning from both successful and failed trajectories.

Result: On three real dual-arm manipulation tasks, PiL-World produces rollouts highly consistent with real robot executions and reduces VLA success rate error between real-world and world-model evaluation from 63.2% to 12.0%.

Conclusion: PiL-World enables effective closed-loop VLA policy evaluation without requiring real robot execution at every step, substantially narrowing the gap between simulated and real-world performance estimation.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies operate in a closed loop in real-world robot tasks: a robot observes the scene, executes an action chunk, and conditions its next decision on the resulting observation. However, most existing world models for robot action evaluation are limited to open-loop prediction along pre-collected action trajectories. This prevents them from supporting closed-loop VLA evaluation, where each action chunk must be conditioned on the observation generated by the previous execution. To address this gap, we propose PiL-World, a chunk-wise world model designed for policy-in-the-loop VLA evaluation. Given the current observation and the action trajectory rolled out by a VLA policy, PiL-World generates multi-view future observations that are consistent with the VLA rollout and match the image inputs required by the policy. By alternating between VLA inference and world-model prediction, PiL-World enables closed-loop evaluation without real robot execution at every step. To improve rollout fidelity, PiL-World conditions video generation on action-derived visual control from head-view robot motion and latent histories that encode task execution context, while jointly predicting complementary multi-view observations. Beyond successful teleoperated demonstrations, it also learns from failed execution trajectories, helping the imagined rollouts better match the distribution of real policy executions. We evaluate PiL-World on three real dual-arm manipulation tasks. PiL-World generates imagined rollouts that are highly consistent with real robot executions. More importantly, compared with the baseline, it reduces the error between VLA success rates measured in real-world rollouts and those estimated through closed-loop world-model evaluation from 63.2% to 12.0%.

</details>


### [124] [Visuotactile and Explicitly Force-Controlled Robotic Ultrasound for Abdominal Volumetric Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05848)
*Adrian Piedra,R Brooke Jeffrey,Oussama Khatib*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A robotic ultrasound system integrating stereo vision, force feedback, and expert-informed strategies achieves autonomous abdominal scans with quality comparable to human experts, while enabling 3D volume acquisition that surpasses expert capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an autonomous robotic ultrasound system that can replicate expert-level scanning quality while overcoming human limitations such as inconsistency and inability to acquire volumetric data for advanced diagnostic analysis.

Method: The system records expert freehand motion and force data, replays it with a 7-DOF torque-controlled manipulator, uses stereo vision for 3D abdominal topography mapping, refines maps via stiffness measurements to identify rib cage boundaries, and executes two scanning paths (upward-angled subcostal sweep and perpendicular soft-tissue sweep) under closed-loop force control.

Result: The system achieves imaging quality comparable to expert scans while dynamically adapting to patient-specific topographies, and surpasses human capabilities by enabling 3D volume acquisition.

Conclusion: Integrating expert knowledge, perception-based autonomy, and physical reasoning into robotic systems can enhance diagnostic performance and expand ultrasound capabilities beyond what human operators can achieve.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a robotic ultrasound acquisition system that integrates stereo vision, touch-based feedback, and expert-informed strategies to perform autonomous and adaptive abdominal scans. The system records freehand motion and force data from expert radiologists, creating a framework to capture transducer motion, applied forces, and anatomical scanning strategies. This expert data is replayed to replicate characteristic scans with the robot, forming a foundation for further autonomous capabilities. Using stereo vision, the system generates three-dimensional topography maps of the patient's abdomen, which are refined through stiffness measurements at key points to delineate the rib cage boundary. These combined techniques enable the robot to execute two distinct scanning paths: an upward-angled sweep beneath the rib cage to visualize structures near the upper abdomen and a perpendicular sweep across soft tissue regions. A compliant, torque-controlled seven degree-of-freedom robotic manipulator is controlled to maintain consistent probe contact through closed-loop force control over the varied anatomical surfaces. Physical experiments demonstrate that the system achieves high-quality imaging comparable to expert scans while dynamically adapting to patient-specific topographies. Furthermore, the robotic system surpasses expert capabilities by enabling three-dimensional volume acquisition, which enhances diagnostic potential and provides volumetric data for advanced analyses. This work highlights the integration of expert knowledge into autonomous robotic systems and underscores the potential of combining perception-based autonomy with physical reasoning for enhanced diagnostic performance.

</details>


### [125] [LadderMan: Learning Humanoid Perceptive Ladder Climbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05873)
*Siheng Zhao,Yuanhang Zhang,Ziqi Lu,Pieter Abbeel,Rocky Duan,Koushil Sreenath,Yue Wang,C. Karen Liu,Guanya Shi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LadderMan is a unified system enabling humanoid robots to robustly climb diverse ladders and perform manipulation tasks under constrained ladder conditions, using a two-stage learning pipeline with hybrid motion tracking, depth-based visuomotor policies, and vision foundation models for sim-to-real transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ladder climbing is extremely challenging for humanoid robots due to sparse footholds/handholds, complex whole-body coordination, and sensitivity to perception/control errors. The authors aim to address these challenges with a unified system.

Method: A scalable two-stage learning pipeline: (1) hybrid motion tracking to learn multiple climbing experts from a single reference motion; (2) distill experts into a unified depth-based visuomotor climbing policy via hybrid imitation and reinforcement learning. Vision foundation models bridge sim-to-real depth perception gap. A separate manipulation policy uses a dual-agent formulation for stable on-ladder teleoperation.

Result: Achieves robust ladder climbing across diverse geometries, zero-shot real-world transfer, and supports various manipulation tasks under challenging ladder constraints.

Conclusion: LadderMan demonstrates that a unified learning-based approach can enable humanoid robots to perform both climbing and manipulation on ladders, with successful real-world deployment.

Abstract: Humanoid robots hold great promise for operating in human-centered environments, yet ladder climbing remains one of the most challenging tasks due to sparse footholds and handholds, complex whole-body coordination, and sensitivity to perception and control errors. We present \textbf{LadderMan}, a unified system that enables humanoid robots to robustly climb diverse ladders and perform manipulation under such constrained conditions. Our climbing policy is built on a scalable two-stage learning pipeline, where we use hybrid motion tracking to learn multiple climbing experts from a single reference motion, and distill these experts into a unified depth-based visuomotor climbing policy via hybrid imitation and reinforcement learning. To enable real-world deployment, we leverage vision foundation models to bridge the sim-to-real gap in depth perception. Building on the learned climbing policy, we further train a separate manipulation policy using a dual-agent formulation, allowing stable on-ladder manipulation via teleoperation. Experiments demonstrate that LadderMan achieves robust ladder climbing across a wide range of geometries, successfully transfers to real-world hardware in a zero-shot manner, and supports various manipulation tasks under challenging ladder constraints. Video results are available at https://ladderman-robot.github.io .

</details>


### [126] [TAGA: Terrain-aware Active Gaze Learning for Generalizable Agile Humanoid Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05880)
*Peizhuo Li,Hongyi Li,Mingfeng Fan,Fangzhou Xu,Shuhao Liao,Yuxuan Ma,Zicheng Zeng,Ze Wang,Yongbin Jin,Yuhong Cao,Hongtao Wang,Guillaume Sartoretti*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAGA is a Terrain-aware Active Gaze learning framework that enables humanoid robots to selectively attend to relevant terrain regions during locomotion, achieving robust performance across challenging terrains including a record 1.2m gap traversal, with gaze behavior emerging naturally from reinforcement learning without explicit supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agile humanoid locomotion across diverse challenging terrain demands both wide perceptual coverage and precise local geometry understanding, but onboard computational constraints limit the ability to process high-density perceptual information across large-scale terrains.

Method: TAGA fuses vision, proprioception, and motion commands to guide a model to learn anticipatory cues and actively attend to specific areas of the height scan, selectively using informative regions for downstream networks via reinforcement learning without explicit gaze supervision.

Result: The policy demonstrates robust and generalizable locomotion in simulation and on hardware, including reliable terrain-aware foothold selection, elevated-platform traversal, competitive sparse-foothold traversal, and the largest reported real-world gap traversal distance of 1.2m among perceptive humanoid systems, with stability under severe perceptual disturbances.

Conclusion: Gaze behaviors can naturally emerge through reinforcement learning alone without supervision, significantly improving training efficiency and enabling fine-grained perceptive locomotion over larger-scale terrains under tight computational constraints.

Abstract: Agile humanoid locomotion across diverse challenging terrain demands both wide perceptual coverage and precise local geometry understanding. Motivated by the way humans selectively look at relevant terrain during locomotion, we introduce TAGA, a Terrain-aware Active Gaze learning framework for Attention-based humanoid control. By fusing vision, proprioception, and motion commands, our framework guides the model to learn anticipatory cues and actively attend to specific areas of the height scan, selectively using these informative regions for the downstream network. This adaptively increases the information density of observations under tight onboard computational constraints, thus enabling fine-grained perceptive locomotion over larger-scale terrains. We find that such gaze behaviors can naturally emerge through reinforcement learning alone, without requiring additional supervision or explicit guidance, significantly improve training efficiency. As a result, the trained policy demonstrates robust and generalizable locomotion in simulation and on hardware, including reliable terrain-aware foothold selection, elevated-platform traversal, competitive sparse-foothold traversal, and the largest reported real-world gap traversal distance of 1.2m among perceptive humanoid locomotion systems, while maintaining stability under severe perceptual disturbances and environmental interference.

</details>


### [127] [A Novel Method with Encoder-Decoder for Cross-Sensor Adaptation in Surface Shape Sensing with Sparse Strain Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05903)
*Shuo Wang,Heng Luo,Dian Jin,Xiaoming Tao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A meta-learning and few-shot adaptation method using an encoder-decoder architecture enables cross-sensor-array surface shape sensing, reducing error from 23.0 mm to 4.0 mm with less than 5% labeled data and under 1 second adaptation time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Performance variations in sensor arrays (due to intrinsic differences or installation conditions) cause inconsistent shape sensing results, requiring large datasets and retraining separate models for each sensor array, which increases data acquisition, transmission, and computation costs.

Method: An encoder-decoder architecture for surface shape sensing based on sparse strain sensors, incorporating meta-learning and few-shot adaptation strategies to enable cross-sensor-array adaptation.

Result: After cross-sensor adaptation, a newly deployed sensor array achieves ~4.0 mm sensing error with <5.0% newly labeled data and <1 second adaptation time, improving from 23.0 mm error without adaptation and 20-minute data collection for retraining. Points with errors below 5.0 mm increased by >65%.

Conclusion: The method substantially reduces cost and training burden for surface shape sensing, with broad potential applications in soft robotics and wearable devices.

Abstract: Performance variations in sensor arrays, caused by intrinsic differences or installation conditions, can lead to inconsistent results during shape sensing. To obtain accurate results, a large amount of data is usually required, and a separate model must be retrained for each sensor array, thereby increasing the cost and time of data acquisition, transmission, and computation. To address this issue, this work proposes an encoder-decoder architecture for surface shape sensing based on sparse strain sensors and further incorporates meta-learning and few-shot adaptation strategies to enable adaptation across different groups of sensor arrays. Experimental results demonstrate that, after the cross-sensor adaptation, a newly deployed sensor array achieves a sensing error of approximately 4.0 mm relying on less than 5.0% newly labeled data and requiring an adaptation time of under 1 second, which represents a substantial improvement from 23.0 mm error without adaptation and 20-minute data collection time required to train a new model. Moreover, the number of points with errors below 5.0 mm increased by more than 65.0%. These results indicate that the proposed method can substantially reduce the cost and training burden of surface shape sensing, and it has broad potential applications in soft robotics and wearable devices.

</details>


### [128] [Learning of Robot Safety Policies via Adversarial Synthetic Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05952)
*Nikolai Dorofeev,Alexey Odinokov,Rostislav Yavorskiy*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an agentic gamification framework where a Red Team generates hazardous scenarios and a Blue Team learns safety policies through adversarial game, enabling discovery of high-risk edge cases for Physical AI safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-risk edge cases for robot safety policies are unlikely to be captured through random simulation or manual enumeration, motivating the need for a systematic approach to discover hazardous scenarios.

Method: The framework models scenario generation as an adversarial game between a Red Team (explores failure space by constructing hazardous situations) and a Blue Team (incrementally refines safety policies). It combines classical risk modeling with adversarial scenario generation and modern learning paradigms.

Result: The paper describes ongoing work; the contribution is a problem formulation and a proposed solution architecture.

Conclusion: This work provides a scalable pathway for embedding safety into Physical AI systems operating in complex real-world environments through adversarial scenario generation.

Abstract: In this work, we propose an agentic gamification framework for hazard-informed learning of robot safety policies through synthetic scenarios. We model scenario generation as an adversarial game between two agents: a Red Team that explores the space of potential failures by constructing hazardous situations, and a Blue Team that incrementally refines safety policies to prevent them. This iterative process enables efficient discovery of high-risk edge cases that are unlikely to be captured through random simulation or manual enumeration. By combining classical risk modeling with adversarial scenario generation and modern learning paradigms, this work provides a scalable pathway for embedding safety into Physical AI systems operating in complex real-world environments. The paper describes ongoing work. The contribution is a problem formulation and a proposed solution architecture.

</details>


### [129] [Towards a Data Flywheel for Embodied Intelligence in Logistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05960)
*Anlan Yu,Zaishu Chen,Zhiqing Hong,Daqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a data-centric framework for industrial embodied intelligence using a logistics data flywheel, including World-Model-based data aggregation (WM-DAgger) for robust imitation learning and alignment of large-scale multimodal data for continual policy improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between laboratory demonstrations and industrial deployment of embodied intelligence by addressing scalability challenges in data collection, organization, and reuse within logistics scenarios.

Method: A data-centric framework with a logistics data flywheel that (1) converts daily operations into reusable data assets, (2) uses World Models (specifically WM-DAgger) to synthesize out-of-distribution recovery data for robust imitation learning, and (3) aligns large-scale in-the-wild multimodal data (human demonstrations, operational videos, robot logs) for policy learning and system feedback.

Result: Initial result: WM-DAgger, a World-Model-based data aggregation framework that synthesizes OOD recovery data, enabling robust imitation learning. Ongoing work explores alignment of multimodal data for continual system improvement.

Conclusion: The logistics data flywheel framework offers a scalable path for industrial embodied intelligence deployment by treating data as a reusable, continuously improving asset.

Abstract: Embodied intelligence is moving from laboratory demonstrations toward industrial deployment, with the logistics industry serving as a key application scenario. Learning-based policies offer a promising path beyond traditional perception-planning-control pipelines, but their scalability depends on how embodied data can be collected, organized, and reused. This research studies a data-centric framework for industrial embodied intelligence by constructing a logistics data flywheel. Our framework converts daily operations into reusable data assets, uses World Models to generate reliable supervision for long-tail parcel manipulation, and feeds deployment feedback back into policy improvement. As an initial result, \textit{WM-DAgger} introduces a World-Model-based data aggregation framework that synthesizes out-of-distribution recovery data for robust imitation learning. Building on this result, ongoing work explores how large-scale in-the-wild multimodal data, including labeled human demonstrations, unlabeled operational videos, and system-level robot logs, can be aligned for policy learning and transformed into feedback for continual system improvement.

</details>


### [130] [World-Language-Action Model for Unified World Modeling, Language Reasoning, and Action Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05979)
*Yi Yang,Zhihong Liu,Siqi Kou,Yiyang Chen,Yanzhe Hu,Jianbo Zhou,Boyuan Zhao,Zhijie Wei,Xiao Xia,Xueqi Li,Pengfei Liu,Zhijie Deng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WLA models are a new class of embodied foundation models that jointly predict textual subtasks, subgoal images, and robot actions by combining world modeling (from WAMs) and language reasoning (from VLAs). Using an autoregressive Transformer with a World Expert and Action Expert, WLA achieves state-of-the-art results on multi-task and long-horizon tasks (e.g., 92.94% success on RoboTwin2.0 Clean) with fast inference (40ms on RTX 5090).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied foundation models have separate strengths: world-action models (WAMs) learn from egocentric videos but lack language reasoning for long-horizon tasks, while vision-language-action (VLA) models excel at language reasoning but lack world modeling capabilities. WLA aims to unify these two paradigms to achieve both robust world understanding and complex task reasoning.

Method: WLA uses an autoregressive Transformer backbone (instead of bidirectional diffusion as in WAMs) to predict the next state, which includes semantic-level textual intentions and fine-grained physical dynamics. It employs a World Expert for world modeling supervision and an Action Expert for action generation. Meta-queries are used to make world prediction implicitly influence action generation, allowing world prediction to be disabled during inference or activated for test-time scaling.
